# Blank Inheritance: the twenty chapters (draft)

*The Casebook, version 9, 2026-10-01: Claude's draft, written at Ingram's request. Version 9 rewrites Episode One whole in the house style Ingram asked for (D056): most important thing first, concise and precise, written for the GM at the table. Part Zero is built from the new canon tree (`canon/`) and now holds every Episode One scene in full; Episode Two is a ghost (D056). Everything after Chapter 1 is still the version 8 outline, a flexible draft (D5). Tags: (Canon) is settled in a decision or a built scene; (Proposal) is Claude's, free to throw away; (GM-only) is secret; (Flag) is open. The published page carries the same text: https://claude.ai/artifact/SdqFGrbUWc6czj3UES8kb8. Edit this file and the page together, or the two will drift.*

**What's new in version 9:**

- **Episode One is rewritten whole, for you at the table** (D056). All eleven scenes after Scene 0 are rebuilt in the house style: the most important thing first, every field inside its budget, notes as labelled lines (Do, If…, Secret, Plant, Watch for). About 27,000 words of GM text became about 4,800, plus 1,700 of check results. Each scene is in Part Zero with its re-read, waiting for your read.
- **One story runs through the day.** Vetch's docket for the bubble in a spirit level (Kit Issue); Manako's plan to requisition the real vial (The Errand); the requisition filed at the hatch, quietly or loudly (The Tram Back); the storm drains as Vetch's payback (Canteen); his briefing at Dock C and the launch across the harbour (The Walk Down); and at the top of the drains, the requisition countersigned in green ink: "Which one of you is Rand?" (Flood Tunnel Clearance).
- **The founders' names come out in play** (D048): Shucks's badge at the medical, then a history lesson with a roll, with the names on the noticeboard if nobody sits it.
- **Every school lesson pays off the same night**: the workshop's +2 on the drain panels, natural history's centipedes already known, the range's +1 on a first shot, and no handlamp for whoever comes in last from local studies.
- **Episode One ends on your night's rests** (D050): each player picks their own on The City, from the ten rests and "somewhere new" (the Magic Show, as a blurred card).
- **Cut:** the Frame sighting in The Road In; the closing "sorted on purpose" beat; the Canteen's knowledge check and meet-someone roll; three scripted pauses for Manako. The Road In's seal of 22 September is reopened, because the scene was rewritten.
- **Yours to check:** the names I proposed (the Deputy Director, Seiichi Takamine; the history teacher, Ota); Dock C and the night lockup, the tea house and the instrument makers, all waiting for a place on the map; and the Paper Lantern, which needs one before it can be offered as a rest.
- **The Scene Runner shows notes as labelled lines**, with each label in its own column and secrets marked, so a note can be read at a glance.

Scenes rebuilt one at a time in the new canon tree (`canon/`), in play order. Each is shown in full, as the GM would run it, with the record of its re-read. A scene is approved when Ingram has read all of it and said so.

### Throughlines to carry into the rebuilds

- **The old town is on the waterfront** (D048). Four old scenes put it on a hill above the harbour road: The Errand (a
  street drops down to the harbour road below it), School: First Period (the hill is where the old town had to stop),
  Unidentified Contact (the contacts are on the harbour edge below it) and First Contact (the harbour road below it,
  with side streets climbing to it). Fix each one as it's rebuilt.
- **The founders' names come out in play, after Scene 0** (D048): a history lesson or another event, with a roll, and
  perhaps a second place that tells it from another angle. Intake Medicals comes first, and Shucks's badge (MORDANT)
  and his "Yes, that one" there were written for players who had just heard the names. Rebuild it with that in mind.
- **Why the Directorate came to Kurose** is the question Scene 0 leaves open. The answer, Rupiah asking the gods, is a
  drop for around Episode 3 or 4 (D048).
- **Cicada comes out piecemeal**, as the party looks into it (D048).

### Scene 0: From Up Here *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 2 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 2 · 2026-09-27 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's second read

#### Ingram's first read, and what each note changed

Her nineteen notes are in `canon/decisions/D048.md`, word for word, and the country's name is D049.

1. **Too much, too fast.** The four founders aren't named, and where the rift stopped isn't said. Rupiah asking the
   gods, and the gods' answer, are gone. In their place is a question: why the Directorate believes there will be a
   next time, and why it crossed the world to wait for it.
2. **Directions.** None left. No beat has one.
3. **The ledger.** Answered in D048 ("What Claude found"). Her First Impact dossier, as first filed, is now a source
   record (`canon/sources/S001.md`), and the rift is told with its detail: the violet light with a black heart, growth
   that sped up in fits and starts, the clerics, wizards and sorcerers it swallowed, the jagged road, the fighters from
   the island and then the region, and the ending nobody who was there could confirm.
4. **Style.** Fuller sentences throughout. Her prophecy wording is kept, word for word, for when it's revealed.
5. **Length.** From 578 words in three beats to 1,296 in seven, about nine minutes: the age, the rift, the Directorate,
   Kurose and its people, Frames, the city now, and the gate.
6. **The old town** is on the waterfront, where the village was.
7. **Her Frame line**, word for word.
8. **What a Frame is**: a walking machine shaped roughly like a person, taller than the warehouses, with a pilot inside
   working its arms and legs.
9. **The direction about the joke** is gone.
10. **"The gods said Kurose…"** went with the prophecy.
11. **Founding Day**, in her words, then the city now: electric light, radio, trams and factories; change so fast it
    made winners and losers; the wealth few can argue with; Minkai's wounded pride; groups who see the Directorate as a
    foreign thorn; unrest; organised crime.
12. **The generals and admirals** now complain in the newspapers about a foreign institution that keeps a private base
    in their harbour, answers to nobody in Minkai, and drills for a disaster that may never come.
13. **Cicada** is out. Unrest and the gangs are in.
14. **The base, the fence and the gate** are described in full: the island, the causeway, the fortress, the fence, the
    barrier, the booth and the guard with a ledger.
15. **The promise line** is gone.
16. **Trainees**: most people think of it as a faff and a bother, a duty that's done once it's done; some volunteer,
    some are encouraged, some are pushed. Then her approved last line.
17. **The direction about the next scene** is gone, and so is every other mention of another scene in the GM text.
18. **No rolls.** The Recall Knowledge answers and their challenge are deleted.
19. **No questions.** The six optional answer beats are gone.

#### What else the re-read changed

- **Minkai (Golarion's Japan)**, with the bracket where Minkai is first named (D049).
- **Everything the scene says is cited**: 61 facts told to the table (48 decided, 1 accepted, 12 Claude's as written
  here) and 11 behind it for the GM. `told` now says when the table hears a fact, apart from who knows it in the world
  (`known`), because Ingram's notes held back things everyone in Kurose knows.
- **Two facts from an earlier version were wrong and are fixed.** The party arriving disguised was stated as a fact;
  D009 makes that each player's call, so it's gone. The rift was "far to the west"; D024 says "the far side of the
  world", which the speech now says.
- **The test that pinned the four names in the speech** now pins the opposite: no founder, no Pharasma, no prophecy
  and no Cicada, and no rolls or questions anywhere in the scene.

#### For you, while reading

These are mine, not yet yours. Each is a `provisional` fact in `canon/world/`:
- **The age** (the first beat): magic still everyday, small and cheap; the great works built with engines; railways,
  steamships, wires carrying voices.
- **The island, the causeway and the gate**: man-made around a rock, a railway beside the causeway, a windowless grey
  fortress at the south end, hangars, workshops, offices and barracks, a fence all round, and the booth and barrier at
  the land end. Taken from the old map's entries, so it should match the map.
- **The first families and the newcomers**: the old families still in the old town; engineers, clerks, scholars and
  pilots, and the people who fed and housed them; newcomers' streets and temples; land sold too cheaply.
- **The Frames at work**: the Directorate builds, crews and runs them; one unloading a freighter, one failing to step
  over a tram.

#### The questions

- **R1, earns its place.** Each beat gives the table part of the world they are about to live in. The scene notes keep
  two things the GM uses: what the picture shows, and what is held back.
- **R2, worse without it.** Each beat carries something no other scene tells: the age, the rift, the question, the city,
  the Frames, the unrest, the gate.
- **R16, directions.** None.
- **R17, negatives.** None in the GM text.
- **R18, prepared for what can't happen.** Nothing: no answers, no questions, no rolls.
- **R3, clean.** No decision numbers, dates or other scenes in anything the GM reads. The notes name the picture's file,
  which the GM needs to show it.
- **R12, early reveals.** Every fact told passes the check. Read for the uncited: "four people were standing in front
  of it" and "the four founders" name nobody; "at least one of the founders might turn up" is Ingram's own line.
- **R19, answers or questions.** The biggest answer (why Kurose) is now the scene's question. The names come later with a
  roll. The rift's ending stays uncertain, as the dossier has it.
- **R20, telling them what it means.** Nothing does. "This year, it sent for you" states what happened.
- **R4, R5, R6, rolls.** None, on purpose: Scene 0 is the one scene with no rolls.
- **R7 to R10, people.** Nobody is in the scene.
- **R15, out loud.** Read through at speaking pace: about nine minutes.
- **R21, clipped lines.** Rewritten: "That coast. That long bay. That city." became one sentence; so did the base and the
  gate. The short sentences left ("Some are pushed.") each carry a whole thought.
- **R22, coined words.** Frame is explained where the players first hear it; so is glamour.
- **R23, length.** Twice the first version, as Ingram asked for this scene.
- **R11, retold.** The Frame and the crate of cabbage, and "This year, it sent for you."
- **R13, facts agree.** All cited; the two wrong facts are fixed.
- **R14, time.** Early morning, the camera's light ("in the early light" on the docks).
- **R24, places.** The old town is on the waterfront. The island, causeway, railway and gate follow the old map. Later
  scenes that put the old town on cliffs are listed in the plan, to fix when each is rebuilt.
- **R25, all her detail.** Rift: D024, D025 and S001 all used. City: D024, D025, D026, D044 and D048 all used. One
  detail left out on purpose: D024's list of what the public knows about Cicada, which D048 moves out of this scene.

#### Re-read against version 3 (2026-10-01, D056)

Four small fixes from the Runner review; nothing Ingram reads aloud changed.

- **R26, R28.** The goal was the longest GM text in the scene (73 words, a list of nine topics the beats already
  list); it is now one line. The summary shown in the goal box is one line too, and the first thing is shorter.
- **R27.** The "carries in" row said only that nothing carries in. It is gone.
- **R21.** "Four of them found the Directorate... Why, it has never said" read as "discovered" at a glance and ended
  on a clipped inversion. It now reads "Four of them founded the Directorate, which crossed the world to Kurose and
  never said why."
- GM text is 212 words for a nine-minute speech.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Everyone has heard what Kurose grew up knowing, from the edge of the sky down to the gate.

**How to play it.** One continuous speech over one picture, moving down from the edge of the sky to the gate. *Runs about 9 minutes.*

**Where.** Nowhere yet. High above the world, looking down.  
**First thing.** The picture: the curve of the world, a long bay and its city, a river through it, hills behind, early sun on the sea.  

**1. Start high: Golarion, and the age it's in.**

> Start high, high enough to see the world curve.
> 
> This is Golarion, and a great deal has changed here in the last two hundred years. Magic is still part of everyday life, small and cheap and everywhere, but the great works of this age are built with engines. Steam and oil came first, and then electricity. Railways run across whole continents now, steamships cross the oceans without waiting for the wind, and wires strung from pole to pole carry voices from one city to the next.

*Facts:* tells the table `era.two-hundred-years`, `era.engines`; GM only none.

**2. The rift at Kortos two hundred years ago, and the four people standing in front of it at the end.**

> Two hundred years ago, on the far side of the world, on the island of Kortos near the great city of Absalom, something opened in the air and would not close. The people who saw it described a violet light with a black heart.
> 
> At first it grew slowly. Then it grew faster, and the speed of its growing kept on climbing, in fits and starts that made it deadly to study. It swallowed the clerics, wizards and sorcerers who went to stop it. It carved a jagged road across the island, changing direction without warning, and the road it carved led towards Absalom.
> 
> Adventurers and magic-users came to fight it, first from all over the island and then from all over the region, and the finest from every neighbouring nation were among them. At the very end, four people were standing in front of it. It was forced back and crushed down into a small space, and then it was either destroyed or turned into something else. Nobody who was there has ever said for certain which.
> 
> Nobody agrees on what it was, either, or on why it happened. Every history of the world tells this story, and every one of them starts in the middle: first there is nothing, and then there is a catastrophe.

*Facts:* tells the table `rift.opened`, `rift.looked`, `rift.grew`, `rift.swallowed`, `rift.path`, `rift.converged`, `rift.four-at-the-end`, `rift.uncertain-ending`, `rift.nobody-knows-how`, `rift.histories-skip-the-start`; GM only `rift.cause`, `rift.entered-absalom`, `rift.how-it-was-closed`, `founders.names`.

**3. Four of them founded the Directorate, which crossed the world to Kurose and never said why.**

> About five years afterwards, four of the people who had stood in front of it founded the Directorate. They promised that if anything like it ever happened again, the world would be ready, and would answer it faster and more decisively than anyone had managed on Kortos.
> 
> Then, about a hundred and fifty years ago, the Directorate packed up everything it had, crossed the whole world, and settled in a tiny fishing village on the other side of it. Why it believes there will ever be a next time, and why it went so far to wait for one, it has never said.

*Facts:* tells the table `founders.four`, `founders.promise`, `directorate.founded-after`, `directorate.came-to-kurose`, `directorate.why-kurose-unexplained`; GM only `prophecy.asked`, `prophecy.answer`, `search-ship.hull`.

**4. Down to Kurose, in Minkai: a fishing village that became a great city, and the people it brought.**

> Come east, then, across the sea and over the curve of the world, and down through this cloud to the coast below: a long bay with a beach, and a city spread along it with a river running through the middle.
> 
> This is Kurose, in Minkai (Golarion's Japan). A hundred and fifty years ago it was a tiny fishing village with one road in. Then the Directorate arrived with a vast amount of money, and within a few generations the village had become a great city. The Directorate dredged a harbour deep enough for anything that floats, and everything you can see grew up around it: the factories and smokestacks along the shore, the tram lines following the river inland, and the streets climbing into the hills. The old town is still down on the waterfront, where the village used to be, and the families who were there first still live in it.
> 
> They are not the only ones any more. Hundreds of people came from all over Golarion to be part of the Directorate: engineers, clerks, scholars and pilots, and after them the people who fed them, housed them and sold them things. The newcomers built streets and temples of their own. Some of the families who were there first did very well out of all this. Some sold their land too cheaply and have never forgiven anybody. The two sides have still not settled their differences.

*Facts:* tells the table `kurose.name`, `kurose.minkai`, `kurose.far-east`, `kurose.fishing-village`, `kurose.grew-fast`, `kurose.shape`, `kurose.old-town`, `kurose.first-families-stayed`, `directorate.newcomers`, `directorate.newcomers-built`, `kurose.winners-and-losers`, `kurose.locals-and-newcomers`; GM only none.

**5. What a Frame is, and what anyone has ever seen one do.**

> Come lower, and you can see the Frames working on the docks.
> 
> A Frame is a walking machine, built roughly in the shape of a person and taller than the warehouses it works beside, with a pilot sitting inside it to work its arms and legs. The Directorate builds the Frames, crews them and keeps them running. They are patched and riveted and loud, and they are hard work to drive. In the early light, one of them is unloading a freighter, and another is trying to step over a tram and not quite managing it.
> 
> Every child in Kurose can tell you the Directorate was built to save the world, but the only thing anyone has ever seen a Frame do is lift girders at a building site or help lift a crate of cabbage. It's the oldest joke in the city, and people are still telling it.

*Facts:* tells the table `frames.what-a-frame-is`, `frames.look`, `frames.directorate-runs-them`, `frames.mechanical`, `frames.clumsy`, `frames.what-people-see`, `directorate.public-view`; GM only `frames.golarion-made`, `frames.gen-2`.

**6. Kurose now: fast change, Founding Day, Minkai's pride, unrest and the gangs, and glamour.**

> The city has changed faster than anyone could keep up with. Kurose has electric light in most of its streets, a radio in most of its kitchens, trams that run on time and factories that work through the night. It all came so quickly that it left people behind: for everyone who got rich, somebody else lost a trade, or a boat, or the street they grew up in.
> 
> On Founding Day there is a painting of the four founders in the shop windows of some of its supporters, and at least one of the founders might turn up to cut the ribbon at a major event. Not everyone trusts the Directorate, but few people can argue with the wealth and the technology it has brought to Kurose in so little time.
> 
> Minkai has its own army and its own navy, and it feels it can more than defend itself. Every year its generals and admirals complain in the newspapers that a foreign institution keeps a private base in their harbour, answers to nobody in Minkai, and drills for a disaster that may never come. Some people call the Directorate intruders, or even warmongers, and others call it their protector. The country's pride is wounded, and there are groups in Kurose who see the Directorate as a foreign thorn in their country's side and want it gone. There is plenty of unrest in the city, and organised crime is a serious problem: the gangs do very well in a city that grows this fast.
> 
> People in Kurose have also learned to be careful about how they look. Aliens are treated as outsiders here. Magic that makes someone look ordinary is cheap and everywhere, and you can buy glamoured clothes on the high street, so anyone who looks too unusual for the neighbours tends to wear a glamour in public. Some call that good manners. Some say it's a city asking people to hide. And some people, aliens especially, refuse to wear one at all, and put up with the stares.

*Facts:* tells the table `kurose.modern`, `kurose.everyday`, `kurose.left-behind`, `founders.painting`, `founders.ribbon`, `directorate.brought-wealth`, `minkai.own-forces`, `minkai.can-defend-itself`, `minkai.grumbles`, `directorate.answers-to-itself`, `directorate.warmongers`, `directorate.protectors`, `minkai.wounded-pride`, `kurose.isolationists`, `kurose.organised-crime`, `glamour.aliens-outsiders`, `glamour.cheap`, `glamour.debate`, `glamour.refusers`; GM only `founders.names`, `founders.alive`, `cicada.quietly`.

**7. Out to the Directorate's island and its gate. It sent for you.**

> Now look out into the harbour. At the end of a long causeway, with a railway line running beside it, is the Directorate's own island. It is man-made, built up around a rock, and at its southern end stands a flat grey fortress with no windows anywhere on it. Around the fortress are hangars, workshops, offices and barracks, and a high fence runs all the way around the island. At the land end of the causeway there is a gate: a red-and-white barrier across the road, a booth not much bigger than a telephone box, and a guard with a ledger.
> 
> Every year the Directorate sends for new trainees. Most people think of being a trainee as a faff and a bother: drills and forms and being shouted at, a duty you get through, and once it's done, it's done. Some people volunteer. Some are encouraged into it by a teacher or a parent. Some are pushed.
> 
> This year, it sent for you.

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.base`, `directorate.gate`, `directorate.sends-for-trainees`, `directorate.trainee-expectations`, `directorate.how-trainees-come`, `directorate.who-joins`; GM only none.

**Ends when.** The last line of the speech.
**Into the next scene.** "This year, it sent for you." The Road In.

### Scene 1: The Road In *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 3 · 2026-10-01 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the rebuild changed

Rebuilt whole in the house style (D056), from the old draft Ingram read in the Scene Runner.

- **GM text went from 2,915 words to 862**, check results included. Nothing she needs at the table was lost: the
  reasons went to the prep notes, the rules about the world to canon facts, and the history to this record.
- **The Frame sighting is cut.** From Up Here already shows the whole table Frames at work on the docks, patched,
  loud and hard to drive, so the beat had no job left.
- **The disguise line is corrected.** The old note said every character arrives looking terrestrial. D009 and D044
  make it the player's call, and plenty of aliens in Kurose refuse glamour, so the note now says that.
- **The violet mark is required.** It was an optional beat whose note said "guaranteed"; it is now a required beat
  with an "If no arrival fits" line.
- **The letter** is Ingram's approved handout (D038), unchanged except one line: it no longer calls Minkai a kingdom,
  since `minkai.government` is open. It now reads "owed to Minkai's own army or navy".
- **The letters arrive in the same week**, not weeks apart, so "Monday next" means the same Monday for all six.
- **The handout card** is one list of "if they ask about" lines, each detail with its answer, and the secret in the
  GM notes. The old card said the same things three times (reserve detail, GM notes, catchables).

#### The questions

**R1, R2. Does every part earn its place?** Yes. The letter is the first handout and plants the stamp; the three
questions give each player their first moment; the small problem is each player's first roll; the violet mark is the
episode's first plant. Cut: the Frame sighting (no job left), `firstThing` and `holdInReserve` (they repeated the
read-aloud and the handout).

**R16. Directions.** Two, both about pace: one question at a time, and hand the story back to the player. Both keep a
six-player montage short, which is the scene's main risk.

**R17. Negatives.** One: "Say nothing about them" for the letter's details, because pointing at a plant spends it.

**R18. Preparing for what the shape rules out.** No. There are no NPCs to answer questions.

**R3. How the scene was made.** Nothing in the GM text mentions decisions, dates, other scenes' insides or tools.
"Show the table" names the Runner's own button, which is what she presses.

**R12, R19, R20. Giving away too much.** The table learns the stamp exists, not why (`directorate.why-brought-forward`
is `told: later`). The marks are described, never explained. No line tells the players what anything means.

**R4, R5, R6. Rolls.** Every player rolls once (the small problem, any skill, DC 15). The mark is a free knowledge
check, DC 17. Critical failure on the small problem is funny, never harmful; failure gets them there late; critical
success gives the player something worth remembering. The mark's critical failure gives a confident wrong belief.

**R7 to R10. People.** No NPCs, on purpose: the players invent their own journeys.

**R15, R21. Read aloud.** Two short sentences, full and plain. The letter is Ingram's.

**R22. Coined words.** None new. "Frame" was explained in From Up Here.

**R23. Length.** 25 to 35 minutes, set by six players' turns. The GM text is short because the players do the talking.

**R26. Most important first.** Every beat line starts with what the GM does. Each note's first line is the one that
breaks the beat if missed (the plant, then the fallback, then the secret).

**R27. Said once.** "Nothing rides on these rolls" is said once, on the small problem; "keep them apart" once, in how
to play it and the ending's stop line; the letter's details once, on the handout card.

**R28. Budgets.** Every field is inside its budget. Longest beat line: 25 words. Longest note: 45 words. Whole scene:
639 words of GM text, plus 223 of check results.

**R11. What a player would retell.** Their own arrival, told in their own words.

**R13. Canon.** Cited: `directorate.sends-for-trainees`, `directorate.brought-forward-stamp` (D038),
`directorate.why-brought-forward` (D002, D011, D020, D038), `glamour.aliens-outsiders`, `glamour.refusers`,
`cicada.violet-marks` and `cicada.marks-meaning` (both provisional, from this scene).

**R14. Time.** Day 0, morning. "Monday next" and "by Monday" agree with Intake Medicals on Day 1.

**R24. Places.** Every arrival point is on the map (the departure's places); the scene ends converging on the
Candidate Gate.

**R25. Ingram's detail.** D038's letter is used in full: the stamp, the printed slip, the private copies, the
typewriter, Form 1-A.

#### For Ingram, while reading

- **The violet marks' meaning** is mine, provisional: Cicada's people mark walls where crowds wait, as places to show
  a crowd something one day.
- **`minkai.government`** is still open; the letter now avoids it.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player has said how their character looks and how we first see them, then rolled for and told one small problem on the way into Kurose.

**How to play it.** A montage, one player at a time. They tell it; you ask the questions and note the answers. **Nobody meets anybody yet.** *Runs about 25-35 minutes.*

**Where.** Six separate mornings, in six separate places, all in the same week.  
**First thing.** undefined  

**1. Read the letter aloud, then press Show the table. Each player gets a private copy with their own character's name typed in.**

> Dear Candidate,
> 
> Your application to Tokubetsu Taisaku Honbu has been reviewed and accepted. You are asked to report to the combined campus no later than Monday next, where you will be issued instructions, quartered, and enrolled.
> 
> Please bring nothing you cannot carry, and nothing you cannot leave.
> 
> We look forward to your service.

*GM:* Plant: The stamp, the typed name and the return slip are for whoever reads closely. Say nothing about them.
If asked what "nothing you cannot leave" means: Pack light. This is enlistment, and the Directorate provides what is needed.

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.sends-for-trainees`, `directorate.brought-forward-stamp`; GM only `directorate.why-brought-forward`.

**2. Ask each player in turn: How does your character look? How do we first see them? What small thing went wrong on the way in?**

*Direction:* One question at a time; a single line is a complete answer. Small means small: a missed tram, a torn strap, a dog. Nothing dangerous, and everyone arrives.

*GM:* If stuck: Ask: "Where are you, and what are you doing with your hands?"
If they want a journey: A motorbike that shouldn't still run, the early commuter train, the causeway on foot, a fishing boat.
If someone arrives undisguised: That is the player's call. People stare; plenty of aliens in Kurose refuse to wear a glamour.

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `glamour.aliens-outsiders`, `glamour.refusers`.

**3. Ask which skill they use to get past it. Post the check (any skill, DC 15), then they tell us how it goes.**

*Direction:* Take any skill they can argue for. Give them the result, then hand it back: "Tell us how it goes."

*GM:* If they would rather not roll: They just tell it. No result costs anything, and everyone reaches Kurose by Monday.

*Roll:* Get past a small problem: , DC 15.
- Critical success: It goes better than it had any right to. The player tells us how, and something about it is worth remembering.
- Success: It works. The player tells us how.
- Failure: It doesn't quite work. They reach the city anyway, a bit late or a bit worse for wear. The player tells us how.
- Critical failure: It goes wrong in a way that is funny, not harmful, and they still reach the city. The player tells us how.

**4. When an arrival ends where people stand and wait, describe a small deep-violet mark at head height on the wall. Then offer the check.**

*GM:* Plant: On a wall a waiting crowd faces, in every arrival it fits. The same violet, at the same height, every time.
If no arrival fits: Put it at the end of one random player's spotlight anyway.
Secret: The marks are Cicada's, laid on walls where crowds wait, as places to show a crowd something one day. Nothing this week explains them.

*Roll:* Recognise the mark: Occultism or Crafting or Society, DC 17. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: It isn't the first one they've seen: always this deep violet, always at head height, always where a crowd stops and waits. This one is fresh.
- Success: Now they think about it, it isn't the first one today. The same deep violet, at the same height.
- Failure: It looks like paint, and nothing more.
- Critical failure: They are sure it's a condemned-building notice, the kind the housing office stencils on empty lots. It isn't, and nobody corrects them.

*Facts:* tells the table `cicada.violet-marks`; GM only `cicada.marks-meaning`.

**Ends when.** All six arrivals are told. Stop before anyone meets.
**Into the next scene.** Skip to Monday morning, in the queue at the Candidate Gate. Intake Medicals, which opens in the queue at the Candidate Gate.

**Write down:**

- For each player: look, first shot, problem, skill, result, and one line of how it went.
- How often the violet mark came up, and who rolled well on it.

### Scene 2: Intake Medicals *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 3 · 2026-10-01 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the rebuild changed

Rebuilt whole in the house style (D056). GM text went from about 3,350 words to 680, plus 259 of check results.

- **It opens at the Candidate Gate**, where The Road In leaves them: the guard with the ledger takes their return
  slips. One sentence, so the gate From Up Here describes is a place they actually pass.
- **Section Nine is the six.** The old draft had "thirty-odd people" in the section and the six sorted on Monday,
  before the screen. Now the section is decided by the screen: the cards are handed out in the corridor afterwards,
  which is what the all-six moment turns on.
- **The founders' names (D048).** The old Shucks answered "that Shucks Mordant, the one from the speech", but Scene 0
  no longer names anyone. Now the table has his badge, a free Society roll to recognise the name, and his own "Shucks.
  Yes, that one." The other three names come out in the first school period. Shucks is shown to the table as "Doctor
  Mordant" until someone learns his first name; one press of the inline key tells them.
- **No local families.** Every character is a newcomer (D026, the induction), so Shucks no longer remembers their
  parents. He asks what they left behind, or about an old injury.
- **The charter moved to Assembly**, where the drill hall hangs it. It was in two places 1.3 km apart.
- **Checks cut:** reading Shucks (folded into asking him straight), making him like you (the private moment already
  does that), and palming the form (the clerk gives a spare to anyone who asks).
- **The location was a file that doesn't exist** (`intake-medicals`); it is now `intake-infirmary`, the one the scene
  set already uses.
- **Craig played Shucks.** A "Watch for" line on the doctor's beat says so, so his character's turn gets room.

#### The questions

**R1, R2.** Every beat is something the table does or hears: the queue (their first words to each other), the form
(the episode's title, twice), the medical (one kind conversation each), the flag, the cards. The three optional
checks each answer a likely question (the staff, the name, the hand, the screen).

**R16.** Two directions: give each player different questions, and say the flag the same way six times. Both change
how the scene lands.

**R17.** No negatives.

**R18.** No.

**R3.** Nothing about how the scene was made.

**R12, R19, R20.** The table learns: he is a founder (allowed here, `shucks.revealed-early`), they were all flagged
and put together, and the form asks twice. Held back: what the marker is, why the forms ask, Rand, Nozomi's reason.
Nozomi's name is a critical-success fragment only.

**R4 to R6.** Five checks, all optional, none costing anything. Each has a critical failure that gives a wrong but
harmless belief, and a critical success that gives a little more.

**R7, R8.** Shucks and the clerk each have what they'll say, what they won't (with the line they deflect with), and
their likely questions answered in their own voice ("His name", "Why the form asks twice").

**R9.** Shucks is a level-15 founder (D047), not built; the clerk is a proposed level-0 civilian. Nothing here is a
fight.

**R10.** Nobody here stops a candidate doing anything; the worst outcome of mischief is a welfare check-in.

**R15, R21.** The read-aloud is three droppable units, full sentences, led by smell.

**R22.** "Irregular" is the only new word, and it is the point that nobody explains it.

**R23.** 30 to 45 minutes: six private moments need the room.

**R26.** Every beat line leads with what happens. The "all six" beat's first note line is the plant: give the facts
and stop.

**R27.** "Nobody explains the screen" is said once (how to play it). The maiden-name secret is said once.

**R28.** Inside every budget. Longest beat line 25 words; longest note 62; GM text 675.

**R11.** The emergency question, and finding out it was all six of them.

**R13.** Cited: `directorate.form-12b` (D038), `directorate.missing-pilot-search` (D011),
`directorate.why-brought-forward`, `shucks.head-of-medical`, `shucks.revealed-early`, `shucks.rand` (D034, D046),
`directorate.the-marker` (D020, D021, D034), and three provisional facts from this scene:
`directorate.section-nine`, `directorate.carriers-streamed`, `shucks.maiden-names`.

**R14.** Day 1, morning: Monday, as the letter asked.

**R24.** The gate, the causeway and the island agree with From Up Here and the map.

**R25.** D011 (the overheard search), D020 (Shucks takes the blood himself; his surprise at six), D038 (Form 12-B
asks twice) and D034 (the hand) are all used.

#### For Ingram, while reading

- **Why the forms ask for mothers' maiden names** is mine, provisional: Shucks traces the marker through families.
- **Streaming carriers into one section** follows the induction design; six at once is Shucks's surprise (D020).

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player has had a private moment with Shucks and been told their screen came back irregular, and the six could find out it was all of them.

**How to play it.** A school medical crossed with a passport office: slow queue, then one kind conversation each, played in front of the whole table. **Nobody explains what the screen is for.** *Runs about 30-45 minutes.*

**Where.** The Candidate Gate, then the infirmary waiting room on the island. Monday, first thing.  
**First thing.** The queue, and how long it has not moved.  

**1. Go round once while the queue doesn't move: each player says or does one thing in character.**

*GM:* If stuck: Someone behind them asks which sign is right.

**2. The clerk hands out Form 12-B, each with the candidate's name already typed on it. Boxes 4 and 19 both ask for mother's maiden name.**

*GM:* Plant: The typed names have the same low, faint e as their letters. Say nothing about it.
If anyone asks for a spare: She hands one over. Grant the blank form.
Secret: Shucks uses mothers' maiden names to trace the marker through families. Nobody else remembers why the form asks.

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.form-12b`; GM only `shucks.maiden-names`.

**3. If anyone listens to the staff instead of the queue, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone listens to the staff.*

*GM:* Secret: The pilot expected to fly first, Nozomi, refused the draft after a magic show. It is why the intake came early.

*Roll:* Overhear the staff: Perception, DC 17. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: Two staff, low: "...the pilot, Nozomi, still hasn't turned up..." Then they see the candidates and stop.
- Success: "...still hasn't turned up." "...keep looking." Somebody is missing, and it matters.
- Failure: Just staff talking.
- Critical failure: They're sure it's about a missing delivery of forms, and they'll repeat it to anyone who asks.

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.missing-pilot-search`; GM only `directorate.why-brought-forward`.

**4. Shucks sees each of them alone: blood sample, then two or three questions about them. Play each in front of the table.**

*Direction:* Give each player different questions. Save this one for last: "If you come in here unconscious, what do I need to know that I wouldn't be able to see?"

*GM:* If they're disguised or alien: He already knows: "Yes, I know. Sit down." He asks how long they can hold it, and if it hurts.
If they're what they appear to be: Ask what they left behind, or about an old injury he can see.
Watch for: Craig played Shucks in the old campaign. Give his character's turn with the doctor room.

*Facts:* tells the table `shucks.head-of-medical`, `shucks.revealed-early`; GM only none.

**5. If anyone asks about the name on his badge, offer the check. If they ask him, he says <trig id="shucks-named"/> "Shucks. Yes, that one. Arm."** *(optional)*

*Someone asks about his badge.*

*Roll:* Recognise the name: Society, DC 15. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: Shucks Mordant: one of the four founders. Half the city is named after them, and this one is the real thing, taking blood.
- Success: Mordant: one of the four founders' names. Half the city is named after them.
- Failure: The name means nothing yet.
- Critical failure: They're sure it's a brand of disinfectant.

*Facts:* tells the table `shucks.revealed-early`; GM only none.

**6. If anyone watches the hand that holds the needle, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone watches his hands.*

*GM:* Secret: His real right hand is Rand, a familiar loose in the city. He doesn't know where it is today.

*Roll:* Watch the hand: Perception or Medicine, DC 17. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: A half-beat of lag in his right hand, as if something else is deciding. He notices them noticing, and flexes the fingers once.
- Success: His right hand isn't bone. The give at the knuckle is wrong: it's a prosthetic.
- Failure: Nothing they can put a finger on.
- Critical failure: They decide it's just what being a skeleton looks like.

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `shucks.rand`.

**7. Shucks tells each of them, alone: "Your screen came back irregular. It does that. Nothing to worry about."**

*Direction:* The same words, the same way, six times.

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `directorate.the-marker`.

**8. If anyone asks Shucks what irregular means, he deflects kindly. Offer the check to ask him straight.** *(optional)*

*Someone asks about the screen.*

*Roll:* Ask him straight: Diplomacy, DC 13.
- Critical success: "It's a screen. Screens flag things. You're fine." Then, quieter: "Ask me again in a year if it still bothers you."
- Success: "It's a screen. Screens flag things. You're fine." As far as he's allowed to say, it's true.
- Failure: He answers a different question, kindly.
- Critical failure: He decides they're a worrier and books them a welfare check-in nobody wants.

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `directorate.the-marker`.

**9. If anyone tries to work out what the screen tests for, offer the knowledge check.** *(optional)*

*Someone tries to work it out.*

*Roll:* Work out what the screen tests: Medicine or Nature, DC 17. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: No standard screen has a result called irregular. This is a custom test for something inherited, which is what a maiden-name box on a medical form is for.
- Success: It's a custom test, not a standard screen.
- Failure: A blood test is a blood test.
- Critical failure: They're certain it's an anaemia screen.

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `directorate.the-marker`, `shucks.maiden-names`.

**10. Out in the corridor, the clerk hands each of them a card: SECTION NINE. Let them compare.**

*GM:* Plant: Give the facts and stop: all six were told irregular, and all six cards say Section Nine.
If nobody compares: A candidate from another queue asks loudly whether anyone else got irregular.
Secret: Candidates with the marker are streamed together as routine. Six in one intake has never happened; Shucks is quietly astonished.

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.section-nine`; GM only `directorate.carriers-streamed`.

**Ends when.** All six hold a Section Nine card.
**Into the next scene.** A bell, and a staff officer marches the whole intake to the drill hall for Assembly. Assembly, in the drill hall.

### Scene 3: Assembly *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 3 · 2026-10-01 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the rebuild changed

Rebuilt whole in the house style (D056). GM text went from about 2,135 words to 296, plus 174 of check results; the
address is about 330 words read aloud.

- **The address carries D053.** In character, the Deputy Director says the Directorate is a school and a service at
  once; that afternoons are theirs to choose between the gymnasium, the range, the library or a lesson; and the two
  rules: curfew is a small thing, a refused order is the most serious thing they can do, and means explaining
  themselves to someone like him.
- **The dig is gone from the speech.** The old address said the city "was built on top of a dig" and it was "on the
  municipal seal". That contradicts canon: Kurose was a fishing village, the Directorate has never said why it came,
  and the digging is known to few (`directorate.why-kurose-unexplained`, `directorate.digs`).
- **No "your parents".** Every character is a newcomer, so the anthem is a song "everyone over thirty knows", not
  one their families sang.
- **Vetch collects Section Nine** at the end and names himself. The episode now meets him in person four times:
  here, Kit Issue, Canteen, and at the hatch.
- **The charter lives here only**, with its four signatures (D044) as a free check.
- **The Deputy Director says his name.** It was a placeholder; I've proposed Seiichi Takamine (see below).
- **Rumours moved to the Canteen**, the real crowd scene, where the Runner's rumour mill deals them in one press.

#### The questions

**R1, R2.** The anthem (the first time being new costs something in a room), the address (the episode's one
explanation), the platform (every adult they'll meet), the charter (a plant), and Vetch (their officer).

**R16.** No directions. The beat line says to read the address as a speech and which units may be dropped.

**R17.** No negatives.

**R18.** The address can't be interrupted; questions come after, and the note says he answers them.

**R3.** Nothing about how the scene was made.

**R12, R19, R20.** Told: what the Directorate says it is, the day's shape, the two rules, the charter's four
signatures. Held back: why Kurose (he refuses kindly), the Frames (not today), whose hands the signatures are.

**R4 to R6.** Three optional checks: the anthem (a newcomer getting through a song; the critical failure gets a note
made), the platform (who's who), the charter (the signatures). None costs anything mechanical.

**R7, R8.** The Deputy Director and Vetch each have what they'll say and won't, with their deflecting lines. The
walk-ons don't speak.

**R9.** Both proposed, non-combat.

**R10.** Nothing here can be fought or stolen; walking out is a curfew-sized thing.

**R15, R21.** The address reads aloud in five units of full sentences. The intro leads with smell.

**R22.** No new words.

**R23.** 20 to 25 minutes.

**R26.** Each beat line leads with what happens. The one bold rule in how to play it is the thing D053 needs.

**R27.** The two rules are said once, in the address; the Deputy Director's card points at them.

**R28.** Inside every budget.

**R11.** "That is a promise, and it is not a guarantee", or a character mangling the anthem.

**R13.** Cited: `founders.promise`, `directorate.school-and-service`, `directorate.free-time`,
`directorate.curfew-minor`, `directorate.orders-serious` (all D053), `founders.charter-signatures` and
`founders.charter-hands` (D044), `shucks.old-handwriting` (D034); provisional from this scene:
`directorate.alarm-never-real`, `section-officer.vetch`; and a proposal, `deputy-director.name`.

**R14.** Day 1, late morning, straight after the medical.

**R24.** The drill hall, which Ingram drew (req-1).

**R25.** D053's words on the speech are used in full.

#### For Ingram, while reading

- **The Deputy Director's name, Seiichi Takamine,** is a proposal. Change one word in the address if you'd rather
  something else.
- **"The alarm has never once been real"** is mine, kept from the old draft: provisional.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** They have heard the Deputy Director's address, seen the staff on the platform, and met their section officer, Vetch.

**How to play it.** Ceremonial and sincere: nobody in this room is lying. Read the address as a real speech. **Make sure the two rules land: curfew is small, a refused order is not.** *Runs about 20-25 minutes.*

**Where.** The drill hall, the only room on the island big enough for the whole intake.  
**First thing.** undefined  

**1. Everyone stands for the anthem. Read it, then wait to hear what each character does.**

> A piano starts, and four hundred people stand. The anthem is old and slow, and everyone in the room over thirty knows every word without looking.

*GM:* If a player says what their character does: Offer the check to get through a song they don't know.

*Roll:* Get through the anthem: Performance or Deception, DC 13.
- Critical success: They carry it off so well that an officer on the platform nods at them.
- Success: Nobody notices a thing.
- Failure: A neighbour notices they don't know the words and asks, kindly, where they're from.
- Critical failure: They're loudly wrong exactly where the piano stops. Somebody on the platform makes a note.

**2. The Deputy Director <trig id="deputy-director-steps-up"/> gives the address. Read it as a speech; drop any part but the last two.**

> My name is Seiichi Takamine. It will be on a great many documents you sign this year, so you may as well hear it said once, by me.
> 
> You have joined Tokubetsu Taisaku Honbu, the Special Countermeasures Headquarters, which this city calls the Directorate. We exist to be ready. We keep the sirens, the shelters and the drills, and on most days we keep the harbour, the drains and the heavy lifting this city can't do without. In a hundred and fifty years the alarm has never once been real. That is a promise, and it is not a guarantee.
> 
> You will find we are two things at once. We are a school, so you will sit lessons. We are a service, so you will drill, carry orders and stand watches. In the mornings you will be taught. In the afternoons, once your section officer releases you, the time is yours: the gymnasium, the range, the library, or a lesson you choose yourselves. Spend it well. Nobody here will spend it for you.
> 
> We have a great many rules, and you will break most of them by accident before the month is out. Two you should know today. If you are out after curfew, that is a small thing: it will be written down, and you will be told off. If you are given a direct order and you refuse it, that is not a small thing. It is the most serious thing you can do here, and you will explain yourself to someone like me.
> 
> That is all. Your section officers have your postings. Welcome to the Directorate. I am glad you are here.

*GM:* Do: Afterwards he takes questions, and answers plainly.

*Facts:* tells the table `deputy-director.name`, `founders.promise`, `directorate.school-and-service`, `directorate.free-time`, `directorate.curfew-minor`, `directorate.orders-serious`, `directorate.alarm-never-real`; GM only none.

**3. If anyone watches the platform instead of the speaker, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone watches the platform.*

*Roll:* Read the platform: Perception or Society, DC 15. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: The shape of the place: machines, radio, medical, stores. And one officer at the far end whom nobody on the platform has looked at once.
- Success: About a dozen departments: who runs the machines, the radio and medical. The doctor from this morning is up there.
- Failure: Too many uniforms to keep straight.
- Critical failure: They're sure the woman with horns runs the whole base.

**4. If anyone looks closely at the charter, offer the check to read its four signatures.** *(optional)*

*Someone looks at the charter.*

*GM:* Secret: The doctor's scrawl is Shucks's own hand from before his right hand came off. Rand still writes in it.

*Roll:* Read the signatures: Society or Perception, DC 15. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: As success, and they would know the doctor's scrawl again anywhere.
- Success: Four signatures: a flowing hand; a quick doctor's scrawl; HYUCK in capitals, pressed so hard the nib tore the paper; and small careful characters in a script nobody here reads.
- Failure: It's a charter. It says what charters say.
- Critical failure: A staffer tells them confidently that the original burned and this is a copy. It isn't.

*Facts:* tells the table `founders.charter-signatures`; GM only `founders.charter-hands`, `shucks.old-handwriting`.

**5. Vetch <trig id="vetch-collects"/> collects Section Nine at the door and gives them the day's timetable.**

> "Section Nine. With me. Yes, all of you." He doesn't wait to see if you follow. "Section Officer Vetch. First period, then the stores, then second period. It's on the sheet. Read the sheet."

*Facts:* tells the table `section-officer.vetch`; GM only none.

**Ends when.** Vetch has led Section Nine out of the hall.
**Into the next scene.** "First period. School block. Don't be late." School: First Period.

- Who caught the Deputy Director's name.
- Who read the charter, and who watched the platform.

### Scene 4: School: First Period *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 3 · 2026-10-01 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the rebuild changed

Rebuilt whole in the house style (D056). GM text went from about 2,240 words to 432, plus 248 of check results.

- **The founders' names come out here, with a roll (D048).** The teacher asks the room to name the four; a Society
  roll decides who looks clever, and on any result the table hears Hyuck, Rupiah, Anabala and Shucks Mordant. If
  nobody sits local studies, the names are on the corridor noticeboard. This is the second angle D048 asked for: the
  first was Shucks's own badge at the medical.
- **No prophecy.** The old teacher said the four "swore to be in Kurose when the next one came", which is the
  Episode 3 or 4 reveal of why the Directorate came here. Gone.
- **The old town is on the waterfront** (D048). The old Survival line put it below a hill; now the map is read by its
  water and its outfalls, which also sets up tonight.
- **The rooms' costs are wired.** A failure in local studies makes them last into the stores, where the handlamps have
  run out (a real cost tonight, in a dark drain). A critical failure in either room sends them into tonight's job
  Fatigued. Passing the induction gives +2 on the drain chamber's panels. The old draft's "lose the afternoon's
  slack" no longer had anything to lose.
- **The tram map is an artefact**, so a critical success leaves the player holding something.
- **Two notes for the table's history:** four of the players played the four founders, and Steve played Hyuck, so the
  mascot joke is his.

#### The questions

**R1, R2.** The room choice (each player's first free decision), the founders (a D048 drop), each room's one roll,
and the corridor (thirty seconds to compare).

**R16.** No directions.

**R17.** None, beyond the forearm she won't let them touch, which is her line, not a GM instruction.

**R18.** No.

**R3.** Nothing about how the scene was made.

**R12, R19, R20.** Told: the founders' names and the approved pictures (both now `told` here), the statue and its
plaque. Held back: what the four really were, that the statue is Hyuck, that he's alive and testing Frames.

**R4 to R6.** Each room has one roll, failure always costs something visible, a critical failure costs a little more
(Fatigued tonight), and a critical success gives something extra (the tram map; Brack saying their name).

**R7, R8.** The teacher and Brack each have a want, an opener, what they'll and won't discuss, and deflecting lines.

**R10.** Skipping both rooms costs only the lesson; nothing here is a fight.

**R15, R21.** A short read-aloud, led by the bell.

**R22.** None new.

**R23.** 15 to 20 minutes.

**R27.** The names are in one place (the founders' beat); the costs are in the check results and the prep notes.

**R28.** Inside every budget.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player has sat a lesson of their choice, and the table has heard the four founders' names.

**How to play it.** Short and light. Each player picks a room; cut between the two. Not rolling is always fine. **The founders' names come out here, however the rolls go.** *Runs about 15-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The school block, first floor: two rooms at opposite ends of one corridor.  
**First thing.** undefined  

**1. Each player picks a room: local studies or the workshop. Splitting up is fine.**

**2. In local studies, the teacher asks the room to name the four founders. Offer the check; on any result the table hears them.**

*GM:* Do: The four are Hyuck, Rupiah, Anabala and Shucks Mordant: "who you'll have met this morning. Don't stare, he hates it."
If nobody chose local studies: The four names are on the corridor noticeboard: "Learn these for Friday."
Watch for: Four of your players played these four. Let them enjoy hearing the names.

*Roll:* Name the four founders: Society or Academia Lore, DC 15. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: They name all four, and add that Rupiah still cuts the ribbon on Founding Day. The teacher is delighted.
- Success: They name all four: Hyuck, Rupiah, Anabala, Shucks Mordant. The teacher writes them on the board.
- Failure: They get two. The teacher fills in the rest, kindly.
- Critical failure: They name a famous general instead. The teacher corrects them, and the room laughs, not unkindly.

*Facts:* tells the table `school-history-teacher.name`, `founders.names`, `founders.pictures`, `rupiah.cuts-the-ribbon`, `shucks.revealed-early`; GM only `founders.what-they-were`.

**3. The teacher takes them round the city on the wall map. If anyone works at it, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone chose local studies.*

*GM:* Plant: The harbour statue: a weathered gnoll with HYUCK on the plaque. He's sure it's a mascot.
Secret: The statue is Hyuck as he was. He's alive, and test-pilots the Directorate's worst Frames.
Watch for: Steve played Hyuck. Let the mascot joke land on him.

*Roll:* Learn the city: Society or Survival or Perception, DC 13. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: The teacher hands over his own tram map, twenty years of pencil notes on which services actually run. Grant it.
- Success: They can find their way across the city this afternoon, and they know which tram to take.
- Failure: Kept back to copy the map: they're last into the stores, and the handlamps have run out.
- Critical failure: Kept back through the break and late for everything after: Fatigued tonight.

*Facts:* tells the table `hyuck.statue`; GM only `hyuck.statue-truth`, `hyuck.test-pilot`.

**4. Hulda Brack runs the safety induction. If anyone joins in, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone chose the workshop.*

*GM:* Plant: Four metres of Frame forearm on trestles at the back, opened along one side, fingers curled. She won't let them near it.

*Roll:* Pass the safety induction: Crafting or Athletics or Medicine or Intimidation, DC 15.
- Critical success: As success, and she says their name out loud in front of everyone, and means it well.
- Success: They can work safely on her floor, and she shows them how a control panel thinks: +2 circumstance on panel work tonight.
- Failure: She stops them with one hand and a word. They sweep while the others work.
- Critical failure: Something that would have cost a finger. She stops the floor and keeps them past the bell: Fatigued tonight.

**5. The bell. Give them about thirty seconds in the corridor to compare notes, then send them to the stores.**

**Ends when.** The bell, and the corridor empties toward the stores.
**Into the next scene.** The timetable says the stores next, and the queue starts before the bell. Kit Issue, at the quartermaster's stores.

- Who sat which room, and what they rolled.

  kit" of the afternoon is real.

**R22.** None new.

### Scene 7: The Errand *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 3 · 2026-10-01 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the rebuild changed

Rebuilt whole in the house style (D056). GM text went from about 6,050 words to 617, plus 306 of check results.

- **Manako owns the tea house** (D034: "the kindly old man who works in the tea shop... running a successful small
  business"). The old draft made him a poor regular at a corner table. His district defers to him: the regulars nod,
  his tea comes unasked (D046).
- **One route to him.** The instrument maker laughs and, on any result, sends them on. The old draft had three
  separate routes (the maker, an ask-around search, a critical success).
- **The docket's joke is the maker's.** "Love, we make the vials. There's nothing in the bubble." The old "obsolete
  catalogue name" device, which needed Manako to decode it, is gone; his job is the plan.
- **His key line opens the plan (D045):** he came back empty-handed, they laughed, he's had a long time to think.
  Then the vial, as D018 has it. He writes the part number on the back of a bill, and they keep it.
- **His line about the Directorate is now a required beat**, as the episode always meant. It is the vault line Ingram
  accepted in D044; `directorate.digs` is therefore told here now (D056 records it).
- **His number goes to one player, at the GM's call, no roll (D045).** The Scene Runner button for it isn't built,
  so the line says to hand the player a note.
- **What he won't say has a real deflection** in his own voice. The old line ("nothing has happened in this city yet,
  he is not acting") contradicted the stencils and the police clash, and was the conspicuous denial D053 warns about.
- **No pauses for effect.** The old read-alouds had three scripted pauses; Ingram's D045 said mystery makes players
  more alert, so he is plainly kind and plainly bitter.
- **Geography (D048):** no "street below the old town". The dogs' street is just a side street.
- **The reagent-stock plant is cut.** It had no payoff anywhere.

#### Manako: the leak check (D044's standing rule)

Every line he says, asked "what could a sharp player deduce, and is that earlier than intended?"

- "Manako. I was a chemist... For them, as it happens." Allowed (D045). Deduction: he worked for the Directorate.
  Fine, and true.
- "They did this to me, you know... I came back with nothing, and they laughed." Allowed (D045). Deduction: the
  hazing is old. Fine.
- The plan. Pure procedure.
- "They're not wicked. They're just very good at not talking." and the vault line. Allowed (D016, D044). Deduction:
  the Directorate keeps things locked away. That is the half-right conclusion Ingram wants available.
- "Somebody's sign for something. The city's full of them." His only deflection, about the violet marks. It is
  ordinary, said with a shrug, and points at nothing. A player who later learns the truth can read it as a lie,
  which is fair.
- "They decided I'd made a fool of myself. Maybe I had." About leaving the Directorate. Matches the very hard
  knowledge check (a breakdown, an expulsion) without saying why.
- Never mentioned: the Watcher, the sky, his wife, Cicada, the conference's cause, the revenge list.

#### The questions

**R1, R2.** The city (place and plants), the instrument maker (the joke lands), the second mark (the repetition is
the clue), Manako (the scene's heart), the plan (the win), the filing choice (theirs), the Directorate line, and the
number (a channel to the antagonist).

**R16.** One direction, on Manako: kind, useful, openly bitter, at an even pace, name given first. It is the one
delivery note that keeps him from leaking.

**R17.** "Say nothing about it" on two plants; pointing at a plant spends it.

**R18.** No.

**R3.** Nothing about how the scene was made. "Hand that player a note" replaces "the button isn't built yet".

**R12, R19, R20.** See the leak check. The table learns his name, his trade, the hazing, the plan and the vault
line; not who he is.

**R4 to R6.** Four checks, none gating anything: the maker (how warmly she sends them on), the mark, the very hard
name check (D045's exact two tiers), and reading him. Critical failures give harmless wrong beliefs.

**R7, R8.** The maker and Manako each have what they'll say and won't, with deflecting lines.

**R9.** The maker is proposed; Manako is deliberately not built (he can't be fought or caught here, D016).

**R10.** Attacking or robbing a kind old man in his own tea house is not a branch this scene supports; his regulars
are there. A table that tries it gets a closed door and a cold district, and he survives.

**R15, R21.** Read-alouds in full sentences; his speech reads like an old man talking, not like prose.

**R22.** None new.

**R23.** 30 to 40 minutes.

**R26.** Every beat line leads with what happens; the plan's beat is the key line, then the plan.

**R27.** Manako's background is said once, on his card. The plan is said once, in his read-aloud.

**R28.** Inside every budget.

**R11.** "Love, we make the vials." And the vial.

**R13.** Cited: `hyuck.statue`, `watcher.animals` (D043), `section-officer.hazing` (D018), `cicada.violet-marks`,
`cicada.marks-meaning`, `manako.name`, `manako.chemist`, `manako.came-back-empty-handed`, `manako.the-vial-plan`,
`manako.disgraced`, `manako.cicada`, `manako.messages-read` (D016, D017, D018, D034, D045), `directorate.digs`
(told here now, D056).

**R14.** Day 1, afternoon, back by nineteen hundred.

**R24.** Unsited: the workshop and the tea house wait for Ingram's siting (D046).

**R25.** D018, D043, D045 and D046 are all used.

#### For Ingram, while reading

- **Siting:** the tea house's district and the workshop a few streets from it are still yours to place.
- **The part number on a bill** is an artefact the players keep: Manako's handwriting.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** They have been laughed at, met Manako, heard his plan for the vial, and chosen how to file it.

**How to play it.** Warm, funny and safe: a humiliation that turns into a win. Manako is kind and useful in every line. **Nothing here hints at who he really is.** *Runs about 30-40 minutes.*

**Where.** The city: an instrument makers' workshop, then a tea house a few streets on.  
**First thing.** undefined  

**1. Read the city as they come off the train, then let them find the instrument makers.**

*GM:* Plant: A side street the dogs won't walk down, and a roof with no pigeons on it. Say nothing about it.
If they pass the harbour square: The gnoll statue, with HYUCK on the plaque.
Secret: Animals hear the Watcher's swarm as a high buzzing, and it's thick on that street.

*Facts:* tells the table `hyuck.statue`; GM only `watcher.animals`.

**2. The instrument maker reads the docket twice and laughs. On any result, she sends them on to Manako.**

> "Love, we make the vials. There's nothing in the bubble. That's the whole point of the bubble. It's the bit that isn't there."

*GM:* If anyone tries to talk her round: Offer the check: it decides how warmly she sends them on.

*Roll:* Talk the instrument maker round: Diplomacy or Deception or Intimidation, DC 13.
- Critical success: She walks them to the door: "Manako. Tea house, few streets on. Tell him I sent you." They arrive half-trusted.
- Success: Cheerful, no help, and the name: "Ask Manako."
- Failure: The docket is somebody else's problem. A voice from the back says, "Ask Manako."
- Critical failure: She calls them "the Directorate" for the rest of the visit, the way you'd say "the electric company". Someone still says, "Ask Manako."

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `section-officer.hazing`.

**3. On the walk to the tea house: a small deep-violet mark at head height, on a tram-stop wall. Offer the check.**

*GM:* Plant: The same violet, at the same height, as on the road in. Say nothing about it.
Secret: Cicada's, like the others.

*Roll:* Recognise the mark: Occultism or Crafting or Society, DC 17. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: Like every one they've seen, it's where a crowd stands and waits. Somebody is choosing these walls.
- Success: Somebody has been doing this for a while.
- Failure: It looks like paint, nothing more.
- Critical failure: They decide it's a tram company mark, and say so with confidence.

*Facts:* tells the table `cicada.violet-marks`; GM only `cicada.marks-meaning`.

**4. Manako <trig id="manako-at-his-table"/> owns the tea house. He reads the docket before they finish explaining: "Ah. They've sent you for the bubble."**

> The tea house is small, warm and full: steam, low voices, a counter polished by a century of elbows. The regulars nod to the old man at the corner table as they pass, and his tea arrives without his asking.

*Direction:* Kind, useful and openly bitter about the Directorate, at an even pace. He gives his name before he's asked.

*GM:* Watch for: Every line of his is a leak risk. If unsure, have him talk chemistry.
Secret: He founded Cicada. Nothing shows it here, because here he is only kind.

*Facts:* tells the table `manako.name`, `manako.chemist`; GM only `manako.cicada`.

**5. If anyone tries to place his name, offer the very hard knowledge check.** *(optional)*

*Someone tries to place the name.*

*Roll:* Place the name: Society or Academia Lore, DC 20. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: The Directorate scientist who had a breakdown at a science conference, caused a huge fuss, and was expelled. Nothing about why.
- Success: A chemist, disgraced, a long time ago.
- Failure: Nothing comes.
- Critical failure: They're sure they've heard it, and can't place it.

*Facts:* tells the table `manako.disgraced`; GM only none.

**6. If anyone watches Manako rather than the docket, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone watches him.*

*Roll:* Read Manako: Perception or Medicine, DC 17.
- Critical success: As success, and he notices being looked at, and isn't used to it. He has decided he likes this one.
- Success: A man who has been very good at something and stopped, and minds.
- Failure: A kind old man with a good coat.
- Critical failure: A faint conviction that he is nobody much. Let it stand.

**7. Manako tells them how he came back empty-handed once, then gives them his plan.**

> "They did this to me, you know. Forty-odd years ago. I came back with nothing, and they laughed, and I've had a long time to think about what I should have done."
> 
> "So. He's written BUBBLE, ONE. Don't argue with him. Take him at his word. The bubble lives in a sealed vial, and the vial is a real part, with a real number, and it isn't cheap. Requisition a new vial against this docket, exactly as he wrote it. It needs a signature from a desk above his, and it makes a form that says who asked for it, and why."
> 
> "You'll have done exactly what you were told. Somebody upstairs will spend four minutes of their afternoon working out why."

*GM:* Do: He writes the part number on the back of a bill for them, in a careful, old-fashioned hand. Grant it.

*Facts:* tells the table `manako.came-back-empty-handed`, `manako.the-vial-plan`; GM only none.

**8. Ask the table how they'll file the requisition. Let them settle it between themselves.**

**9. Before they go, if it hasn't come up, he asks who they work for, and says one true thing about them.**

> "They dig things up, you know, the people you work for. Whatever they find goes in a vault, and nobody talks about it again. That's not wicked, mind. Some things ought to be locked away. But keeping quiet gets to be a habit."

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.digs`; GM only none.

**10. He asks them to come back and say how it went. If one was especially kind, he writes them his number on a napkin.**

*GM:* Do: Your call who, if anyone. No roll. Hand that player a note with the number.
Secret: Trainee messages are read: by the Directorate, as routine, and by the Watcher.

*Facts:* tells the table none; GM only `manako.messages-read`.

**Ends when.** They leave the tea house with a plan, a part number and the time.
**Into the next scene.** The train back to the island, and the stores' requisitions hatch before nineteen hundred. The Tram Back, to the island.

**Write down:**

- Who holds Manako's number, if anyone.
- How they're filing the requisition.
- Who heard what he said about the Directorate.

> On the radio, a father is trying hard not to shout. His daughter is seventeen, she's being held without charge, and nobody will tell him where.

*GM:* Plant: Nobody in the hall reacts to the radio. Say nothing more about it.

**2. Taka <trig id="taka-arrives"/> arrives with two chairs, claims the six as his table, and asks each of them where they're properly from.**

- Failure: It sounds like a duty list.
- Critical failure: They decide it's the easiest job on the list, he read it so evenly.

**5. The hall goes back to its supper. The next table gloats about harbour patrol, and Taka gets the last word.**

> "Storm drains." Taka is quiet for a moment, which is new. "Wear the waders. Trust me."

*Someone asks Taka about the drains.*

*Roll:* Ask Taka what's down there: Diplomacy or Perception, DC 15. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: "I did the drains my first month. Something nests down there every spring. Get up on the walkways and stay out of the water."
- Success: "Something lives down there. Keep out of the water if you can." He doesn't want to be asked again.
- Failure: He makes a joke of it and asks about the pickles.
- Critical failure: He laughs it off so well that they decide there's nothing down there.

**Ends when.** Taka has had the last word.
**Into the next scene.** Dock C at twenty-one hundred, with waders from the night lockup. The Walk Down, to the night lockup and Dock C.
