### Scene 11: Flood Tunnel Clearance *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 3 · 2026-10-01 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the rebuild changed

Rebuilt whole in the house style (D056). GM text went from about 3,390 words to 590, plus 132 of check results.

- **It ends on the night's rests (D050).** Each player picks their own on The City: the ten approved rests (D051)
  and "somewhere new" (D052), which on the first night is the Magic Show, shown as a blurred mystery card. The old
  ending, a closing beat about being sorted together on purpose, was an unratified proposal and is cut; the six
  already know they're all irregular and all Section Nine.
- **Rand pays off tonight.** Vetch is waiting at the top with their requisition, countersigned in green ink: Rand,
  Medical. "Which one of you is Rand? Nobody called Rand works in Medical. I've asked." That is the episode's last
  laugh and its open question, in place of Episode Two's reveal (Episode Two is a ghost, D056).
- **Every earlier choice pays off here, in six carry-in lines:** natural history (the centipedes known), its
  critical success (a show of force works), the workshop induction (+2 on the panels), the range (+1 on a first
  ranged Strike), anyone who missed out on a handlamp at the stores, and anyone Fatigued. Lamps are personal kit
  from the stores; the night lockup issues only waders, so local studies' cost is real down here.
- **The three endings are one choice** (kill, drive off, use the room), each worth full XP, in place of three beats
  and three notes.
- **Reading the water is a real check** with written results, in place of an unwritten GM call.
- **The power check's failure has a real cost:** the nest wakes before anyone is in position (and a critical failure
  leaves the worker off-guard).

#### The questions

**R1, R2.** The room (place), reading the water (optional, and it shapes the fight), the two jobs (the actual work),
the fight (the climax), the panel (real work for a player who won't fight), and the top (the payoff and the hook).

**R16.** One direction, on the fight: fast, and where the high ground is.

**R17.** None.

**R18.** No.

**R3.** Nothing about how the scene was made.

**R12, R19, R20.** Told: the signature, and that nobody called Rand works in Medical. Held back: what Rand is.

**R4 to R6.** Three checks and a fight. Failures cost position or surprise, never the job; critical failures add a
small, real cost (off-guard) or a harmless one.

**R7, R8.** Vetch has what he'll say and won't. The centipedes are animals.

**R9.** Vetch proposed, non-combat. The centipedes are in the encounter file.

**R10.** A player who won't go down waits at the ladder and can come down any time. A table that runs for the
ladder can; the job is unfinished, and Vetch is at the top.

**R15, R21.** The read-aloud is led by smell and is full sentences.

**R22.** None new.

**R23.** 45 to 60 minutes.

**R26.** The fight beat leads with the nest waking; its first note line is "let them choose how it ends".

**R27.** The endings are said once, in the choice. The panel bonus is said once, in the carry-ins.

**R28.** Inside every budget.

**R11.** "Which one of you is Rand?"

**R13.** Cited: `shucks.rand-signature` (provisional, told here), `shucks.rand` and `shucks.rand-signs` (D034,
D043, D046) as secrets.

**R14.** Day 1, night.

**R24.** Under the old landing, on the mainland waterfront.

**R25.** D050's ending (each player their own rest, on The City) and D052's "somewhere new" are both used.

#### For Ingram, while reading

- **The Paper Lantern isn't offered as a rest** because it isn't on the map yet; once it's sited, it's one more
  card.
- **"Somewhere new" stands at the covered market** on The City, blurred, because a marker needs a place. Players see
  where it is, not what.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** The line runs, the nest is dealt with their way, Vetch has asked about Rand, and each player has picked a rest.

**How to play it.** Physical first: the smell, the dark, the job. When the nest wakes, keep it fast. **Let them choose how the fight ends.** *Runs about 45-60 minutes.*

**Where.** A disused drainage chamber under the old town, down a ladder from the quay.  
**First thing.** undefined  

**1. Let them look round before anyone touches the panels.**

*GM:* If someone won't go down: They wait at the ladder and can come down any time. The job doesn't wait for them.

**2. If anyone studies the water before touching anything, offer the check.** *(optional)*

*Someone studies the water first.*

*Roll:* Read the water before touching anything: Nature or Survival or Perception, DC 15. Costs nothing on any result.
- Critical success: A nest under the grate, several of them, still asleep. Starting the machinery will wake them, so they can get into position first.
- Success: Something large lives under that water, more than one of it, and it's asleep.
- Failure: Black water. Nothing they can read.
- Critical failure: They're sure the water's empty.

**3. The job, part one: get power back to the drainage line from the panels. Runs alongside clearing the jam.**

*Roll:* Get power back to the drainage line: Crafting or Computers, DC 15.
- Critical success: Clean, first try. The others notice.
- Success: The line comes up.
- Failure: It comes up with a bang that carries through the drains. The nest wakes now, before anyone's in position.
- Critical failure: As failure, with a shower of sparks: whoever was working it is off-guard in the first round.

**4. The job, part two: clear the jam in the compactor feed, down on the floor. Runs alongside powering the line.**

> The feed is packed solid with whatever the old town has been putting down its drains, pressed into something with a grain to it. It comes apart if you work it in the right place.

*Roll:* Clear the jam in the compactor feed: Athletics or Crafting, DC 15.
- Critical success: Clean and fast. The machinery starts smoothly.
- Success: It clears.
- Failure: It clears with a shove, and the compactor lurches into motion: if it's running, start it at 1.
- Critical failure: As failure, and a face full of standing water. Nobody's hurt.

**5. The machinery wakes the nest <trig id="nest-wakes"/>. Six giant centipedes come up out of the water around the grate.**

*Direction:* Fast and physical. The flooded floor is difficult terrain in dim light; the walkways are dry high ground.

*GM:* Do: Ask how they want it to end, and back their answer. Each ending earns full XP.
If someone won't fight: Holding a walkway or working the panels is real work.
If it goes badly: Once half are down, the rest break for the grate.

**6. If the compactor is closing in, anyone at the north panels can spend their turn overriding it.** *(optional)*

*The compactor is running.*

*GM:* Do: Say plainly that it's as useful as an attack: it buys everyone on the floor their ground back.

**7. They climb out. Vetch <trig id="vetch-at-the-top"/> is waiting with their requisition, countersigned. Read it, then let him ask.**

> Vetch is waiting at the top of the ladder with a lamp and a sheet of paper. It's your requisition for the vial, countersigned in green ink over Medical's stamp: Rand, Medical.
> 
> "Which one of you is Rand?"

*GM:* If they don't know: "Nobody called Rand works in Medical. I've asked." He folds it away and dismisses them.
Secret: Rand is Shucks's own right hand, loose in the city. It signed tonight, from Shucks's desk. Nobody knows, Shucks included.

*Facts:* tells the table `shucks.rand-signature`; GM only `shucks.rand`, `shucks.rand-signs`.

**Ends when.** Vetch has dismissed them, and each player has picked a rest.
**Into the next scene.** Dismissed. Each of them picks where to spend the night. The night's rests, each player their own. Episode One ends there.

**Write down:**

- How they ended the fight, and who did what.
- Injuries carried into the night.
- Who has a theory about Rand.

### Scene 1: The General Hospital *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### Does every part earn its place?

**R1** Each part gives the GM something to run and a player something to do. The ward read-aloud sets the room; the
admission card gives every player a turn to say what happened to them; the deck gives rolls (Society, Medicine,
Diplomacy, Perception) and in-character prompts; the three regulars carry attitudes the next visit reads from. The
postcard drawer (hold in reserve) is a reward for kindness, not a plot hook. Changed: nothing cut.

**R2** Each part would be missed. Without the admission card, the one full-rest haunt would have no guaranteed turn for
each player; without the regulars, a return visit would read the same. Changed: nothing.

**R16** Directions kept only where critical: the rest field says plainly that nothing on the ward adds to or takes
from the full recovery, because D050 makes the hospital the sober choice with no buff, and a GM improvising a critical
success would otherwise be tempted to hand one out. Changed: no delivery directions in the beats beyond "give them a
moment", which is Ingram's own ask (D051).

**R17** No negative directions to the GM. The one "never" in the deck is the cleaner's firm's rule and the soldier's
note says what is held back, which is critical for a plot event. Changed: nothing.

**R18** The scene's shape is a quiet ward with interruptible conversations; nothing is prepared that the shape rules
out. Changed: nothing.

#### Is it clean?

**R3** Nothing the GM reads mentions decisions, dates, tools, other scenes or files. The statblock lines ("Made with
Ingram once the NPC generator exists") follow the finished Crew Mess and are the one place a tool is named, because
the stat blocks genuinely wait on it. No path outside `canon/` appears. Changed: nothing.

#### Does it give away too much?

**R12** Nothing forbidden is said or hinted: no founders, no Watcher, no screening, no blood, nothing about the
characters' nature. The facts cited are all `told: start` (`directorate.base`, `directorate.gate`,
`minkai.own-forces`). The spring drill Gennai mentions rests on what the table already hears in the opening: the
Directorate drills for a disaster. The twenty extra beds point at "something big on the island this week" and say
nothing about what; Ingram may want to check that against Episode Two's exercise before it is run. Changed: Gennai
knows how many beds and when, never why.

**R19** The soldier's event gives no answers, only observations a skill earns: the knuckles (Medicine), the lie
(Sense Motive), and on a critical success "Everyone's agreed I fell", which raises the hush-up as a question.
The nurse's line "I didn't write his" is a second, independent route behind a Diplomacy check at her Will DC, so the
question can be found from two angles. Who he hit, and why, stays held. Changed: the gate is named only as where he
was brought from.

**R20** No line says what anything means. The cleaner's dilemma shows the new city firm's rule and lets the choice
speak; the soldier's "You're theirs, then" shows the friction without explaining it. Changed: nothing.

#### Do the players roll?

**R4** Wherever a character acts, there is a check: helping on rounds (Medicine), the crossword (Society), the new
father (Diplomacy, or Stealth/Deception), finding the way back (Perception or Survival), the cut hand (Medicine plus
Stealth/Deception), the soldier (Sense Motive, Medicine or Diplomacy). The three role-play prompts and the tea
trolley are talk, with nothing to roll by design. The rest itself has no roll, as the approved idea promises.

**R5** The dilemma resolves through rolls if they choose the quiet way (Medicine, then Stealth or Deception against
the nurse's Perception DC); telling the nurse is a choice with a cost, and needs none.

**R6** Every roll pays on all four degrees. Critical failures escalate socially (the ward woken, the nurse down a
step, the soldier shouting) and never touch the full recovery. Critical successes give something extra that is not a
mechanical benefit: a finished crossword in the drawer, a name in the ward book, the harbour lit up below, the
baby's name, the soldier's "Everyone's agreed I fell".

#### Who's in it?

**R7** The three regulars each have what they say freely, what a check gets (only the nurse is coercible: Diplomacy
at her Will DC for the soldier's card) and what they never say, with the deflecting line. The soldier, the porter,
the cleaner and the new father live inside their events, each with their lines written there.

**R8** Five or six likely questions per regular, answered in their voices. The soldier's likely questions ("What
happened?", "Who brought you?") are covered by the event's outcomes rather than a separate map.

**R9** Not yet. Every regular and the soldier has a proposed level and archetype; the actual stat blocks, with
Perception, Will and a Deception DC, wait for the NPC generator and Ingram. The checks already point at those DCs by
name (the nurse's Will and Perception, the soldier's Deception).

**R10** In the notes: a fight, lying to the nurse, the medicine cabinet, sneaking out, splitting up and discharging
themselves, each with its roll and consequence. There is no danger event, as the scene's allowance says.

#### Is it well said?

**R15** The read-aloud was read aloud: it runs as one person describing a room, with the joke of the Gentlemen's
Outfitting sign and the coin-bounce sheets to end on.

**R21** Full sentences throughout; no clipped fragments in the read-aloud, beats or events. Changed: the outro line
was rewritten so it describes what happens rather than what anyone thinks of breakfast.

**R22** No coined word needs explaining here beyond what the table already has. "Directorate kit" and "candidate" are
heard from the first scenes; "harbour pilot" is explained by Mr Aucourt when asked.

**R23** Ten to twenty minutes of play, the same length as the other rests: a short read-aloud, one turn each, and a
deck of ten.

#### Does it hold up?

**R11** The retellable moment is likely the crossword finished for the first time in twenty years, or the new father
on the back stairs with his chrysanthemums.

**R13** Cited: `directorate.base` (the island) and `directorate.gate` and `minkai.own-forces` (the soldier and where
he was brought from). New and uncited, and Claude's own, for Ingram to accept or change: Ward Four and its Gentlemen's
Outfitting sign, the maternity ward in the old toy department, the three regulars, Gennai the porter, the city
cleaning firm and its rule, the spring drill, and Private Kuroki and his sister. None of these is yet a fact in
`canon/world/`; the soldier's truth sits only in his event note.

**R14** The plan puts this on day 1, at night, after Flood Tunnel Clearance. "Today" in the prompts means that first
day, and the soldier "came in this afternoon", which agrees.

**R24** The hospital is on the island, and the soldier is brought over the causeway from the gate at its land end,
which agrees with the base as described. Its map footprint is the island-centre site; the location is not yet in
canon, which the check notes.

**R25** Everything the approved idea promised is here: full HP, no buff, no roll for the rest, the soldier with a
wrist he won't explain, the crossword aloud, a patient talking in her sleep. Ingram's D050 reasoning (the sober
choice against the gamble) is honoured by making sure no event on the ward can add a benefit.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for Nurse Tsubaki, Mrs Imai, Mr Aucourt and Private Kuroki (waiting on the NPC generator).
- The hospital as a canon location, and the new people and details above as provisional facts, if Ingram keeps them.
- Whether the twenty extra beds should be tied to Episode Two's exercise, or kept vaguer.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors (what their attitude opens and closes): Nurse Tsubaki opens the three o'clock kettle and a bed on any night, and closes with the night book going to the section officer and, at hostile, no bed without a reason. Mrs Imai joins the island-exchange faction and Mr Aucourt the harbour-hands. One upgrade, Beds by the night desk (a disease or poison save at the two o'clock rounds). Two setbacks: Twenty extra beds (temporary, from the tea trolley) and Nurse Tsubaki retires (permanent). No danger event here, so no fight. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent a night on the ward, in character, and wakes with all their Hit Points back and nothing more, knowing the night nurse and the long-stay patients a little, with a standing among them that the next visit will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the ward, put each character to bed with a turn to say what brought them in, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls and chances to talk in character; none of them changes the rest. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** Ward Four of the General Hospital, on the island, late at night.  
**First thing.** The quiet, and the smell of carbolic soap and floor polish.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Nurse Tsubaki's bottom drawer is full of postcards from patients who went home, and she keeps every one. If a character is kind to her, or asks what she's smiling at, she shows them one.

**1. Read the ward, then let Nurse Tsubaki put each character to bed.**

*Facts:* tells the table `directorate.base`; GM only none.

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "Nurse Tsubaki has her pen over your admission card. What happened to you today, and where does it hurt?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

*Facts:* tells the table `minkai.own-forces`, `directorate.gate`; GM only none.

**4. At six the day nurse comes on, the blinds go up, and breakfast arrives on a tray: rice, pickles and weak tea. Apply the rest: every character here has all their Hit Points back.**

**Ends when.** The day nurse comes on at six and breakfast arrives, or the group discharge themselves.
**Into the next scene.** Breakfast is rice, pickles and weak tea on a tray, and Nurse Tsubaki's last act before she goes home is to watch every patient eat it. Morning.

**Write down:**

- What each character told Nurse Tsubaki had happened to them.
- What they learned about the soldier in bed eight, and who asked.
- Whether anyone noticed Aiko's cut hand and got it treated.

### Scene 2: An Early Night *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

Built on the approved idea in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md` ("The night's rest", number 2): the bunk in the overflow block
where half the furniture hasn't arrived, half HP, and a deck of dormitory life (a snorer, a homesick roommate, a
lights-out inspection, the radiator). All four promised things are in: the snorer is Grenna's event, the homesick
roommate is Haru's, the inspection is "Lights, please", and the radiator is in the place itself, said on every visit.
The rest is half HP and nothing else, as the idea has it; the deck carries one modest buff of its own (Haru's plums).

#### The questions

**Does every part earn its place?**

- **R1, earns its place.** The place (`haunt`) is read on every arrival and sets the jokes the deck pays off (plywood
  walls, crates, radiator, Wash House). Each regular is someone a player can talk to, with a want. Each event gives a
  player something to roll, say or decide: five rolls (Performance, Stealth, Crafting, Diplomacy, Athletics), one buff,
  two role-play prompts, one dilemma and one informant. The held-in-reserve pencil map is a thing a player can find and
  keep. Changed: the radiator was going to be its own buff event; it was only texture, so it moved into `everyVisit`.
- **R2, worse without it.** Each event is a different idea and none repeats another haunt's. Cutting the dilemma would
  lose the one event that mirrors the city changing; cutting the informant would lose the one glimpse of how the island
  works.
- **R16, directions.** Two kept, both about delivery that matters: "Roll it for the player in secret" on the Stealth
  check (Conceal an Object is a secret check), and "Take a moment" in the role-play prompts, which is Ingram's own
  instruction to give players time to think. Nothing else tells her how to deliver a line.
- **R17, negatives.** None in the GM text. Mrs Morishita's rules ("no food in the rooms") are her lines, not
  directions.
- **R18, prepared for what can't happen.** Nothing. There is no speech anyone must sit through; every regular can be
  talked to at any time.

**Is it clean?**

- **R3, clean.** No decision numbers, dates, tools, files or other scenes in anything the GM reads. The warden's
  jokes about the roof, and the pencilled ladder in Row E, are things in the world, not references to another haunt.
  Changed: Haru's answer "the bit with the tunnel was good" named the night's duty from inside the scene before it,
  and assumed he had been there; it is now "some of it was good".

**Does it give away too much?**

- **R12, early reveals.** Nothing the table shouldn't hear. No founders, no rift, no screening results, nothing about
  anyone's blood or nature. Read for the uncited: the informant says the warden's card goes to the section officers
  and "the office reads all of them". That is ordinary office life on the island, and it names no watcher and suspects
  nothing. Changed: the first draft said "somebody, somewhere, reads all of them", which leaned toward a mystery this
  scene has no allowance for; it is now plainly the office.
- **R19, answers or questions.** The scene gives small answers only (which dryer eats coins, when the real timetable
  goes up), which is what an informant is for. The larger things stay questions: why the overflow block has been
  "temporary" for six years, and what the section officers do with the card. Neither is answered here.
- **R20, telling the players what it means.** No line does. The dilemma's note tells the GM that either choice is fair
  and that the change comes back; nothing tells the players what the crates stand for.

**Do the players roll?**

- **R4, a roll where they act.** Yes: whistling, hiding food, mending the bunk, talking Haru round, the pillow fight,
  and saving the names in the dilemma. The two role-play prompts have no roll on purpose: they are for talking.
- **R5, the dilemma through rolls.** The choice itself is a choice, but a player who wants a third way (save the
  names and take the lockers) gets one through Society, Crafting or Thievery, with four outcomes.
- **R6, all four degrees.** Every roll has all four. Each critical success gives something extra (the block's
  whistler, "tidiest room", Grenna's approval, Haru's seat at breakfast, prisoners of war, the porters signing the lid).
  Each failure moves the night on. Each critical failure escalates: the block wakes, the warden confiscates everything,
  the bunk folds, Haru knocks on the warden's door, feathers, or nobody gets lockers.

**Who's in it?**

- **R7, free, on a check, never.** Mrs Morishita gives the rules, the furniture saga, the Wash House and her own
  history freely; on a Diplomacy check at her Will DC (friendly or better) she says what the card says; she never
  discusses the card's contents otherwise ("Heads. Counted.") or her biscuit tin. Haru talks freely about his village,
  his grandmother and the day; he never says why he came, and deflects with "It's a good opportunity. Everyone says."
  Grenna talks about home, mending and the block; she never discusses the snoring and deflects by offering to fix
  something. Sachi, the informant, is a one-off and gives everything freely.
- **R8, likely questions.** Five or six per regular, answered in their voices.
- **R9, stat blocks.** **Missing.** Each regular carries a proposed level and archetype (all level 1, non-combat) to
  be made with Ingram once the NPC generator exists. Checks cite each regular's Will or Perception DC with the
  level-based DC (15 at level 1) as the fallback until then. Sachi and the porters have none; they never need to roll.
- **R10, if they do something else.** `notes` covers sneaking out (Stealth against her Perception DC, and the card),
  lying (Deception), stealing or raiding the biscuit tin (Thievery), starting a real fight, splitting across rooms, and
  walking out.

**Is it well said?**

- **R15, read aloud.** Read through out loud: six sentences, each one breath or two, ending on the radiator knocking
  like somebody at the door, which is the joke the table will hear all night.
- **R21, fragments.** None found in the GM text. Voice samples are short because they are speech.
- **R22, coined words.** None needed. "Candidate" is what the table has been called all day; "Frame" is not said.
  "Second-termer" explains itself in the informant's own line ("I was you last term").
- **R23, length.** A downtime menu, the same length as the other haunt: ten events, three regulars, four short beats.

**Does it hold up?**

- **R11, one thing to retell.** Grenna's mother's three pages of snoring remedies, ending "stop blaming the friend,
  Grenna"; or the bunk folding like a deckchair and the warden being delighted.
- **R13, facts agree and are cited.** **Not cited.** The scene tells no fact from `canon/world/`, and the beats' fact
  lists are empty, as in the other haunt. It does state things about the place that are mine and not yet canon: Row C,
  Block 3 as first-term overflow at the landing end, packing crates for nightstands, the Wash House next door and
  Thursday washing, Block 7 as second-term housing further down the row, a ladder in Row E, the last train on the
  causeway (which agrees with the provisional causeway-and-railway fact), the warden's card, and six years of
  "temporary". They should become provisional facts if Ingram keeps them. The place itself is not in canon yet (the
  check notes `row-c-block-3`).
- **R14, story time.** The plan puts this on Day 1, at night, after the night's duty. "Tonight" and "from tomorrow"
  agree with that. Changed: the warden's line about lights at half past ten now says the group are already late, since
  they arrive after the duty; her inspection event is her last round; the morning bell no longer has a fixed hour, so
  it cannot clash with the next morning's scenes.
- **R24, places.** Checked against the map: Block 3, the Wash House and Row E, Block 9 are all at the landing end and
  close together. Changed: the first draft had the second-termer talking "across the path" from Block 7, which is about
  a hundred and fifty metres away; she now stops at the window on her way back from the Wash House.
- **R25, all her detail.** Ingram's words on haunts are in: a menu, not a script; skill rolls that produce results;
  a fun buff; chances to role-play, with "tell us about your character's day" asked in full and time to think; a
  dilemma that mirrors the city changing (the steel lockers replacing the crates with every intake's names in them);
  an informant with an unconsidered line of information, shared willingly (how the first week really goes, and who
  reads the warden's card); regulars whose attitudes move with the events, including by accident (the pillow that
  bursts). Allowance respected: no plot event and no danger event.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for Mrs Morishita, Haru and Grenna (the NPC generator).
- The place's own facts in `canon/world/`, and `row-c-block-3` in canon.
- The scene is not yet copied to where Foundry reads it; that happens when the scenes are built together.
- Ingram's read of all of it.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors: Mrs Morishita (block-wardens) closes with a -2 to Stealth and Deception against her and, at hostile, a corridor mattress and a quarter HP; Haru and Grenna have none of a faction. Upgrades: The furniture (three-quarters HP) and The radiator. Setbacks: A crowded floor (temporary, quarter HP) and A block like the others (permanent). No danger event. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent a night in the block, in character, and wakes with half their HP back and a standing with the warden and their neighbours that the next night will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the room, give each player a turn to say how their character makes the bunk their own, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls and chances to talk in character. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** Row C, Block 3, the first-term overflow block at the landing end of the island, late at night.  
**First thing.** The warden in the doorway with a torch and a clipboard, counting them in.  
**If a question isn't covered.** On the underside of the top bunk in the group's room, where only the person on the bottom bunk would ever see it, somebody from an earlier intake has drawn a map of the island's rooftops in pencil. A ladder in Row E is marked with a star and the words "nobody locks it".

**1. Read the block, then let Mrs Morishita count them in and point them to their room.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "Top bunk or bottom? What's the one thing your character puts on the crate beside the bed to make it theirs?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. The block falls quiet at last, apart from the radiator and Grenna. Apply the rest: half of each character's maximum HP. The morning bell rings, and Mrs Morishita is already in the corridor with her clipboard.**

**Ends when.** Everyone in the block is asleep, or the morning bell rings.
**Into the next scene.** The last train of the night rattles along the causeway, the radiator knocks once more, and the block sleeps. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Whether Haru's bag is packed or unpacked in the morning.
- Lockers or crates, and whether the names were saved.
- Whose names are on labels in Mrs Morishita's biscuit tin.
- Who is the block's official whistler.

### Scene 3: The Bathhouse *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

Built on the approved idea in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md` ("The night's rest", number 3): half HP, and automatically
temporary HP equal to the character's level until the next rest; a deck of regulars (dockworkers, a retired Frame
fitter with stories, the attendant who runs the place like a ship). All of that is kept. The shape, length and voice
follow The Crew Mess.

#### Does every part earn its place?

- **R1** Every part is something the GM runs or a player does. The read-aloud sets the room; `your-bath` gives each
  player a turn to speak in character; the deck gives rolls (five), buffs (two), a role-play prompt, a dilemma and an
  informant; the closing beat applies the rest. The letter under the brass weight (`holdInReserve`) is there for a
  player who asks, and seeds the dilemma. The milk at closing is something to do and laugh at (hand on hip, facing the
  mural). Nothing was cut; nothing was found idle.
- **R2** Each event is a different kind of fun: a custom to get right, an endurance contest, a picture to search, a
  farce, a board game, a kindness, a small gift, an argument, a gossip and a story. Taking any one out loses its idea
  from the haunt.
- **R16** Directions kept only where delivery matters: "Give them a moment to picture it" in `your-bath`, "Take a
  moment first" in the scars prompt (Ingram's own ask: time to think before speaking), and the dilemma's note on which
  attitude buttons to press, which the Runner can't tell on its own.
- **R17** No negatives to the GM. The notes say what happens, not what to avoid. Two NPC lines ("never repeats what
  anyone said about a candidate by name", Kazue "never turns it over while anyone is looking") describe the people, not
  the GM.
- **R18** Nothing prepares for what the shape rules out. It is all interruptible conversation.

#### Is it clean?

- **R3** Nothing the GM reads mentions how it was made: no decision numbers, dates, tools, files or other scenes. The
  one reference outward is "the overflow block" in Mori's gossip, which is a place in the world the characters sleep
  in, not a scene. The check found no path outside `canon/`.

#### Does it give away too much?

- **R12** Nothing about the founders, the rift, why the Directorate came, other ships, the screening or the player
  characters' nature. Mori's gossip is about beds and surveyors, and he explicitly never repeats anything said about a
  candidate by name. **Deliberately left out: glamours.** A bath is where a disguise would matter most, and any rule
  or joke about glamours in the water could press on a player's private choice about their character's disguise, so the
  scene never raises them.
- **R19** The answers the scene gives are small and ordinary (the rules, the regulars' lives, the offer). The offer is
  held back twice: behind the weight, then behind a Diplomacy check at Kazue's Will DC, and the informant tells it from
  a second angle (the surveyors), so it can be found two ways.
- **R20** No line tells the players what anything means. The dilemma's note says both answers are fair, for the GM.

#### Do the players roll?

- **R4** Five roll events, a check on the dilemma, and checks for every "something else" in the notes. The two buffs
  and the role-play event have no roll on purpose: they are the fun buffs and the chance to speak that Ingram asked for.
  The informant has none: Mori shares willingly.
- **R5** Yes, for the harder answer. Keep or sell is a spoken choice with attitude costs either way; arguing for a third
  way is a Diplomacy roll against Kazue's Will DC with four outcomes.
- **R6** Every roll has all four. Critical successes give something extra and lasting (a numbered bucket, the champion's
  peg, finding young Hachi and Kazue in the mural, a friend in the locker deck, a rematch forever, Kazue writing the
  idea down). Failures move the night on. Critical failures escalate (the pool drained, a week's ban from the hot pool,
  paint off the mural, forty dockworkers in one heap, a nickname, both regulars turning on the character).

#### Who's in it?

- **R7** Three regulars, each with `willDiscuss`, `willNotDiscuss` with the deflecting line in their voice, and what a
  check gets: Kazue's offer (Diplomacy at her Will DC, friendly or better), Hachi's advice on turning a Frame on the
  ball of the foot (Diplomacy at his Will DC, friendly or better). Sigrun has nothing behind a check; her soft spot (her
  brother) she guards with "Next year. Get in the pool." Mori the stoker and the stevedore are walk-ons inside events.
- **R8** Five or six likely questions each, answered in voice.
- **R9** **Missing: stat blocks.** Each regular has a proposed level and archetype only (Kazue level 2, Hachi level 1,
  Sigrun level 1), to be made with Ingram once the NPC generator exists. Until then the Will and Perception DCs the
  events name have no numbers; the level-based DC (15 at level 1) stands in where the event offers it. Mori and the
  stevedore need none unless a fight starts.
- **R10** The notes cover fighting, lying, stealing from the baskets, sneaking to the boiler room, crossing to the other
  side, splitting up across the wall, and leaving before bathing (half HP and no temporary HP). Following a regular
  home was missing and is added: Stealth against their Perception DC, where each one lives, and what happens to a
  follower who is caught.

#### Is it well said?

- **R15** The read-aloud was read aloud: one breath per sentence, the whistle as the joke, and the story in the steam as
  the hand-off to Hachi.
- **R21** Full sentences throughout; no fragment lists.
- **R22** Shogi is explained where it appears (Minkai's chess, wedge-shaped pieces, captured pieces come back). A
  boatswain is explained in Kazue's history. The speaking pipe is explained in its event and in Kazue's topics. "Frame"
  and "candidate" are the campaign's words the table already has by nightfall.
- **R23** The length of The Crew Mess, for a 10 to 20 minute visit with a deck to draw from.

#### Does it hold up?

- **R11** The candidate who wakes up on the tiles with a crane driver fanning them with a washbowl, or the one whose name
  hangs on the champion's peg; or the whole bathhouse sitting on stools while the big pool is refilled.
- **R13** Canon it leans on: the Directorate's base is its own island in the harbour with barracks and workshops
  (directorate.md); Frames are tall, clumsy, purely mechanical and mostly seen lifting girders and crates of cabbage
  (frames.md), which Hachi's cabbage-crate story fits. Everything else (the regulars, the mural, the offer, the speaking
  pipe) is the place's own and new, and cites no fact, as in The Crew Mess. The quays, the east-quay crane and the dry
  dock the dockworkers come from are assumed to be on the island; Ingram should confirm the island has working quays.
- **R14** Day 1, night, after Flood Tunnel Clearance, as the plan's table has it. The scene says "tonight" and "today"
  only, and the flood tunnel's mud is the one link to the day.
- **R24** The bathhouse (`the-bathhouse-447`) is on the map in the middle of the island, and the scene puts it there.
  The mural shows the old town low along the waterfront, which agrees with the map. The check notes the location is not
  in canon yet.
- **R25** All of the plan's promises are kept: half HP, automatic temporary HP equal to level, and the three kinds of
  regular. All of the task's events are here: etiquette, the hottest pool (Fortitude), the mural and its painter, the
  basket muddle, a board game on the bench, a back to scrub, old-timers arguing about the bathhouse being sold for
  Directorate housing (the dilemma that mirrors the city), and the one who hears everything (Mori, through the pipe).
  From D050 and D051: rolls with four outcomes, buffs, prompts with time to think, a dilemma that mirrors the city, an
  informant sharing willingly, and attitude buttons on the events. No plot or danger events, as allowed.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for Kazue, Hachi and Sigrun (R9), waiting for the NPC generator.
- The two buffs are new and unbalanced by play: +1 circumstance to Athletics (or the end of Fatigued) from Hachi's
  scrub, and +1 circumstance to Diplomacy to Make an Impression from Kazue's press. Both last until the next rest,
  alongside the rest's own temporary HP.
- Whether the island has its own working quays and a crane on the east quay (R13).

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors: Kazue withholds the temporary HP at unfriendly and bars the door at hostile; Hachi joins repair-crews and Sigrun harbour-hands, so the offer now splits two factions. Upgrade: The new boiler (three-quarters HP). Setbacks: The boiler cracks (temporary) and The curtain comes down (permanent, the offer taken). No danger event. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent an evening in the bath among the island's working people, in character, and leaves clean, with half their HP back, temporary HP until their next rest, and a standing with the regulars that the next visit will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the room, give each player a turn to say how their character takes a bath, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, buffs and chances to talk in character. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The Bathhouse, in the middle of the island, late at night.  
**First thing.** The heat and the steam as the curtain drops behind them, and a brass whistle blowing because somebody has walked in with their boots on.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Behind Kazue's high seat, a letter on Directorate paper is pinned face down under a brass weight. She never turns it over while anyone is looking. It is the housing office's offer for the bathhouse.

**1. Read the room. Kazue stops them at the step, takes their boots, and gives them the rules once.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "How does your character take a bath? Long and slow, in and out, or only when someone makes them? And where in all this steam do they end up?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. Kazue rings the ship's bell over her seat for closing, and hands every bather a cold bottle of milk on the way out, to be drunk standing up with one hand on the hip, facing the mural. Apply the rest: half of maximum HP, and temporary HP equal to each character's level until their next rest.**

**Ends when.** Kazue rings the ship's bell for closing, or the group leave.
**Into the next scene.** The night air is cold after the steam, and the bottles of milk are colder, and the walk back to the bunks is quiet. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Who has a numbered bucket on the shelf, and whose name hangs on the champion's peg.
- What each character told Kazue about the offer.
- Who handed a uniform to Kazue to be pressed, and who had their back scrubbed by Hachi.

### Scene 4: The Shrine Gate *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

A night's rest and a haunt, built on the approved idea in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md` ("The night's rest", item 4): a
quarter of maximum HP, and each character draws a fortune (great blessing: a Hero Point; blessing: a +1 status bonus to
one check before their next rest; ordinary: a line to keep; curse: tied to the rope and left behind). The fortune is a
d10 table with ten slips, in the rest's `buff`. Two regulars, ten events, one `danger`, no `plot`.

#### For you, while reading

These are mine, not yet yours, and none is in `canon/world/` yet:
- **The shrine** belongs to the Net-Mender, a kami (a small local spirit) of the old town's fishermen who mends what
  is torn. The keeper says the fishermen left offerings on the rock before there was an island, and the builders laid
  the streets round the shrine rather than move it. That is his story, told in his voice; the rock itself is canon
  (`directorate.base`: the island was "built up around a rock").
- **Tokuji Tsuruta**, the keeper, seventy-eight, a retired old-town fisherman who crosses on the last train every night;
  his son works on the island and thinks the shrine is nonsense; he can't write well.
- **Mira Kostel**, a switchboard operator on the island's night exchange, from a mountain country far to the west,
  who prays to her own "lady of stars and roads, with the butterflies" at home (Golarion's Desna, not named) and to
  the Net-Mender on her way home. She wishes every night for her brother's travel papers.
- **The works office** plans to widen the street and move the shrine to a plinth in the office gardens.
- **Linesmen switch off and swing aside street lamps** on a Frame's route before it walks through the island at night.
- **Kaito**, a crane hand laid off when new cranes came, in debt to a lender in the city, who has been taking coins
  from the offering box.

#### The questions

- **R1, earns its place.** The rest gives HP and the fortune; the regulars give two people to talk to with wants and
  soft spots; every event gives a player something to do, say, roll, decide or keep (a wish, a lap cat, a stolen glove,
  a festival debt, a letter, a way to spot a Frame moving). The holdInReserve stone is for a table that lingers.
- **R2, worse without it.** Each event carries a different idea: speaking a wish, doing the rite right, an animal, the
  city's wishes in writing, a festival, the city squeezing the shrine, a behind-the-scenes trick, a wish made for
  someone else, a quiet buff, and a danger. None repeats the Crew Mess (no drink, no brawl, no petition to sign).
- **R16, directions.** Two kept as critical: "Give them a moment to picture it" and "Take a moment" in prompts, which
  are Ingram's own instruction for role-play prompts; and the danger's "This happens on the walk home, after the
  fortunes", because a drawn event would otherwise land at the wrong moment.
- **R17, negatives.** None in the GM's directions. The "don't" and "won't" that remain are in characters' mouths or
  describe what an NPC does ("won't chase far").
- **R18, prepared for what can't happen.** No. Nothing is uninterruptible, and every question has someone to answer it.
- **R3, clean.** No decision numbers, dates, files, tools or other scenes in anything the GM reads. The Crew Mess is
  never mentioned. The bathhouse nearby on the map is left out on purpose, so no other haunt is referenced.
- **R12, early reveals.** Nothing from the forbidden list: no founders, no rift, no other ship, no screening, nothing
  about the candidates' blood or nature. Every fact the scene states is known to everyone in Kurose and told at the
  start (the old town, newcomers, organised crime, change making winners and losers), or is new and ordinary to this
  place. The one fortune I rewrote: a draft great blessing about the sea bringing everything home again read as a
  hint about lost founders or far-off homes, so it became "The Net-Mender has mended your net already."
- **R19, answers or questions.** The scene answers only small, local things. The keeper's letter, his son and his own
  wish board are held behind attitude and rolls (Diplomacy at his Will DC, Perception critical success), so the table
  finds them rather than being told. The linesman's trick is the one piece of behind-the-scenes knowledge, and it
  is ordinary.
- **R20, meaning.** No line says what the shrine or the letter means. The dilemma's note lays out the cost of each
  answer for the GM only, and says either is fair.
- **R4, rolls.** Religion (the rite), Nature (the cat), Perception (the wish boards; spotting the men on the road),
  Crafting or Performance (the festival), Society (the letter), Diplomacy, Intimidation and Deception (the road), and
  Thievery and Deception in the notes. The wish, Mira's wish and the broom are role-play with no roll, by design.
- **R5, dilemma through rolls.** Yes: the letter is decided by what the player writes, and how the office receives it
  is a Society check with four results.
- **R6, degrees.** Every roll has all four. Critical successes give something extra (the rock story, the Abbot's hoard
  and a ring to return, the keeper's own wish board, a place in the festival, a meeting with the works office,
  Kaito's confession). Failures move on. Critical failures escalate (the bell off its hook and lights on across the
  street, a stolen glove, forty boards on the ground, a festival debt, the move brought forward, a fight aimed at the
  speaker and a purse gone).
- **R7, NPC maps.** Both regulars have willDiscuss, willNotDiscuss with the line they deflect with, and the keeper's
  letter is coercible (friendly or better, Diplomacy at his Will DC). Mira's one secret, what she hears on the lines,
  is never coercible, and says why. The linesman and Kaito are walk-ons inside events, with what they say written in.
- **R8, likely questions.** Six each, in their voices.
- **R9, stat blocks.** Missing, honestly: the keeper and Mira (proposed level 0, non-combat), the lender's men
  (proposed level 1 thugs with clubs), and Kaito (proposed level -1) all wait for the NPC generator. Their Perception,
  Will and Deception DCs are referenced but not yet numbers.
- **R10, if the group does something else.** In `notes`: attack, lie, steal from the offering box, go round the back,
  follow Mira, split, walk out early. Sneaking round the back is covered by what's there.
- **R15, read-aloud.** Read it aloud: it runs as one person describing a corner at midnight, and ends by explaining the
  kami in a single sentence.
- **R21, fragments.** Fortune slips are traditionally terse; every slip is written in full sentences instead. The read-
  aloud and event texts have no clipped lists of nouns. The rite in the Religion event is a list of steps, which the
  keeper would say that way.
- **R22, coined words.** "Kami" is explained where the players first hear it, in the read-aloud. "Net-Mender" is
  explained in the same sentence. "Frame" appears only in the linesman's event, and the
  players have heard it explained before this night.
- **R23, length.** About the Crew Mess's length: a menu of ten events for a ten-to-twenty-minute rest.
- **R11, the retold moment.** The Abbot stealing a candidate's glove and having it waiting on the next visit, or the
  player who wishes for Mira's brother.
- **R13, canon.** Agrees with `directorate.base` (island built around a rock, fence, offices, billets),
  `kurose.old-town` and `kurose.first-families-stayed` (the keeper), `directorate.newcomers` and
  `directorate.newcomers-built` (Mira and her own god), `kurose.left-behind` and `kurose.winners-and-losers` (Kaito and
  the new cranes), `kurose.organised-crime` (the lender), `kurose.everyday` (electric light, trams). The facts are not
  yet cited in the beats' `facts` arrays, which stay empty as in the Crew Mess; the new facts above wait for Ingram.
- **R14, story time.** Day 1, night, after Flood Tunnel Clearance, matching the plan. The scene says "tonight",
  "midnight" and "a very long first day", which agree.
- **R24, places.** The map puts the shrine in the island's centre, among offices and billets, which is what the scene
  describes. `the-shrine-gate-444` is not in `canon/` yet (the check notes it). Whether the island's original rock
  stands at this corner is the keeper's claim, not stated as fact; if Ingram wants it true, the map should agree.
- **R25, all her detail.** From D050 and D051: a quarter of HP, the fortune exactly as approved, roll-heavy with buffs
  and role-play, a dilemma that mirrors the changing city (the road widening), an informant with an unconsidered line
  (the linesman), an unannounced danger with ways out that aren't a fight and a theatre-of-the-mind fight if it comes,
  regulars whose attitudes move with events. The brief's "who else comes to a shrine at night" is the keeper, Mira,
  the linesman and Kaito.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for everyone, as above.
- A place record in `canon/` for the shrine.
- The fortune slips as a printable handout, if Ingram wants one to hand over.
- `node tools/story/story.mjs build` has not been run, so the scene is not yet copied into `data/`.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors: the keeper (harbour-hands) opens the fishermen's stone and his lantern on the dark road, and closes the stick box at unfriendly and the gate at hostile; Mira joins island-exchange. Upgrades: Net-Mending Night and The corner lamp. Setbacks: Moved to the gardens (permanent, from the letter) and The rope comes down (temporary). The dark road has a fight: the bigger of the lender's men is the one to get down, and both give up when badly hurt. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

**Corrected after the D052 retrofit (2026-09-29):** Tokuji Tsuruta's cast id was "genzo", which collided with Genzō Amemiya at the met office and Isamu Hamada on the roof (three different regulars sharing one id, only harmless because attitudes are kept per place). Renamed to "tokuji".

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent a quiet hour at the shrine, in character, and leaves with a quarter of their HP back, a fortune slip that might be worth something tomorrow, and a standing with the keeper and his regular visitor that the next visit will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the gate, give each player a turn to say what their character does first, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, a wish, a fortune and chances to talk in character. Before they leave, everyone draws a fortune. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The Shrine Gate, a small roadside shrine on a corner in the middle of the island, around midnight.  
**First thing.** The quiet: a broom going back and forth on stone, and a bell somebody rang a minute ago still humming.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Under the little roof, the grey stone has a smooth groove worn into one side, the shape of a thumb. The keeper says fishermen touched it for luck before they went out, for longer than anyone has been counting. A character he likes, friendly or better, may touch it too.

**1. Read the gate. The keeper looks up from his broom and points at the stone basin.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "Your character has walked through the gate at midnight at the end of a very long first day. What do they do first, and what are they still carrying from today?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. The keeper sets the stick box on the step. Each character shakes it and draws a fortune; the slips are listed with the rest. A curse gets tied to the rope before they go. Then the keeper puts out his lantern, and the rest is applied.**

**Ends when.** The keeper finishes sweeping and puts out his lantern, or the group leave.
**Into the next scene.** The road back to the bunks runs dark between the office blocks, and for a while the only light behind them is the shrine's lantern, and then that goes out too. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Each character's fortune: who holds a blessing's +1 or a Hero Point, and whose curse is tied to the rope.
- What each character wished for, if the player told you.
- What the letter to the works office said, if one was written.
- What anyone owes Net-Mending Night, and anything the Abbot took.
- What happened on the dark road, and whether Kaito's secret reached the keeper.

### Scene 5: The Crew Mess *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

The first haunt (`D051`), written as the model for the other nine. Built on the approved idea in the plan ("The bar:
where the repair crews drink when a refit finishes") and on Ingram's example, word for word in D051: "Did the group
trash the bar by accident by failing a skill roll? Whoops! That lowered the attitude..." That is `the-keg`.

**Does every part earn its place?**
- **R1** Every event gives a player something to roll, say, decide or keep; every regular has a want and a line per
  attitude. The slate is the room's memory and pays for the attitude system.
- **R2** Cutting any event leaves the deck thinner, not broken. That is the point of a deck.
- **R16** One direction survives, in `howToPlay` and `the-night`: how many events to draw. Everything else is a prompt
  or a result.
- **R17** No "don't". `ren`'s deflection ("Doesn't matter.") is a line, not a direction.
- **R18** No preparation for something the shape rules out.

**Is it clean?**
- **R3** Nothing mentions how it was made. One GM note names canon facts by id in the plot event, which is how canon
  notes point at the truth.

**Does it give away too much?**
- **R12** The plot event (`the-one-who-paid`) plants a question: a founder once came in and paid the slate, and
  looked like a picture older than the fitter. It names nobody and confirms nothing. It leans on `founders.alive` and
  the founders' look, both held for later; the table only hears an old man's story. **For Ingram:** whether even that
  story is too early is her call; drawing it can simply wait for a later episode (`episodes`).
- **R19** The crews' insight (the fitness list, which Frames are unsound) is given as talk, never as fact about the
  plot.
- **R20** Nothing tells the players what the petition means.

**Do the players roll?**
- **R4, R5, R6** Five events roll (`stand-a-round`, `the-keg`, `name-the-part`, `the-one-who-paid`, `two-crews`), each
  with all four degrees: a critical success gives something extra (a name on the slate, a free drink, a round from the
  tug crew), a failure moves the night on, a critical failure escalates (the bar shut, a brawl aimed at them). The
  dilemma (`the-petition`) resolves by choice, not by roll, on purpose: both answers cost something.

**Who's in it?**
- **R7, R8** Tomoe, Petr and Ren each have a conversation map: what they give freely, what they won't say and why,
  what leaning on them gets, and five or six likely questions answered in their voice.
- **R9** **Missing:** stat blocks. Each regular carries a proposed level and archetype, and the tug-hands in
  `two-crews` need brawler stat blocks. All wait for the NPC generator.
- **R10** In `notes`: leaving without paying, stealing, lying about being pilots, searching, splitting.

**Is it well said?**
- **R15** The read-aloud was read aloud; it is five full sentences, ending on the singer.
- **R21** No clipped fragments.
- **R22** "The slate" and "refit" are explained in the read-aloud and Tomoe's lines. "Frame" is known from the start.
- **R23** A haunt is long in deck and short in beats; this is about 2,500 words, most of it optional.

**Does it hold up?**
- **R11** The keg going through the mirror, or the toast to the wrong crew.
- **R13** Facts agree with canon: Frames are old and hard to drive (told from the start); the founders' Founding Day
  print is in supporters' windows (`founders.painting`); no founder is named or gendered.
- **R14** Day 1, night, in the plan. No time words that disagree.
- **R24** The Crew Mess is behind the dry dock, as the map has it.
- **R25** D050 and D051 read in full: the quarter HP, the bar-versus-hospital choice (the morning Fortitude save), the
  attitude system, the trashed bar, a dilemma mirroring the city (the petition), an informant, one plot event, one
  danger event with ways out.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** All three regulars join repair-crews, shared with Hachi at the Bathhouse and the night shift. Tomoe closes credit at unfriendly and service at hostile. Upgrades: On the crews' slate and The back room. Setbacks: Shut for a week (temporary) and Crew Three moved on (permanent). Two crews, one grudge has a fight: the big tug-hand is the one to get down, and tug-hands give up when knocked down. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent an evening among the repair crews, in character, and leaves with a quarter of their HP back, a hangover risk, and a standing with the regulars that the next visit will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the room, give each player a turn to say how their character spends the evening, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, buffs and chances to talk in character. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The Crew Mess, a bar behind the dry dock, late at night.  
**First thing.** The noise: a room full of repair crews, and somebody singing badly at the far end.  
**If a question isn't covered.** The slate has one line rubbed out and never rewritten: a crew that stopped coming in. Anyone who asks gets a shrug from Tomoe and a long look from Petr.

**1. Read the room, then let Petr wave them over.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "What does your character order, and where in this room do they end up sitting?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. Tomoe calls time by ringing a spanner on the armour plate. Apply the rest. Tomorrow morning, before breakfast, each character who drank rolls the Fortitude save.**

**Ends when.** Tomoe rings the spanner on the armour plate for closing, or the group leave.
**Into the next scene.** The walk back to the bunks is cold after the room, and quiet. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Whose name went on the slate, and what each character owes.
- Whether anyone signed the petition.
- Who failed the Fortitude save in the morning.

### Scene 6: The Card Game *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

#### What the scene is

A haunt (`design/haunts.md`) built on the approved idea in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md`, "The night's rest", item 6, and
the place's own note: a block-wide card game running longer than anyone living there has been a candidate, stakes of
chores and favours, and a debt board behind the common-room door taken with total seriousness. A quarter of maximum HP;
no automatic buff, because the game is the gamble. The plan's "pot that grows with level" became the board itself:
the stakes are chores and favours, never money, and the roll is at the level-based DC, so it stays as live at level 10
as at level 1. The plan's "a critical failure means you owe someone" is kept as the critical-failure result, and "the
regulars know every candidate on the island" is kept as the informant.

The game invented for it is **Last Ferry**: four cards each; every round you pass one to your left; the first to hold
four of one suit slaps the table and calls "Last ferry!"; whoever holds the Ferryman (the one joker left in) when
somebody calls pays double. Michiru says it in one breath.

#### For you, while reading

These are mine, not yet yours:
- **The three regulars**: Michiru Arai (keeper), Lucan Farrow (the cheat, from Absalom), Chiyo Nakamura (never wins).
  Their three secrets are tied together: Lucan cheats to make Chiyo win, Chiyo drops every card he slips her, and
  Michiru is saving forty-one favours to wipe Chiyo's list on the day she leaves.
- **The Ninth Pier**, a harbour gang, and its two collectors, Mister Kanda and Goro, who work days in the base's coal
  yard. This assumes city men work on the island by day; it is how a city gang reaches a fenced base.
- **Candidates have leave days in the city.** Lucan's debt comes from one.
- **Warden Ogata**, Row F's block warden, who sleeps by the telephone and grows tomatoes.
- **Blue Hat**, the painted oldest debt, and the board being an old door with a brass number 3.
- **"Third-termer" and "second-termer"**: this assumes candidates stay for several terms.

#### The questions

- **R1, earns its place.** Every event gives a player something to roll, decide or say, and each regular gives the GM a
  want and a secret to play. The four beats are the frame: room, names, the night, the last hand. The coal-yard detail
  and the warden's tomatoes are there because events use them.
- **R2, worse without it.** Each event does a different job: a dilemma (the cheat), a custom (Blue Hat), a raise
  (something that isn't a chore), a character prompt (the tell), an informant (the exercise books), danger (the Ninth
  Pier), a buff (watch the hands), and two light rolls (the tomatoes, the cat). None repeats another haunt's idea.
- **R16, directions.** Two kept: "Give them a moment before they answer" on the name beat and "Take a moment" in two
  prompts, because Ingram asked for thinking time before speaking (D051). Nothing else tells her how to deliver.
- **R17, negatives.** None in the GM text. "Chiyo mustn't know" is the secret itself, not a direction.
- **R18, prepared for what can't happen.** No. Nothing is a speech; every answer can be asked for.
- **R3, clean.** No decision numbers, dates, tools, files or other scenes in anything the GM reads. Checked by eye;
  `story.mjs check` raises nothing for this file except that the `data/` copy is not built yet.
- **R12, early reveals.** Nothing about the founders, the rift, the screening, anyone's blood, or the characters' own
  nature. Organised crime is `told: start`. The Ninth Pier is an ordinary gang with an ordinary grudge.
- **R19, answers or questions.** The one answer given freely, that the base runs on favours as much as on orders, is an
  informant's gift and harmless. Michiru's column is a question with its answer held behind a Diplomacy check, and
  Blue Hat stays a question for good.
- **R20, meaning.** No line says what anything means. The favour economy is said by Michiru as gossip, and the
  cheat dilemma's note says either answer is fair rather than which one is right.
- **R4, rolls.** Every hand is a roll; seven of nine events carry a check. The tell and the exercise books are pure
  role-play, as the deck's mix asks.
- **R5, dilemma through rolls.** Calling out the cheat is Society or Diplomacy at the level-based DC; staying quiet
  needs no roll and has its own cost (a private favour from Lucan, and complicity).
- **R6, four degrees.** Every roll pays on all four. A failure always moves the night on (the hand plays on, the night
  goes on, the hand is void, one more try). Critical failures escalate: red chalk, a debt written up for the week, a
  six o'clock tomato lecture, a fight.
- **R7, what each NPC says.** Each regular has willDiscuss, willNotDiscuss with the deflecting line, and coercible where
  there's something to earn: Michiru's column (Diplomacy at her Will DC, friendly or better), Lucan's debt (Diplomacy or
  Intimidation at his Will DC). Chiyo has nothing to force; her secret comes out only if she chooses.
- **R8, likely questions.** Five or six each, in their own voices.
- **R9, stat blocks.** Still missing. All five people (three regulars, Kanda, Goro) have a proposed level and
  archetype only, waiting for the NPC generator. The scene uses Michiru's Will and Perception DCs, Lucan's Will DC,
  Kanda's Perception and Will DCs and Goro's Perception DC, none of which exist yet.
- **R10, if they do something else.** The notes cover walking out, leaving in debt, rubbing a chore off, lying,
  attacking, sneaking upstairs, following Kanda, and splitting up, each with its roll.
- **R15, read aloud.** Read it through out loud: one breath per sentence, and it ends on Michiru's question to the table.
- **R21, fragments.** None found; the samples are spoken lines, and "Lovely sea." is Lucan dodging, which is how he
  talks.
- **R22, coined words.** Last Ferry, the Ferryman, the debt board's colours, Blue Hat and the Ninth Pier are each
  explained where they first come up. No Frame is mentioned.
- **R23, length.** Matches the Crew Mess: three regulars, four beats, nine events. A quick scene for ten to twenty
  minutes.
- **R11, the retold thing.** Chiyo winning her first hand, or the Admiral in a lap all night, or the tomatoes at six.
- **R13, facts.** Leans on `kurose.organised-crime` (decided). Everything else is new and ordinary, listed above as
  mine. Like the Crew Mess, the beats carry no fact citations; the new facts are not yet in `canon/world/`.
- **R14, story time.** Day 1, night, after the flood tunnel, as the plan has it. "Tonight" is the only time word, and
  "in the morning" for the tomatoes.
- **R24, place.** Row F, Block 5 is a candidates' block on the island, as its map entry has it; the party walk back
  "across the rows" to their own bunks. The coal yard and the causeway are on the island, as Scene 0 describes the
  base. `row-f-block-5` is not in `canon/` yet (the check notes it).
- **R25, her detail.** Used all of it: the rotating players, "longer than anyone living there has been a candidate",
  chores not money, the board behind the common-room door and its total seriousness (the game starts when the door
  shuts), Deception, Thievery or Society, owing someone on a critical failure, and the regulars knowing every
  candidate.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for all five people, from the NPC generator.
- The credits figure for Lucan's debt ("about a month of a candidate's pay"), because candidate pay isn't set.
- `node tools/story/story.mjs build` has not been run, so the `data/` copy doesn't exist yet.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** The three regulars form the card-table faction. Lucan closes with a -2 on their hand at unfriendly and red chalk at hostile; Michiru deals round their chairs at hostile. Upgrades: The stove (half HP) and Keeper of the board. Setbacks: Credits on the board (permanent) and The board taken down (temporary). The Ninth Pier has a fight: Goro is the one to get down, and Kanda gives up when Goro falls or the whistle goes. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

**Corrected after the D052 retrofit (2026-09-29):** Michiru Arai's cast id was "setsuko", which is also Mrs Imai's first name at the hospital. Renamed to "michiru".

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has sat at the table in character, played at least one hand, and leaves with a quarter of their HP back and their name on the debt board, owing something or owed something, with regulars who will remember how they played.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the room, have each player chalk their character's name on the board, deal everyone a hand, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, buffs and chances to talk in character. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The common room of Row F, Block 5, a candidates' block on the island, late at night.  
**First thing.** The door being shut behind them, and the board on the back wall that the door was hiding.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Michiru's column is at the far end of the board. Every line in it is a favour owed to her, forty-one of them, and not one has ever been called in. Anyone who asks is told it's the keeper's column and isn't in play.

**1. Read the room. Michiru explains Last Ferry in one breath, and Lucan pulls out a chair.**

**2. Michiru hands the chalk round. Ask each player, one at a time: "Your name goes on the board under 'the sinks'. What does your character write there: their own name, a nickname, or something else?" Give them a moment before they answer.**

**3. Every character who sits in plays a hand and makes the roll, and the result goes on the board. Then draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. Michiru calls the last hand, then reads the whole night's board aloud, name by name. Apply the rest.**

**Ends when.** Michiru calls the last hand and reads the board aloud, or the group leave.
**Into the next scene.** The walk back across the rows is cold and quiet, and every window is dark except the one in Row F, Block 5. Morning.

**Write down:**

- The name each character chalked on the board, and what each one owes or is owed.
- Whether anyone called out Lucan's cheating, or kept quiet.
- What happened with the Ninth Pier, if they came, and whether Lucan's debt is still owed.
- The table's guess about Blue Hat.
