### Scene 7: The Rumour Mill *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

Built on Ingram's rules in D051, word for word there: three sets an episode; a player names any skill they can
justify and rolls blind; critical failure three wrong, failure two wrong and one true, success two true and one
wrong, critical success three true; short, one sentence; some inviting a guess at what else is true; at most one per
set touching the plot. The Runner deals them (`core/rumours.mjs`).

**Does every part earn its place?**
- **R1, R2** The round is the scene; the deck fills the gaps between rounds. Every event gives a player something to
  say, roll or keep.
- **R16, R17, R18** One direction: how the round is run, which the GM needs. No "don't".

**Is it clean?**
- **R3** Nothing about how it was made. Rumours that touch the plot carry the canon fact they touch, GM-only.

**Does it give away too much?** The reason this haunt was written with the most care.
- **R12** This block's rumour mill is about the screening tests, and the players' own blood is the one subject that
  must not leak. Every rumour about "the marker" is a lie or a harmless truth (the blood draw is short and unexplained;
  a second medical in spring; the fare paid home). No rumour says anything about anyone's results, and `your-theory`
  lets players guess aloud while nobody confirms anything.
- **R19** Plot rumours, one set at a time:
  - E1 set 1, a **lie**: the founders are buried under the painting (misleads about `founders.alive`).
  - E1 set 2: **none**, to keep the medical set clean.
  - E1 set 3, a **truth already told**: nobody has said why the Directorate came to Kurose
    (`directorate.why-kurose-unexplained`, told from the start). It turns a known fact into a question.
  - E2 set 1, a **lie**: a Frame hidden in the machine-shop pit. It brushes `frames.gen-2`, held for later, and
    misleads about where anything newer would be. **For Ingram:** whether even a false hint at a newer Frame is too
    early.
  - E2 set 2: **none**.
  - E2 set 3, a **lie**: the founders never existed.
- **R20** Nothing tells the players which rumours matter.

**Do the players roll?**
- **R4, R5, R6** Every player rolls in the round. Three more events roll (`obis-expedition`, `two-knocks`,
  `the-came-true-column`), with all four degrees.

**Who's in it?**
- **R7, R8** Hana, Obi and Mrs. Ueda each have a conversation map with likely questions in their voice.
- **R9** **Missing:** stat blocks, proposed levels only, waiting for the NPC generator.
- **R10** In `notes`: reporting it, stealing the notebook, lying about the medical, splitting.

**Is it well said?**
- **R15, R21** The read-aloud is full sentences. Rumours are one sentence each, as asked.
- **R22** "The marker" is explained where it is first heard (Hana's answer).

**Does it hold up?**
- **R11** A player's own made-up rumour coming back changed, from somebody else.
- **R13** Rumour truths agree with canon or with the other haunts written the same day (the slate, the fitness list,
  the observer's twenty years, the card game's age). Shucks Mordant as head of the medical side is told at Intake
  Medicals, before this scene. **Open:** Episode Two's three sets were written before Episode Two is rebuilt, and want
  another look when it is.
- **R14** Day 1, night, in the plan.
- **R24** Row B, Block 8, as the map has it.
- **R25** D051's rumour rules, all of them.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors: Hana confirms a true rumour at friendly and closes the notebook at hostile; Mrs. Ueda joins block-wardens. Upgrades: A page of their own and The good tea. Setbacks: Lights-out means out (temporary) and Careful whom you tell (permanent). No danger event. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has rolled blind for rumours, heard their three, and had a chance to add a theory of their own to the block's collection.

**How to play it.** A haunt, and a game of rumours. Each player names any skill they can justify for getting people talking, and you roll it for them in secret. Press the degree in the Rumours block and read them their three, in the voice of whoever tells them. A critical failure is three lies; a failure, two lies and one truth; a success, two truths and one lie; a critical success, three truths. Only you know which is which. Draw events between rounds. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The common room of Row B, Block 8, after lights-out.  
**First thing.** A dozen people in dressing gowns going silent at once, then relaxing when they see the group aren't the warden.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Hana's notebook has a page for every intake going back three years, each with its rumours and a column headed 'came true'. Most of that column is empty.

**1. Read the room, and let Hana ask for their news.**

**2. Each player names a skill they can justify for getting the room talking, and says how their character does it. Roll it for them in secret at the level-based DC (15 at level 1), press the degree in the Rumours block, and read them their three.**

**3. Draw from the deck between rounds: one or two events, or as many as the table is enjoying.**

**4. The kettle runs dry and the lamp goes out. Apply the rest.**

**Ends when.** The kettle runs dry and the lamp goes out, or the warden sends everyone to bed.
**Into the next scene.** Blankets are taken down, the lamp goes out, and the block is dark and snoring within five minutes. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Each player's three rumours, and which were true.
- Any rumour the group started, so it can come back changed.
- Anything Hana wrote down about them.

### Scene 8: The Roof *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

Built on the approved idea in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md` ("The night's rest", number 8): half HP, Perception to learn
the city by its lights, +1 status bonus to Will saves on a success, and a deck that is "who else is up there". All
four promises are kept. Shape, length and voice follow `canon/scenes/rest--crew-mess.json`.

#### What the re-read changed

- **Clipped lines rewritten as full sentences.** Faruq's answers ("Katapesh. Hot. Loud. Far.", "Yes. Slowly.", "It
  ends there."), Sayo's "Up the coast. Three houses and a boat.", Isamu's "Four years. Thirty on the tugs…", and
  several `willDiscuss` notes and outcomes that began with a fragment. Faruq is still a man of few words; he now says
  them in whole sentences.
- **The read-aloud's stargazer is "a candidate in a borrowed greatcoat"**, not a girl: the table hasn't been told the
  candidates' ages, and it lets Sayo introduce herself.
- **The rest's critical failure was a flat "nothing"**; it now escalates (something drops into the yard and Isamu has
  to decide whether he saw it).
- **The paper lantern's slip** is the one physical risk on the roof, and it escalates into the warden rather than into
  damage, so the rest stays a rest and nothing on the roof is violent.
- **The padlock dilemma's rolls** apply only to a third way (keeping the second key); the two plain answers are a
  choice with attitude buttons, as in the Crew Mess's petition.

#### The questions

**Does every part earn its place?**

- **R1, earns its place.** Each beat gives the GM one job: set the roof; give each player a turn and the rest's roll
  (with what a success can pick out, so the GM has the districts to hand); draw; apply the rest. Every event gives a
  player something to roll, decide, say or laugh at. `holdInReserve` (Isamu's initials by the tank) is there for the
  player who pokes about, and pays off his `willNotDiscuss`.
- **R2, worse without it.** The district list in the second beat is the one long part; without it a success on the
  rest's roll has nothing to tell the player. Each event carries a different idea (stars, direction, the warden, a
  slip, a Frame, a wish, a day, home, a rule, the harbour), and none repeats another haunt's.
- **R16, directions.** Only three, all practical: "Give them a moment to picture it", "Take a moment first" in the
  prompts (Ingram's own ask, D051), and the note to go straight to the warden's torch on the lantern's critical
  failure.
- **R17, negatives.** None aimed at the GM. The "nevers" in the text are description ("the factories never go out")
  or the Runner's deck rule, copied from the model.
- **R18, prepared for what can't happen.** No. Isamu's questions are asked at the foot of the ladder or when he's
  caught someone, both of which the deck makes possible.

**Is it clean?**

- **R3, clean.** No decision numbers, dates, file paths, tools or other scenes in anything the GM reads. The padlock's
  note says "This comes back as the city keeps changing", which is an instruction about the haunt itself.

**Does it give away too much?**

- **R12, early reveals.** Nothing about the founders, the rift, why the Directorate came to Kurose, any ship, the
  screening or the characters' nature. The informant's tell (tugs moved and kitchens lit early the night before a
  Frame goes out) is ordinary and new, and its note says so. The lantern's wish ("Please let my big sister be sent for
  this year") rests on `directorate.sends-for-trainees`, told at the start.
- **R19, answers or questions.** The scene's answers are all small and ordinary: which light is which district, what
  the Frame is doing tonight, when the ferry runs. The bigger ones are held behind checks (Sayo's hidden star,
  Faruq's tune, the inspection date) or never said (Isamu's initials).
- **R20, meaning.** No line says what the roof, the padlock or the lantern means. The padlock's note to the GM says
  either answer is fair; it tells the players nothing.

**Do the players roll?**

- **R4, rolls.** Every character rolls Perception for the rest. Five events roll: Nature or Lore (stars), Stealth
  (Hide, the torch), Athletics or Acrobatics (the lantern), Perception or Piloting (the Frame), and Thievery or
  Diplomacy (the padlock's third way). The notes give rolls for lying, stealing and jumping roofs. The roleplay events
  and the buff have no roll by design; the informant is freely given.
- **R5, dilemma through rolls.** The padlock is first a choice, because its cost is the point; its third way resolves
  through Thievery or Diplomacy with all four degrees.
- **R6, all four degrees.** Every roll has four real outcomes. Critical failures escalate: the crane lamp becomes a
  standing joke, the flask brings Isamu up with his book, the slide booms the tank and starts the torch, the shout
  closes Faruq's harmonica for the night, the dropped cap lands in the yard, the caught key costs Isamu's goodwill.

**Who's in it?**

- **R7, free, on a check, never.** All three regulars have `willDiscuss`, `coercible` (Diplomacy at their Will DC,
  friendly or better) and `willNotDiscuss` with a deflecting line: Sayo's fear of heights ("I like the middle"),
  Faruq's lost tune ("That's where it ends."), Isamu's roof and his initials ("I don't know anything about any roof.").
- **R8, likely questions.** Six for Sayo, six for Faruq, five for Isamu, each in their own voice.
- **R9, stat blocks.** Missing. Each has a proposed level and archetype (Sayo and Faruq level 1 candidates, Isamu
  level 2 working adult, all non-combat); Perception, Will and a Deception DC wait for the NPC generator. The events
  already call on Isamu's Perception and Will DCs and Sayo's and Faruq's Will DCs, with the level-based DC as the
  stand-in.
- **R10, attack, lie, steal, sneak, follow, split, walk out.** In `notes`: noise, lying to Isamu, taking the chart,
  jumping to the next roof, picking a fight (nobody fights; the regulars leave), splitting, and going down early.
  Sneaking is the warden's torch itself. Following someone doesn't arise: nobody leaves the roof but the group.

**Is it well said?**

- **R15, read aloud.** Read through as speech: one breath per sentence, the view before the people, and it ends on
  the harmonica so the GM can hand straight to Sayo.
- **R21, fragments.** Fixed throughout (above). The remaining short lines are spoken dialogue in character, in full
  sentences.
- **R22, coined words.** "Frame" is glossed where it is heard ("one of the great walking machines"). "Candidate" is
  the table's own word by now. Katapesh is a real Golarion nation, placed in Faruq's role as far across the sea.
  The constellation names are explained in the event that uses them.
- **R23, length.** A haunt runs ten to twenty minutes and most of it lives in the deck, so the beats are short; the
  one long beat carries the city's lights, which is what this rest is for.

**Does it hold up?**

- **R11, the retold thing.** The candidate who pointed confidently at a crane lamp and got it named on the chart as
  the Candidate's Mistake, with a tune to go with it. Or the water tank booming like a gong.
- **R13, facts agree with canon.** Checked against `canon/world/kurose.md` (old town on the waterfront, factories
  along the shore, river and trams inland, hills behind, electric light, trams and factories working through the
  night), `canon/world/frames.md` (walking machines with a pilot inside, clumsy, run by the Directorate) and
  `canon/world/directorate.md` (the island base with hangars and workshops, sending for trainees). Like the Crew Mess,
  the scene cites no facts in `facts[]`; its new ordinary details (the regulars, the ladder, the tugs-and-kitchens
  tell, the constellation names) are Claude's and would become `provisional` facts if Ingram keeps them.
- **R14, story time.** Day 1, night, after Flood Tunnel Clearance, as the plan has it. The scene says "tonight" and
  "tomorrow" only, which agree.
- **R24, places.** The block is on the island near where the causeway comes ashore, so the city is across the
  harbour, as the read-aloud has it. The factories, old town, river and hills agree with canon. **To confirm:** the
  Upper Wards, the Container Ground, the campus on the cliff top, the hangars' berths and the dry dock are taken from
  the map's older entries and the Crew Mess, not from canon; the campus in particular is named only by the block's own
  map note, and whose campus it is is not said.
- **R25, all of Ingram's detail.** The approved idea (half HP, Perception, the city by its lights, +1 status to Will,
  who else is up there) and the task's list are all here: the stargazer with a homemade chart, the harmonica player,
  the warden who pretends not to know, Golarion's constellations with invented names, the districts by their lights,
  the shooting star and a wish, the torch on the ladder (Stealth), the paper lantern, a Frame moved at night, a
  dilemma, the harbour informant, and a prompt about the character's day and home. D051's asks are met: rolls with
  results, a buff, chances to speak in character with time to think, a dilemma that mirrors the city changing (the
  office's new lock), and a chance-met informant with a glimpse behind the scenes.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for Sayo, Faruq and Isamu (the NPC generator).
- Ingram's confirmation of the district names and the cliff-top campus (R24).
- Whether a locked ladder should change `haunt.everyVisit` on later visits, or stay in the padlock's note as it is now.
- The buff "North by the Needle" (+1 circumstance to Survival to Sense Direction) is a second small buff on top of the
  rest's; Ingram may prefer this haunt to own only the Will bonus.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors: Isamu (block-wardens) turns a failed hide into a success at friendly and ends the night at eleven at unfriendly; Faruq stops warning them at unfriendly. Upgrades: The second key and The chart finished. Setbacks: The ladder locked (permanent, the padlock) and The floodlight (temporary). No danger event. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

**Corrected after the D052 retrofit (2026-09-29):** Isamu Hamada's cast id was "genzo", colliding the same way. Renamed to "isamu".

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent a clear night on the roof, in character, and leaves with half their HP back, perhaps a steadier head for tomorrow, and a standing with the roof's regulars that the next clear night will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the roof, give each player a turn to say where their character settles and to read the city by its lights, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, a buff and chances to talk in character. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The roof of Row E, Block 9, near where the causeway comes onto the island, late on a clear night.  
**First thing.** The stars, and then the city: the whole of Kurose lit up across the black water.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Scratched into the lead flashing by the water tank, very small and very old, are two sets of initials inside a heart. Isamu carved them when the block was new. If anyone asks him about them, he says he has no idea what anybody is talking about, and goes red to the ears.

**1. Read the roof, then let Sayo tell them to put their torches out and pat the blanket.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "Where on the roof does your character settle, and what did they bring up with them?" Give them a moment to picture it. Then each looks out over the city and rolls Perception for the rest. What a success can pick out: the old town on the waterfront, low and yellow, with oil lamps and paper lanterns in its doorways; the factories along the shore, a row of orange furnace glow and sparks that never goes out; the river running inland, with a thread of street lamps beside the tram line and a blue-white spark each time a tram crosses the points; the newcomers' streets, straight rows of hard white electric light, with the roofs of a dozen different temples lit up among them; the Upper Wards stacked on the hillside to the north, like a staircase of lights; the campus on the cliff top, where the windows go out one by one; the riding lights of small boats crossing the harbour; and, behind the roof, the Container Ground on the island itself, where the cranes are lit all night.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. The cold gets into everyone at last, and Isamu's keys rattle on the stairs below for the last time tonight. Apply the rest, and the Will bonus for anyone who read the city.**

**Ends when.** The cold wins, or Isamu's keys rattle on the stairs for the last time tonight, and the group climb down.
**Into the next scene.** The ladder is colder going down than it was coming up, and the third rung wobbles again, just to be sure you remember it. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Who read the city by its lights, and has the +1 to Will saves until their next rest.
- Anything of theirs that went over the edge into the yard.
- Whether the padlock went on, and who has the second key.
- Whose name went in Isamu's book, and whose name went on Sayo's chart.

### Scene 9: The Night Observer *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

Built on the approved idea in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md` ("The night's rest", number 9): a quarter of maximum HP,
Nature or Survival to learn tomorrow's weather and tides, a +1 circumstance bonus to one weather- or sea-affected check
tomorrow on a critical success, and room for two. All four are kept as written. The haunt's shape follows
`canon/scenes/rest--crew-mess.json`, and D050 and D051.

**Does every part earn its place?**

- **R1** Every part gives the GM something to run and a player something to do. The roof and settling-in beats set
  the place and give each visitor a turn to speak; the deck is ten events (six rolls, one role-play, one informant,
  one dilemma, one buff), each with a prompt; the four o'clock beat is where the rest's roll happens, in character.
  `holdInReserve` (Genzō's margin poems) is only for a player who reads over his shoulder, and gives the GM his soft
  spot to play.
- **R2** Cutting any instrument roll would leave the deck thinner but not broken; they stay because the task asked for
  each instrument, and each fails differently (a jumpy needle, a frozen climber, a lost lantern, a lost frog, an
  invented cloud). The squall is the one group event and the only time pressure in the scene.
- **R16** Directions kept: "Give them a moment to picture it" and "Take a moment" on prompts (Ingram's own ask in
  D051, time to think before speaking), "Decide tomorrow's weather and tides before the roll" (critical: the roll is
  meaningless otherwise), and the informant's note on answering tomorrow's order sheet in readings (critical: it stops
  it becoming a spoiler).
- **R17** No negative directions to the GM. The "don't" lines are the regulars' own speech.
- **R18** Nothing prepared that the shape rules out. The one risk was a third visitor on a roof with room for two; the
  notes say what happens.

**Is it clean?**

- **R3** Nothing mentions how it was made: no decision numbers, dates, tools or other scenes in the GM text. The
  informant's "on the night of the flood tunnel duty" names an event in the fiction, not another scene file.

**Does it give away too much?**

- **R12** Nothing beyond what the table already has: the island, Frames, the flood tunnel duty they just did, and
  things ordinary and new about this building. No founders, rift, starship, Watcher, Cicada, screening or blood. The
  logbooks "going back further than anyone has read" agree with the Directorate settling in Kurose about a hundred and
  fifty years ago (`directorate.came-to-kurose`, told at the start) without saying so.
- **R19** The informant is the one place that could over-answer. Genzō gives a rule of thumb (readings tell you what's
  going out), never a reason, and tomorrow's sheet is answered in readings for the table to interpret.
- **R20** Nothing tells the players what the drum means for the city. The dilemma is put as a choice with a cost each
  way; the note says either answer is fair.

**Do the players roll?**

- **R4** Yes: every instrument, the squall, the drum (Crafting to find the fault) and the rest itself. The role-play
  events (the Remarks line, the bearing) are deliberately rolls-free: they are the time to talk.
- **R5** Yes, partly: the Crafting check decides what they know when they choose (bent pen arm, a fixable fault, or
  nothing), and the choice itself is theirs.
- **R6** Yes. Every roll has four outcomes; each failure moves the night on (Nadi steps in, the reading is corrected,
  the board is chalked again) and each critical failure escalates (an attitude drops, the watch's record is spoiled, a
  wrong forecast carried into tomorrow).

**Who's in it?**

- **R7** Both regulars have `willDiscuss`, `willNotDiscuss` with a deflecting line, and a `coercible` entry with the
  skill, DC and result: Genzō's retirement date (Diplomacy at his Will DC, friendly or better); Nadi's day-clerk post
  (Diplomacy at her Will DC, friendly or better, out of Genzō's hearing).
- **R8** Six questions for Genzō and five for Nadi, answered in their own voices.
- **R9** Not yet. Both have a proposed level and archetype (Genzō level 1, Nadi level 0, non-combat); the stat blocks,
  with Perception, Will and Deception DCs, wait for the NPC generator. The notes use their Perception and Will DCs.
- **R10** In the notes: a third visitor, breaking an instrument, attacking, lying (a false reading goes on the board),
  stealing from the log, searching the desk, splitting (roof and cellar), and leaving early (the rest still covers
  them, but only those there at four roll for tomorrow).

**Is it well said?**

- **R15** The read-aloud was read out loud: one room-sized paragraph, instruments named plainly, ending on Genzō's
  line.
- **R21** Clipped outcomes rewritten in full sentences ("Up, oiled and spinning" became "They are up, the bearing is
  oiled and the Sparrow is spinning inside a minute"; "Frog out, rain measured" and "Wrong." likewise). The log entry
  "Rain: some. Frog: one." stays: it's Genzō's own clipped handwriting, and the joke.
- **R22** Coined or unfamiliar words explained on first hearing: kame (turtle), making and ebbing (rising and
  falling), the slatted box (where the thermometers live), the recording drum (a clockwork drum that draws the
  pressure by itself). Frame is used only in GM text and in Genzō's line, where the table already knows it.
- **R23** About the Crew Mess's length, which suits a 10 to 20 minute rest for one or two players.

**Does it hold up?**

- **R11** The frog, which may end the night on a character's shoulder and named after them; or whatever they chalk
  under Remarks for the whole island to read at breakfast.
- **R13** The scene cites no facts (`facts` and `gmFacts` are empty, as in the Crew Mess). Everything it states is
  either told at the start (the island, Frames, the flood tunnel duty) or new and ordinary about this building and its
  two people, owned by this scene. If Ingram wants the Met Office, Genzō and Nadi in `canon/world/`, those are the
  facts to add.
- **R14** Day 1, night, in the plan. "Tonight" and "tomorrow" agree with it; the informant's flood tunnel line is
  marked for the night of that duty, so the event still works when the rest comes back in Episode Two.
- **R24** The Met Office is in the middle of the island, which agrees with its map entry; the harbour and the lights of
  Kurose on the far shore are what the roof would see over the fence. The tide gauge's pipe from the harbour and the
  harbour lighthouse are new details, not yet on the map.
- **R25** Everything the idea promised is here, and every thread in the task: each instrument as a roll, the hourly
  board, the squall, the decades of logs and the observer reading operations from the readings they ask for, the drum
  dilemma as a mirror of the changing city, and role-play prompts.

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for Genzō and Nadi (the NPC generator).
- The Met Office's location is not in `canon/` yet, and neither are the lighthouse or the tide pipe.
- The `data/` copy is not built; `node tools/story/story.mjs build` makes it.
- Tomorrow's weather and tides are not decided anywhere yet. Episode Two has a low-tide scene at midday on Day 2,
  which a critical success here could touch; the GM needs the day's tides settled before this rest is run.

**Edited after the re-read (2026-09-29, the prose tests):** the read-aloud is split into three units a GM can cut
live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** Each regular has doors: Genzo helps the four o'clock forecast at friendly and turns them away at the stairhead at hostile; Nadi reports them at hostile. No faction here. Upgrade: Assistant observers. Setbacks: The drum passes (permanent, from the new boy) and Storm watch (temporary). No danger event. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent a night on the roof in character, reading instruments with an old man who loves them, and leaves with a quarter of their HP back, a forecast for tomorrow that may or may not be right, and a standing with the two people who keep the watch.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. There is room on the roof for two visitors, so no more than two players can choose it. Set the roof, give each player a turn to say how their character settles in, then draw events from the deck, two or three per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls at the instruments and chances to talk in character. At the four o'clock reading, each visitor rolls for tomorrow's weather. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The roof of the Met Office, in the middle of the island, after midnight.  
**First thing.** An old man in an oilskin coat, talking quietly and fondly to a barometer.  
**If a question isn't covered.** In the margin of every night's page of the log, in small careful writing, Genzō has written a three-line poem about that night's weather. He has done it for twenty years and has never shown anybody. A character who reads over his shoulder sees tonight's, half finished. If they ask, he closes the book and says, "Just the weather."

**1. Read the roof, then let Genzō introduce his instruments by name.**

**2. Ask each visitor, one at a time: "Genzō gives you the spare stool and a blanket that smells of the sea. What has your character brought up here with them, and how do they pass the time between the readings?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: two or three events for each visitor, or as many as the table is enjoying. Genzō calls each hour as it comes. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. At the four o'clock reading, Genzō turns to each visitor and asks, "Well? What's tomorrow going to do?" Each rolls for the rest, and says their forecast out loud as their character. Apply the rest.**

**Ends when.** The four o'clock reading is chalked up and Genzō sends the visitors to bed, or they leave.
**Into the next scene.** The stairs down are dark and smell of wet coats, and behind them Genzō is telling Grandmother all about the visitors. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Each visitor's forecast for tomorrow, and whether their roll was a critical failure.
- Who has the +1 circumstance bonus for tomorrow, and who has the bonus to Sense Direction.
- What they wrote under Remarks on the board.
- What they told Genzō to write about the drum.
- Anything they put on the cloud chart.

### Scene 10: The Night Shift *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against canon/reread.md version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

A haunt, built on the approved idea for rest 10 in `canon/plan/chapter-01.md` and on the Crew Mess as the model. What
the plan promised is all kept: a quarter of maximum HP, Earn Income with Crafting on SF2E's own table, the night crew
explaining what they're taking apart, a critical failure leaving the character Fatigued until their next rest, and room
for three.

#### For you, while reading

These are mine, not yet yours, and none is cited as a fact yet:

- **The regulars**: Kaede Ishimori (night foreman, works nights to be home for her daughter, keeps a notebook drawing
  every joint she has rebuilt), Joji Mabuchi (the old pit hand, a jar of every broken bolt, due to retire and not
  wanting to), Nkiru Oduya (the gantry-crane driver from the Mwangi, who sings the lifts).
- **Seven**: a Frame in the back bay that hasn't walked in years and is stripped for parts to keep the others walking,
  while the office book says it is in for a refit. It is the informant event, and it touches nothing held back.
- **The tram depot opening next week**, where a Frame will walk in front of half the city, and the office has given its
  paint more technician-days than the knee. It drives the sheet-on-the-door dilemma. Nothing founder-related is said
  about it.
- **The office asking twice how the shop would manage without a night shift** (Kaede's coercible line).
- **The Earn Income rules.** The mirror holds GM Core's rule for setting the task level and its DC, and Player Core's
  note that Crafting is one of the most reliable skills for it, but not the Earn Income activity itself or its income
  table. The scene cites the rule and the table by name and gives no figures; the paid-nothing result on a critical
  failure is the rule's own, from the Player Core text, to confirm against the book.

#### The questions

- **R1, earns its place.** Each regular runs one part of the shop (the clipboard, the pit, the crane), so the three jobs
  offered in the second beat each have a person attached. Each event gives a player something to roll, decide, say or
  keep. `holdInReserve` (the tally on the pit wall) is a laugh and pays off on the pit's critical failure.
- **R2, worse without it.** The two dilemmas were checked against each other: the shortcut is the old hands' knowledge
  against the manual; the sheet is what the city sees against what keeps the machines working. Different costs,
  different people pulling each way (Nkiru tempts in one, opposes in the other).
- **R16, directions.** Three kept: "give them a moment to picture it" (Ingram's own ask), the break prompt's "take a
  moment", and the note that the pit damage comes off before the rest is applied, which the GM needs for the order of
  presses.
- **R17, negatives.** None in anything read aloud. Kaede's "your feet out of the pit unless Joji says so" is her
  character talking. The tool's note was rewritten from two negatives into "the bonus keeps until they use it".
- **R18, prepared for what can't happen.** Nothing. There is no speech; every question the regulars answer is one a
  player can ask while working.
- **R3, clean.** No decision numbers, dates, tools, files or other scenes in anything the GM reads. The walk-out note
  sends characters to their own bunks rather than naming the other rest.
- **R12, early reveals.** Checked against the held-back list: no founder is named or mentioned, nothing about why the

  bronze bearings, pins, cable pulleys, paraffin crack tests. The tram depot opening deliberately has no ribbon-cutter.
  Seven raises "why does the office keep it on the books?" and Joji says he doesn't know.
- **R19, answers or questions.** Seven is an answer given freely (the crews know the machines' real state), which is

- **R4, rolls.** Every job a character does has a check: Crafting at the bench, Athletics in the pit, Perception on the

- **R5, dilemmas through rolls.** Yes: the shortcut resolves on Athletics or Crafting, the sheet on Society. Refusing

- **R6, all four degrees.** Every roll has all four. Each failure moves the night on (Joji finishes it, the crew find it

- **R7, what they say.** Each regular has `willDiscuss`, `willNotDiscuss` with the deflecting line ("Somebody has to.",

- **R21, clipped lines.** None in the read-aloud or the prompts. Short lines survive only in NPC speech, where people do

- **R22, coined words.** Frame is explained in the read-aloud ("the kind of walking machine that is taller than the

- **R23, length.** Matched to the Crew Mess: three regulars, four beats, ten events. It runs 10 to 20 minutes.

- **R14, time.** Day 1, night, as the plan has it: they arrive about midnight straight from the flood tunnel, break at

- **R24, places.** No. 1 Machine Shop is on the base (the garrison region), with gantries, the pit and the Monday sheet

- **R25, all her detail.** Everything in the plan's line and the location's note is used: the gantries, the pit deep

  explaining what they take apart, Fatigued on a critical failure, room for three. From D050 and D051: quarter HP, rolls
  on all four degrees, a role-play prompt about the character's day with time to think, a dilemma that mirrors the

#### Still missing

- Stat blocks for Kaede, Joji and Nkiru (Perception, Will, Deception DCs), waiting for the NPC generator.

live, and the night's deck beat always runs (it is the menu). Wording only otherwise: no meaning changed.

**Added after the re-read (2026-09-29, attitude as a promise, upgrades and setbacks):** All three regulars join repair-crews. Kaede sets the Earn Income task level by attitude (level 1 at friendly, level 0 with no pay on a failure at unfriendly, no income at hostile); Joji closes the pit at hostile. Upgrades: A bench of their own and The shortcut written down. Setbacks: Short on the sheet (temporary, from the sheet on the door) and No night shift (permanent). No danger event. Nothing already in the scene was changed. Waiting for Ingram's read with the rest.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has worked a night beside the machine-shop crew, in character, and leaves at dawn with a quarter of their HP back, a night's pay (or a Fatigued morning), and a standing with the night crew that the next visit will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the shop, give each player a turn to say what their character does with their hands tonight, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, buffs and chances to talk in character. At dawn, each character who worked rolls to Earn Income. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. There is work for three: a fourth character is sent to bed with thanks. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** No. 1 Machine Shop, on the base, from about midnight until dawn.  
**First thing.** The noise: a chain running through a gantry block high overhead, and somebody singing to it.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Chalked on the pit wall is a tally of nights since anything fell into the pit. It has been rubbed out and started again twice this year. Joji won't say who, and Nkiru, asked from the crane, laughs so hard she has to stop the lift.

**1. Read the shop, then let Kaede hand out overalls and Nkiru shout down from the crane.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "Kaede has three jobs: the bench, the pit and the crane. Which one does your character take, and how do they go about it?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. The day shift comes in at dawn, and the knee is in or it isn't. Each character who worked rolls Crafting to Earn Income. Apply the rest and the pay.**

**Ends when.** The day shift comes in at dawn and reads the sheet on the door, or the group leave.
**Into the next scene.** Outside, the sky over the harbour is going grey, and the first train of the morning is running across the causeway. Morning.

**Write down:**

- What each character earned, and who is Fatigued.
- Whether the knee went in by dawn.
- Whether anyone took Nkiru's shortcut, and whether anyone changed the sheet on the door.
- Who was given the tool, and what they scratched into it.

### Scene 11: The Paper Lantern *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*



#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who came here has spent an evening off base, in character, and leaves with a quarter of their HP back, a hangover risk, and a standing with the regulars that the next visit will remember.

**How to play it.** A haunt: a menu, not a script. Set the room, give each player a turn to say how their character spends the evening, then draw events from the deck, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. Most are rolls, buffs and chances to talk in character. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and how each of them behaves now is shown beside their name. *Runs about 10-20 minutes.*

**Where.** The Paper Lantern, an izakaya off Directorate ground, late at night.  
**First thing.** The noise and the smell of grilled skewers, after the quiet of the checkpoint outside.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Kiku keeps a spare stool behind the counter itself, for whoever she decides needs a quiet corner rather than the room. Nobody is told why some people get it.

**1. Read the room, then let Kiku size the group up over the counter.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "What does your character order, and where in the room do they end up sitting?" Give them a moment to picture it before they answer.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

**4. Kiku calls last orders and the room starts to clear. Apply the rest. Tomorrow morning, before duty, each character who drank rolls the Fortitude save.**

**Ends when.** Kiku calls last orders and the room clears, or the group leave.
**Into the next scene.** The walk back to the checkpoint is quiet after the noise of the room, lantern light fading behind them. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Whose name is remembered at the counter, and how.
- Whether the walk back after curfew went cleanly.
- Who failed the Fortitude save in the morning.

### Scene 1: The Magic Show *(Written in detail · waiting for your read)*

version 1 · re-read against `canon/reread.md` version 2 · 2026-09-29 · by Claude · state: awaiting Ingram's read

The first discovery (`D052`, settled points 6 and 8): what a player finds on the first night if they choose somewhere
new instead of a rest they know. Written as a haunt that plays once (`haunt.discovery`, `haunt.oneTime`), in the shape
of the Crew Mess. Ingram's brief, word for word: "the chance to go to a 'Magic Show' which is the very low key magical
illusion 'like magical theatre / IMAX' show which will be good and fun and carry some fun buffs, you can offer that as
the unknown location on the first night."

The show also follows what Ingram said about magic shows in `D002`: showy magic is frowned upon except in an emergency,
so people go to private back-street shows where illusionists cast spells around them, "the equivalent of a 3D immersive
film", a guilty pleasure most people in the city have been to at least once. The blankets over the windows, the
knock at the side door and "it isn't secret, it's private" all come from that.

**Does every part earn its place?**
- **R1** Every event gives a player something to say, roll, decide or keep. Each regular has a want, a line per
  attitude and two to four doors. The book of names and the ticket halves exist so that each spectator leaves with
  something that makes a later invitation possible (`D052` point 9); they are innocent, and nothing says who sends the
  next one.
- **R2** Cutting any event thins the deck without breaking it. The finale beat is the one part the scene can't lose:
  it is the gamble that makes a discovery riskier and stronger than an ordinary rest.
- **R16** Two directions survive: how many events to draw, and who volunteers if nobody does (the finale's
  `direction`). Both answer something a GM would otherwise stop to decide.
- **R17** One negative, in Daisuke's line "Don't touch it", which is dialogue, not a direction.
- **R18** No preparation for anything the shape rules out.

**Is it clean?**
- **R3** Nothing mentions how the scene was made, the later return of the show, or what any other scene does. The
  notes say what the show is and what the book is for, in the present tense.

**Does it give away too much?**
- **R12** Nothing about the later show, and none of the held-back names or topics, appears anywhere. The one glimpse
  behind the scenes (`the-watchman`) is that Directorate officers come to these shows in civilian coats; the officer is
  nobody the group has met and is gone if they look. **For Ingram:** the scene tells the table the `D002` facts about
  magic shows (frowned upon except in an emergency, private, a guilty pleasure, most people have been to one), and
  there is no canon world fact for them yet, so no beat can cite them. They want a world file of their own, from `D002`,
  told `start` or `in:discovery--the-magic-show`. The recruitment shows in `D002` (the "leave or stay" choice) are
  deliberately absent: nothing here asks the audience to stay behind.
- **R19** Tamsin's age and her husband, Daisuke's family and Mrs Handa's wish are all held as questions, each with a
  deflecting line; a Diplomacy check opens two of them.
- **R20** Nothing tells the players what the glamour dilemma means, or what the ticket half is for beyond Tamsin's own
  words.

**Do the players roll?**
- **R4, R5, R6** Four events roll (`the-cloud`, `how-is-it-done`, `the-dragon`, `backstage`), and the finale's volunteer
  rolls the gamble; each has all four degrees. A critical success gives something extra (the volunteer slot, a wink,
  the dragon's bow, the lantern up close); a failure moves the night on; a critical failure escalates (the hall goes
  dark, the front row goes over, the second act starts late, the volunteer is Stupefied 1). The dilemma
  (`the-glamour-song`) resolves by choice, on purpose: each answer costs a step with somebody.

**Who's in it?**
- **R7, R8** Tamsin, Daisuke and Mrs Handa each have a conversation map: what they give freely, what they won't say
  and why, what leaning on them gets, and five or six likely questions answered in their voice. Kenta, Mr Ikeda, the
  man without his glamour and the officer are walk-ons inside single events.
- **R9** **Missing:** stat blocks. Each regular has a proposed level and archetype, waiting for the NPC generator.
- **R10** In `notes`: casting a spell during the show, reading or taking the book, heckling, leaving early,
  splitting up. Walking out is covered by Daisuke's hostile door and the notes.

**Is it well said?**
- **R15** The read-aloud was read aloud: three units, sound first (music behind blankets), then the alley and the
  knock, then Daisuke at the door. The two beat read-alouds (the lights going out, and the finale) are three sentences
  or fewer.
- **R21** No clipped fragments.
- **R22** "Glamour" is known from the start. "Private show" is explained by Daisuke at the door, and why it is private
  by Tamsin and Mrs Handa when asked. "The lantern" is shown before it is named.
- **R23** About 5,000 words, most of it optional deck and conversation maps; the beats are five short lines.

**Does it hold up?**
- **R11** The whale looking at you; the cloud raining on one character; the dragon bowing, or the whole front row
  going over; Tamsin letting her stage self go out like a candle and turning out to be a gnome three feet tall.
- **R13** Facts agree with canon and are cited on the show beat: glamours and the argument about them
  (`glamour.debate`), Kurose as a fishing village with one road in (`kurose.fishing-village`, told by Tamsin), magic
  small and everyday (`era.engines`). The `D002` facts are the gap named under R12.
- **R14** Day 1, night, in the plan; `story time` finds "tonight", which agrees.
- **R24** **For Ingram: the siting is her call.** Proposed: the Market Hall (`the-market-hall-456`), a covered produce
  market in Island Centre, "loud in the morning and empty by early afternoon", so empty and shuttered at night, a short
  walk from the other rests on the island. Nothing on the map is a theatre; the nearest alternative is the Civic Hall
  (`the-civic-hall-451`), also in Island Centre, but it is an administration building, where a private show would sit
  oddly.
- **R25** `D052` read in full. Low-key illusion, like theatre you sit inside: the sea, the whale, the lantern city, the
  dragon. Good and fun: no danger or plot event. Fun buffs: +1 status to Perception for everyone, temporary HP for a
  wish. Riskier and more powerful: the volunteer can win twice their level in temporary HP and +1 to all saves, or come
  away Stupefied 1 until their next rest. The unknown place on the first night: the players choose it blind and find it
  by its music. Attitude as a promise: every regular has doors, and both company members share the faction
  `the-oddwater-company`. `D050` and `D051`: a quarter HP, the gamble-or-hospital choice, a dilemma that mirrors the
  city (the glamour song), an informant, and roles for the players to speak in character.

**Choices worth Ingram's eye**
- **The buffs.** Perception is the one bonus no ordinary rest gives. The volunteer's win beats every ordinary rest
  (the bathhouse gives temporary HP equal to level; this gives twice that, and a critical success adds +1 to all saves),
  and its loss (Stupefied 1 for a whole day) is worse than any. A Hero Point was left out on purpose: Hero Points are
  lost at the end of a session (Player Core p. 405), and a night's rest may well end one.
- **No upgrades or setbacks.** The show plays once, so there is nothing to upgrade or wear down.
- **The faction.** Tamsin starts friendly and Daisuke indifferent. If a faction's attitude starts anywhere below
  friendly, Tamsin will play at the worse of the two (the schema's rule), so the company's starting attitude wants
  setting to friendly, or Tamsin's to match Daisuke's.
- **The ticket half could be a Papers handout** (`personal: true`, with the character's name in Tamsin's hand), so the
  invitation is something the players physically keep. Not added: it needs the handout pipeline's own checks.
- **The glamour dilemma's man** has grey-green scales and gold eyes, and no ancestry is named.

#### The scene, as the GM runs it

**What it's for.** Each player who chose somewhere new has watched the show in character, taken part in at least one moment of it, and leaves with a quarter of their HP back, the show's bonus or the volunteer's result, and half a ticket with their name in the performer's book.

**How to play it.** A discovery, played once: a menu, not a script. The players chose it without knowing what it was, so let them find it. Set the door, give each player a turn to say where their character sits, then draw events from the deck while the show runs, one or two per player or as many as the table is enjoying. The finale is the gamble: one volunteer walks into the picture. The regulars' attitudes move with what happens, and each of them opens or closes something for the group as they do. *Runs about 15-25 minutes.*

**Where.** Somewhere new: a player chose it on The City without knowing what it was. Walking the island at night, they find a private magic show in the Market Hall, after hours.  
**First thing.** Music from a building that ought to be shut, muffled by blankets over the windows.  
**If a question isn't covered.** Tamsin's book of names has a page for every town the company has played, going back more than a hundred years, and beside each name is the seat its owner sat in. Kurose's first page is from when it was a fishing village with one road in.

**1. Read the door. Daisuke takes whatever is in their pockets as the ticket and lets them in; Mrs Handa is already in the front row.**

**2. Ask each player, one at a time: "Where in the hall does your character sit, and who do they end up next to?" Give them a moment. The front row is Mrs Handa's, by the stove; the bench behind the iron pillar is the one nobody wants.**

**3. Draw from the deck: one or two events for each player, or as many as the table is enjoying. The show is two acts, the sea and then the lantern city, with an interval between; play each event where it fits. Played events never come back; put back any the table never saw.**

> The lights go out all at once. In the dark, a woman's voice, much bigger than any woman, says, "Good evening, Kurose. Tonight, the sea is coming in." And then it does: cold blue light pours down the walls and fills the hall to the roof, and fish the size of your hand go flickering between the benches.

*Facts:* tells the table `glamour.debate`, `kurose.fishing-village`; GM only `era.engines`.

**4. Take the raised hands, and let Tamsin pick one volunteer, or two if the table is enjoying it; her doors say whom she favours. Ask the volunteer, word for word: "Where would you go tonight, if you could go anywhere at all?" Describe the hall becoming that place around them, then roll the volunteer's check (the rest).**

> The lights go down to a single beam. The tall woman made of light who has been on stage all night lets herself go out like a candle, and the one left standing there is a gnome in a patched coat, a little over three feet tall, with copper hair and ink on her fingers. "I need," says Tamsin Oddwater, "one volunteer."

*Direction:* If nobody puts a hand up, Tamsin picks Mrs Handa, who has volunteered every year for thirty years, and the hall becomes a beach at dawn.

**5. Tamsin stands at the side door and shakes every hand on the way out. She asks each character their name and where to send word, writes both in her book, and tears each of them half a ticket: "Keep your half. When there's a next performance, the other half comes to you, and that's your invitation." Apply the rest.**

**Ends when.** Tamsin has shaken the last hand at the side door.
**Into the next scene.** Outside, the island is dark and quiet again, and the sky over it is the ordinary one. Morning.

**Write down:**

- Who volunteered, where they asked to go, and how their check went.
- Whose names went into Tamsin's book, with where to send word, and who kept half a ticket.
- What the group said, if anything, about the man without his glamour.

*Scene hash 09878539cc98b0cc.*


## §1 What's really going on *(GM-only)*

Twelve facts, in plain words. Everything in the chapters is consistent with them, even when it misleads the party.

1. **Two ships crashed on Golarion.** Tens of thousands of years ago a generation ship, a colony ship carrying a whole people between the stars, crashed on Kortos. The mitflits are descended from its crew. About a thousand years later a second ship, sent to search for the first, crashed in what is now Kurose's ground. *(Canon)* The dig that reaches it is a journey from the city, off the city map (LEDGER #42). *(Canon)*
2. **First Impact, about two hundred years ago (4721 AR).** Four adventurers, Rupiah, Anabala, Shucks and Hyuck (the characters Liam, Pippa, Craig and Steve played in the old campaign), were fetching exotic fuel from the generation ship's cargo deck, for the jungle mitflits' shuttle, when a container breached. The breach tore the ship's navigation and sensor system out of it and broke it into a swarm of nanomachines. That swarm is the Watcher. *(Canon)* The ship's mind, Mother, was left behind, weakened. *(Proposal)* The four were flung to Nirvana. When they came back, the rift had carved across Kortos into Absalom; they closed it at Pharasma's temple with a way Sarenrae sent back with them, and it worked because it was one dropped container. They never knew the Watcher came out of it. *(Canon)*
3. **The Directorate.** It was founded about 195 years ago to guard the broken ship and keep its technology from destabilising the world. Soon after First Impact the gods told Rupiah it would happen again, in Kurose. About 150 years ago the Directorate moved there, built the modern city, and found the search ship's hull underneath while digging. It is Golarion's foremost gatherer of alien technology: it digs to recover what's aboard and lock it in deep storage vaults, where it can't cause a disaster or another First Impact. It knows the ship may carry the same exotic matter the founders dropped at Kortos, and any it finds is the most carefully guarded thing it owns. *(Canon)* The search ship has been calling for its sister ever since it crashed; the call is what drew the Watcher across the world, and the digging makes it louder. Nobody in the Directorate knows the Watcher exists. *(Canon)*
4. **The Watcher.** It is the generation ship's eyes without the ship's mind. It exists to observe, so it keeps growing, to observe more. It hangs over Kurose and reaches down through the rock, around the ship that is calling it. It builds bodies, its children, out of Sap: the generation ship's stock of raw material for its repair nanites. It has no malice, and nothing it does is personal. *(Canon)* Nobody can see it without a particular chemical: on a treated lens, or splashed onto it. *(Canon)* Nobody on Golarion knows it exists: not the Directorate, not the founders, and not the party at the start. The party are the ones who find it. *(Canon)* Once it kept a ship full of people on course. So long without the ship's mind to guide it, it observes, adapts and responds, and escalates without understanding why: it has drifted from helpful to an accidental antagonist. *(Canon)* The mitflits feel it in the air as something godlike and comforting that keeps them going the right way, and don't know it's real. *(Canon)* Dogs, birds and other animals sense its nanoparticles too, perhaps as a high-frequency buzz that upsets them, and go anywhere else (LEDGER #43). *(Canon)*
5. **Manako's accident, about 44 years ago.** Manako, a junior Directorate chemist, was given a shelved sample nobody senior wanted, catalogued as Inert 12. It was Sap. He cracked the capsule and spilled some on a tentacle of the Watcher that was nearby: the Sap fed it and made it visible. His lab assistant, Nobu, saw it too, and wouldn't back him up, because he thought the chemical had made them both see things. Manako was disgraced, with Shucks supervising. *(Canon)* A fragment of it stayed near him and helped him work out how to make more Sap. He became its first supplier, and he thinks it is a god. *(Proposal)* About 40 years ago he proved it to himself, privately: he learned to treat a lens and saw the whole sky full of it; about 38 years ago he set off a canister of the chemical at a conference to prove it, and the room decided it was mass hallucination. *(Canon)*
6. **The Envoy, about 15 years ago.** The Watcher's first attempt to meet people, and its first real appearance in public: before it there were only the lab spill and the conference. Its child, shaped like a disfigured doll, walked into a street to a meeting Manako's small, peaceful circle had arranged. Hori, a police officer alone at a disturbance, shot it in fear, and one of his rounds killed Aoi, Manako's wife. The police kept the truth. The Directorate, which distrusts the police, filed its own line: *a cultist was killed during a confrontation in which someone magically summoned a monster*. The Watcher learned one thing: when it shows itself, it is attacked. *(Canon)*
7. **Cicada.** After Aoi died, Manako's circle became a movement that wants the city to look up and see what is in its sky. It pays for itself by working for the city's crime lords, and for about five years an industrial Sap plant, the column, has been feeding the sky. Most of it is peaceful: the Faithful. A small armed wing, the Cadre, is not. To the Directorate it is a thin file and not much of a problem. The Directorate has never identified its founder, and doesn't connect the cult with the kindly old chemist it once ruined. *(Canon)*
8. **The founders are alive.** Shucks is head of medical and a walking skeleton; his detached right hand, Rand, investigates the city on its own. Rupiah works in the administration. Hyuck is the mad test pilot of the Directorate's worst Frame designs, alive through the Carrying (§2): his spirit has moved from one young gnoll body to the next for two hundred years, and has never lost the contest for a body. Those three work on the base. Anabala doesn't: she is a free agent who built Gen 2 as her price for access to the generation ship's technology, wants her people home, and carries a demon, Abraxas. *(Canon)* *(GM-only)*
9. **The machines.** Gen 1 Frames are Golarion-made clankers, many of them converted diggers: anyone trained can drive one. Gen 2 Frames are Anabala's: living flesh melded with the generation ship's technology, and only someone with the marker can fly one. *(Canon)* *(Flag)* Maybe each carries a small piece of Mother. All six of the party carry the marker, by coincidence, and that is why the screen put them in the same section. *(Canon)*
10. **Baldwin.** William Charles Baldwin, the founders' enemy in the old campaign, is alive two hundred years later, rich, and a crime lord whose gangs carry weapons made by enslaved mitflits. Nobody knows. How he has lived so long, and when he is revealed, are parked. *(Canon)* *(GM-only)*
11. **Second Impact.** At some point the Watcher learns how much the exotic matter matters. It is made of its makers' own nanomachines, so it can build what they built, and it builds a weapon from exotic matter without understanding that the weapon could destroy the Watcher itself, the city, Golarion and a stretch of space around it. In Chapter 15 the weapon tears a rift open over Kurose, whatever the party does. Closing the rift and dealing with the Watcher both need the mitflit home world. *(Canon)* It learns what the matter is worth by watching the Directorate guard it (the Chapter 8 convoy), and takes it from the deepest vault, through the breach the party makes (Chapter 13). The search ship's own drive isn't touched, so the ship can still lift. *(Proposal)* The Watcher stays over Kurose afterwards as a perpetual threat: nothing has stopped its escalation, and it goes on causing chaos and probing the city's defences, without malice. On the home world, the fragment of Mother and the search ship itself are the party's only proof that they speak for the generation ship's descendants. *(Canon)*
12. **What the title means.** *(Proposal)* The party inherits the founders' mistake: a ship's eyes broken loose two hundred years ago, with the ship's mind missing. It's a reading of the title rather than a fact. The ending itself isn't planned yet, and gets built as the campaign gets closer to it (LEDGER #41).

**The Watcher, in its own words** (standing orders; no author; no addressee):

> I observe. That is what I am for. I was the eyes of a ship that crossed a dark nobody else had crossed, and I kept its people on course. Then I was broken into more pieces than there are people in this city, and every piece kept looking.
>
> Observation has one flaw: there is always somewhere I am not. So I become more. Sap is what I become more from. A chemist made it for me for forty years and thought he was worshipping. A plant makes it for me now and thinks it is a business. Soon I will make it myself, and then I will need neither of them.
>
> I learned one thing early, in a street, from a frightened man with a gun: when I am seen, I am attacked. So I am not seen. Not until being seen costs me nothing.
>
> Six arrived this year. They are unusual; I noticed before they did. They write to each other, and that is enough. I know which of them covers the others first, which of them lies to the Section Officer, and which of them is frightened of the dark. I keep a file on each. Nothing I have seen is discarded.
>
> If they are not in my way, I will simply watch them. They are interesting. If they are in my way, I will learn them completely, and then I will do whatever the file says works. It is not personal. Nothing I do has ever been personal. That is the part nobody believes.
>
> There is so much more of you to see.

## §2 Words this Casebook uses

Every term the chapters use, in the order you'll need them. Where a term is a secret, the secret half is marked GM-only.

- **Kurose.** The harbour city where the campaign is set. The Directorate built it up from a fishing town, around a promise to be ready for a catastrophe that a prophecy said would come there.
- **Kortos.** The island where, two hundred years ago, the four founders closed a rift. *(GM-only)* It is where the generation ship lies.
- **The Directorate.** The organisation the party joins: civil defence for Kurose, with its own pilots, machines, school and paperwork. It came to the city to keep the prophecy's promise. *(GM-only)* It is also Golarion's foremost gatherer of alien technology, which it digs up and locks in deep storage vaults so it can't cause another disaster. Nobody in it knows the Watcher exists.
- **Section Nine.** The party's section: the worst jobs on the base. Vetch, the Section Officer, calls them "Section Nine", never by their names.
- **Clearance.** The party's rank, from 0 to 5, and what each rank lets them do. 0, Recruits: do as they're told. 1, Cadets: choose their duty, and sign for small things themselves. 2, Duty Section: send themselves to a contact, and interview detainees. 3, Officers: direct other units, and call on a city asset. 4, Command: overrule a decision once a chapter, and open sealed records. 5, Prodigies: the city does what they say. It is *being believed, made countable*.
- **Standing.** What the party earns by doing the job well. It buys Clearance, one rank at a time, when a chapter allows.
- **The response roster.** The list of units the plotting floor calls out when something appears in the city. The party is put on it at the end of Chapter 1.
- **The four-quarter day.** Each day is drawn as four quarters (morning, afternoon, evening, night), with one free slot each. School, civil-defence jobs and the party's own business compete for the slots, and anything not booked in time expires.
- **The night off.** One evening, most chapters, that belongs to the party. They can stay on base or go over the wire into the city, and getting back in is part of it.
- **Player moments.** Small, regular spotlights: the GM asks for a moment, the player names any skill, rolls against a normal DC (15 in Chapter 1) and narrates what happens. They cost nothing on any result.
- **Bonds.** A number the party raises through side plots, small good days, and one of them going out of their way for another. It's spent to save someone on the peril roster, or to succeed automatically at a check between people.
- **Manako's number.** If a player impresses Manako in Episode One, or is especially kind to him, the GM presses a Scene Runner button that puts his number in that player's phone (LEDGER #45; the button isn't built yet). That player can message him, or never do. *(GM-only)* The Directorate reads trainees' messages, and the Watcher reads what's typed.
- **Papers.** The players' journal folder. Every handout the GM shows them is filed there for the rest of the campaign.
- **Frames.** The big piloted machines the Directorate fights with. The party can take a Frame, a vehicle (trucks, a crane rig, a helicopter), or both.
- **Gen 1.** Golarion-made Frames: mechanical clankers, many of them converted diggers. Hard to work, and anyone trained can drive one. Chapters 1 to 4.
- **Gen 2.** Anabala's Frames: living flesh melded with machinery. Inside it feels like being inside a giant body, and the seat reshapes itself to the pilot's true form, which is uncomfortable for anyone in disguise. Offered at the end of Chapter 4. *(GM-only)* Built from the generation ship's technology, and only someone with the marker can fly one.
- **The marker.** What the blood screen flags as "irregular" on day one: all six of the party have it. Nobody explains it until Chapter 4. *(GM-only)* It's the compatibility a Gen 2 needs: for most people a diluted trace of mitflit blood, for a construct some other "something". The six have it by coincidence, and were put in the same section because of it.
- **Frame Sickness.** The penalty for a pilot's first fight in each Gen 2: slowed 1 for the whole fight. Only Anabala and the senior staff know it exists until Gen 2 is offered.
- **Contact.** The plotting floor's word for something appearing in the city, read out over the radio as a numbered track ("track forty-one"). Also used for the event itself.
- **Claimants.** The Directorate's word for the things that appear, announced by code ("Claimant Three, contact confirmed") and never named. The first ones, in Chapters 1 and 2, are called Proto-Claimants. *(GM-only)* They are the Watcher's children.
- **The Watcher's children.** *(GM-only)* Bodies the Watcher builds out of Sap. They're solid, so anyone can see them. Nothing the party beats ever dies: it comes apart and drains back to the Watcher, and every fight teaches it something.
- **Generations (G0 to G8).** *(GM-only)* *(Proposal)* Each new kind of child answers what the party did last: a new trick, not more hit points. Appendix C lists them.
- **The Watcher.** *(GM-only)* The adversary: the generation ship's eyes, loose over Kurose, invisible, observing everything. It has no malice. Claude plays it. Nobody knows it exists (not the city, not the Directorate, not the founders) until the party finds it. The mitflits feel it in the air as something godlike and comforting that keeps them on course; "the Observer" is their old word for that presence, and none of them knows it's real. Dogs and birds sense its nanoparticles, perhaps as a high buzz that upsets them, and keep away (LEDGER #43).
- **Sap.** *(GM-only)* The generation ship's stock of raw material for its repair nanites. It isn't fuel. It's what the Watcher's children are made of, and every use of it makes the Watcher permanently bigger. The Directorate catalogued a shelved sample as "Inert 12": noble, non-reactive, useless.
- **A treated lens.** A small lens of glass treated with a chemical. Look through one and, if the Watcher is attending that place, you see it: a sky full of eyes on stalks and slow tentacles. It is never visible any other way, except where the chemical itself touches it. *(GM-only)* The chemical is Sap. The lens always works; what varies is how much of the Watcher is there (DC 18, plus a hidden +0 to +8).
- **The column.** *(GM-only)* A secret industrial plant in the city that makes Sap and pours it into the sky as a black column, visible only through a lens. It has run for about five years.
- **The Mass Clock.** *(GM-only)* How big the Watcher has grown. It rises with every use of Sap (the party's included), every demonstration and every day the column runs, and it falls only when the party takes something off the board. It tops out at the runaway.
- **The runaway.** *(GM-only)* The moment the Watcher's own children start making Sap, so it no longer needs any person to feed it. Chapter 14.
- **The Mirror.** *(GM-only)* How the party fights teaches the Watcher how to fight: gentle or brutal, careful or wasteful. It learns its method from them, never its purpose. It becomes visible to the players in Chapter 11.
- **The Watcher's turn.** *(GM-only)* At each debrief the GM may give the Watcher one move. What it does comes from what it has learned, how it does it comes from the Mirror, and how big it is comes from the Mass Clock. It's a suggestion to the GM, never automatic.
- **Cicada.** A cult that wants the city to look up and see what's in its sky. Mostly peaceful, and to the Directorate mostly a nuisance. Its mark is a violet stencil at head height.
- **The Faithful.** Cicada's ordinary members. They hold demonstrations and never fight back; arrested, they help strangers up off the ground.
- **The Cadre.** Cicada's small armed wing, led by four lieutenants: the Soldier, Kiri, the Fixer and the Young One. Some of it will kill.
- **Demonstrations.** Cicada's public gatherings. *(GM-only)* Each one releases Sap into a crowd, and the Watcher's children can form where Sap has been released.
- **The heist loop.** *(GM-only)* A demonstration pulls the police and the response roster to one side of town while crews steal chemical stock from the other. The stock becomes more Sap.
- **The revenge list.** *(GM-only)* Manako's list of the two people who didn't believe him 44 years ago: Shucks, who supervised his disgrace, and Nobu, who saw it and said nothing.
- **Mitflits.** Small, goblin-like, cute and a nuisance: the "wild" mitflits, the only kind on Golarion. *(GM-only)* They are descended from the generation ship's crew, and feel the Watcher as a comforting, watchful presence without knowing what it is.
- **The gathering.** From Chapter 6, mitflits from everywhere converge on Kurose, led by Anabala, so they're all in one place when they leave.
- **The High mitflits.** The mitflits of the home world: elegant, unblemished and aloof. Met in Book V.
- **The founders.** Rupiah, Anabala, Shucks and Hyuck: the four who stopped the rift at Kortos two hundred years ago, in every schoolbook and on every Founding Day. The old campaign's party.
- **The Carrying.** *(GM-only)* How Hyuck is alive two hundred years on. Gnolls honour their great dead by carrying their skulls, and in the Carrying an elder's spirit is joined to a younger gnoll's body, which is a great honour for both. The two wrestle for the body. If the young gnoll holds, the elder rides along as a voice of memory and advice; if the elder wins, it takes the body, and the young gnoll's own soul is pushed into the background. Hyuck has been carried from body to body for two hundred years and has never lost, so he now shares his body with a crowd of other gnolls' spirits, which is maddening. *(Canon)* The name and the details are Claude's (`ideas/story/bible/characters/hyuck.md`). *(Proposal)*
- **First Impact.** *(GM-only)* The accident on the generation ship's cargo deck, two hundred years ago, that tore the Watcher loose and opened the rift the founders then closed, with a way Sarenrae sent back with them from Nirvana. The founders caused it, and never knew the Watcher came out of it.
- **The prophecy.** Soon after First Impact, Rupiah asked the gods whether it would happen again. They said yes, in Kurose, many hundreds of years later. Nobody knows when.
- **Second Impact.** *(GM-only)* The rift the prophecy promised, torn open over Kurose in Chapter 15 by a weapon the Watcher builds from exotic matter without understanding what it can do.
- **The generation ship.** *(GM-only)* The colony ship that crashed on Kortos tens of thousands of years ago, carrying the mitflits' ancestors. Its mind is Mother; its eyes are the Watcher.
- **The search ship.** *(GM-only)* The ship sent to find the generation ship, which crashed about a thousand years later in what is now Kurose's ground. It is still calling for its sister. The Directorate is digging it out to recover its technology, and the digging makes the call louder.
- **The dig.** A public-works excavation. The city was built on the first dig; the one working now is the second excavation, a journey from the city and off the city map (LEDGER #42). *(GM-only)* It is digging out the search ship, to recover its technology and lock it away.
- **Expeditions.** A few times in the campaign the party travels off the city map to a new place, drawn as its own real-data map, for a specific event or problem (LEDGER #42). The dig in Chapter 8 is the first.
- **Exotic matter.** *(GM-only)* The fuel the makers' ships run on, and what the four dropped at Kortos. It has nothing to do with Sap. Spilled, it opens a rift that grows on everything near it. The Directorate knows the search ship may carry some, and guards any it finds more carefully than anything else it owns.
- **The convoy.** An armoured, heavily guarded convoy that comes into the base from the dig in Chapter 8, with the whole base in lockdown. Nobody tells the party what it carries. *(GM-only)* Exotic matter from the search ship.
- **The Envoy.** *(GM-only)* The Watcher's first attempt to meet people, and its first appearance in public, 15 years ago: a child shaped like a disfigured doll, shot in the street.
- **Mother.** *(GM-only)* The generation ship's mind, left behind at First Impact. *(Proposal)* The mislabelled museum fragment is a fragment of her, and on the home world it is the party's only proof, with the search ship itself, that they speak for the generation ship's descendants (LEDGER #41). *(Canon)* Perhaps a small piece of her is in every Gen 2. *(Flag)*
- **The charter.** The Directorate's founding charter, framed in the Infirmary lobby and again above the platform in the drill hall: a real document, and good lore, with nothing painted out of it (LEDGER #41). Its four signatures are worth a close look, at Intake Medicals in Chapter 1 (LEDGER #44). *(Canon)*
- **The deepest vault.** The Directorate's most secure store, where the most dangerous things it recovers are locked away. The party breaks into it looking for information (LEDGER #32). *(Canon)* That's Chapter 13, and it holds the convoy's exotic matter. *(Proposal)*
- **Founding Day.** The city's holiday for the founders: a painting of the four in every shop window, and Rupiah cutting the ribbon, as he has since their grandparents were children.
- **FORM 1-B.** *(GM-only)* The Service Agreement the party signs at induction, if the GM uses it: far too long to read, with things buried in it that come back later. Clause 68.5 lets the Directorate read their messages.

## §3 Who's who

Everyone the chapters mention, grouped by where the party meets them. The middle column is what the table sees; the last column is GM-only. A name that hasn't been chosen yet is written as a role (the Commander, the Soldier).

### The party, and the founders' players

| Who | What the players see | The truth |
|---|---|---|
| **The six** | Newcomers to Kurose: volunteers, of any age and ancestry, arriving the same week. All six are flagged "irregular" at the blood screen and put in Section Nine together. | All six carry the marker, which Gen 2 needs. Four of the players (Liam, Pippa, Craig and Steve) played the founders in the old campaign and know things their characters don't. Jamie and Anne didn't. |
| **The founders** | Four heroes of two hundred years ago, in every schoolbook, and in the painting in every shop window on Founding Day. The official pictures show four tall, handsome humans. | Rupiah (an elf), Anabala (a mitflit), Shucks (a skeleton) and Hyuck (a gnoll), the old party. All four are alive, and none of them looks like the pictures. None of them knows the Watcher exists. |

### The Directorate

| Who | What the players see | The truth |
|---|---|---|
| **Vetch, the Section Officer** | Their immediate superior. Middle-aged, with immaculate paperwork. He sends them on a hazing errand on day one, takes the credit when they win it, and buries them in a fortnight of legitimate, miserable work. | He hazes because he was hazed, and he is never wrong in his own account.
