He isn't cruel or dangerous. He is still there at the end of the campaign, doing the same to the next intake. Don't redeem him. |
| **The Deputy Director** | The second most senior person in the Directorate. His unreadable signature is on every acceptance letter; he gives the address at Assembly, and in Episode Two he calls them in to answer for what they did. | Intimidating at first, then plainly a good and wise leader. His name isn't chosen yet. |
| **The Commander** | The head of the Directorate, seen at a distance: a former pilot. | Puts the mission above the people carrying it. Older notes call him Oda; his name isn't settled. |
| **Shucks Mordant** | Head of medical, and a walking skeleton ("Mordant. Shucks Mordant. Yes, that one. Arm."). He examines each of them at Intake Medicals and tells each one their screen came back irregular. In Episode Two he walks into the Deputy Director's office without an appointment and is let straight in. | A founder. He has known what the marker means since their intake. Forty-four years ago he supervised Manako's disgrace, so he is on Manako's revenge list. |
| **Rand** | A name on paper: "Rand" of Medical countersigned their roster, and someone chalks "Same six. Tomorrow." on the board. | Shucks's detached right hand, living its own life: it doesn't speak, flies on a kite, trusts nobody, and keeps the only honest paper archive on the base. It writes in Shucks's old handwriting, and never types, so the Watcher has never read it. It read Shucks's intake notes, found six carriers of the marker, and did what Shucks wouldn't: it countersigned their requisition and their roster, wrote the override, and chalked the board (LEDGER #43, accepted in #46). *(Canon)* |
| **Rupiah** | A name in the history lesson, and the elf who still cuts the ribbon on Founding Day, as he did when their grandparents were children. | A founder, an elf noble, the Star Singer who asked the gods and got the prophecy. He works in the administration. |
| **Nobu, the Quartermaster** | Runs Kit Issue and the budgets. Harried, and generous in small, unexplained ways ("Right. Section Nine. I've got you."). He is who they argue with about repairs. | Manako's lab assistant 44 years ago. He saw the tentacle, and wouldn't back Manako up, because he thought the chemical had made them both see things. Now he secretly funds the column, through the crime lords, as private amends. He is on Manako's revenge list, and Manako suspects him. |
| **Hulda Brack** | Head of the machine shop; every repair crew answers to her. Loud and tough, and the best friend their machines have. A nuar: bovine head, heavy horns, and no glamour. | A nuar, and so an alien (LEDGER #39, #44). Disguise magic is cheap here (glamoured clothing sells on the high street), and aliens are treated as outsiders. Hulda refuses to hide or tone down what she is for anyone, like many aliens in the city. On the base nobody stares; out in the city, people would. |
| **Taka** | A trainee a year ahead of them who flies a Gen 1. He sits with them at the canteen, sends them off to the drains, and turns up at six in the morning before their first machine to tell them the one true thing nobody official will. | *(Proposal)* In Chapter 3 he puts his own clanker between a monster and an evacuation route. It's wrecked, and he doesn't fly again for a while. |
| **The Young One** | A Directorate Frame pilot a year or two ahead of them, a little competitive ("You're Section Nine? Rough. We got harbour patrol."). | Cicada's source inside the Directorate: turned at one of Kiri's shows, like Nozomi was nearly turned. Masked, he is the fourth member of the Cadre. He kills someone in a heist in Chapter 10 and is unmasked in Chapter 12. His name isn't chosen yet. |
| **Nozomi** | A name struck through in older chalk on the Hangar Two board, and a name overheard at Intake Medicals. Her disappearance is why every acceptance letter this year is stamped BROUGHT FORWARD. | At a Cicada show she was told she was working for the wrong side. The next day she turned off her beeper and stopped answering. She is detained and being questioned. |
| **Nao** | A classmate. | One of Cicada's quiet Faithful. In Chapter 9 the party finds Nao's own file among Cicada's records. |

### Cicada

| Who | What the players see | The truth |
|---|---|---|
| **Manako** | Manako, an old man at a tea house, who in Episode One shows them how to finish the errand properly and embarrass the man who sent them. He was a chemist, and the Directorate once hazed him the same way. He gives his name freely, and his number to anyone who impresses him. | Cicada's founder and author. The Directorate chemist it disgraced 44 years ago. The Watcher's first supplier, and the only person who can steer it at all. He doesn't know what he is to it, and he thinks it is a god. He is sad, not evil. Every scene with him needs careful handling so it leaks no secret and explains nothing early (LEDGER #44). He talks freely about his past and never about the Watcher; he is openly bitter about the Directorate and fatherly to recruits, which is part of why he makes an effective cult leader (LEDGER #45). |
| **Aoi** | Never seen. | Manako's wife, killed at the Envoy incident 15 years ago by one of Hori's rounds. The Directorate filed her as "one deceased, unidentified". |
| **The Faithful** | Cicada's ordinary members: they hold demonstrations and never fight back. Arrested, they help strangers up off the ground. | Most of them believe the thing in the sky is holy, because its attention feels like being seen. |
| **The Soldier** | The disciplined leader of an armed Cadre squad. His people use non-lethal force, won't fire on children, withdraw by the book, and call the party "Section Nine", which they shouldn't know. | A former Directorate instructor. He was on the Directorate cordon at the Envoy incident, helped file Aoi as unidentified, and never knew her name. He wants to be caught. |
| **Kiri** | An elderly illusionist on the private-show circuit ("Watch my hands."). Her shows are invitation only. | Runs Cicada's recruiting shows. She loves Manako and would kill for him, not for the cause, and she is quietly planning who takes over from him. |
| **The Fixer** | Cicada's logistics man, who fights with a hook, a chain and a crowbar ("It's already been signed for."). | His crew died and nobody read the file; Manako was "the first man who ever read my paperwork". He skims money for the dead crew's families. The way to beat him is an audit. |
| **Rika** | Doctor Rika, a sceptic, reached through the others who were never believed. | One of the few at Manako's conference, 38 years ago, who refused the "mass hallucination" explanation. She investigated alone and was believed by nobody. |

### Others

| Who | What the players see | The truth |
|---|---|---|
| **Hori** | A retired police officer, reached in Book III. | He shot the Envoy in fear 15 years ago, and one of his rounds killed Aoi. He rushed her to hospital and couldn't save her. A court cleared him, and that is where he learned her name. He retired anyway, in self-imposed disgrace. |
| **Shinobu Kudo** | "The girl in the news": seventeen, held without charge. In Chapter 6 she is hit by a lorry in front of a crowd and walks away. | Splashed point-blank with Sap, she carries a colony of the Watcher's nanites, and she is its favourite subject. After the lorry she is jailed again, in a glass cell. Her father is Kudo. |
| **Bobbin** | A mitflit Elder. In Chapter 10 he sits inside a Frame so that nobody can use it: a strike. | Freed long ago from Baldwin's workshop by the founders. When the mitflits leave, he refuses to go. |
| **Anabala** | A founder, and the mitflits' High Priestess, who arrives in Kurose in Chapter 6 with the gathering. | She built Gen 2 as her price for the generation ship's technology, and she wants her people home. *(GM-only)* She carries Abraxas. |
| **Hyuck** | A gnoll statue at the harbour with HYUCK on the plaque, which a teacher calls somebody's mascot. Then, in Chapter 7, a mad gnoll test pilot in a ridiculous prototype. | A founder, alive through the Carrying (§2): two hundred years of young gnoll bodies, never once losing the contest for one, so he shares his body with a crowd of other gnolls' spirits (LEDGER #41). He flies the Directorate's worst Frame designs. *(Canon)* The statue is him, in the body he wore when it was carved. *(Proposal)* |
| **The mitflits** | Small, goblin-like, cute and a nuisance: the "wild" mitflits, living around the city and the hangars. From Chapter 6 more arrive from everywhere: the gathering. | Descendants of the generation ship's crew, gathering so that they're all in one place when they leave. They feel the Watcher in the air as a comforting, godlike presence that keeps them on course, and don't know it's real. Their cousins at home, the High mitflits, are elegant, unblemished and aloof. |
| **Abraxas** | Nothing, until Chapter 13. | *(GM-only)* A female knowledge demon who lives in Anabala and feeds on information. Once loose, she and the Watcher make a dangerous pair. |
| **William Charles Baldwin** | Nothing, for a long time: only impossible weapons in gang hands. | *(GM-only)* The founders' old enemy. Alive, rich, and a crime lord. |
| **Mother** | Perhaps a faint voice in a mislabelled museum fragment. | The museum fragment is a fragment of her, and on the home world it's the party's only proof, with the search ship itself, that they speak for the generation ship's descendants (LEDGER #41). *(Canon)* The generation ship's mind, left behind at First Impact, weakened. *(Proposal)* Whether a small piece of her is in every Gen 2 is open. *(Flag)* |
| **The Watcher** | Nothing, for most of the campaign. Its children, from the first contacts on. | *(GM-only)* The campaign's adversary, played by Claude (§1, T4). Nobody knows it exists until the party finds it. |

## §4 How the mystery works

What the party is trying to find out, Book by Book; the rules that keep it fair; how they come to see the thing over the city; and the knot it all leads to.

### 4.1 One question per Book

| Book | The question | The first answer on offer | What's true | Who holds the answer |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| I | What is attacking the city? | Cultists are summoning monsters. It's the Directorate's official line, and every early attack happens somewhere Cicada has been. | The Watcher's children form where Sap has been released, and each new kind learns from the last fight. | Vetch, for the official line; Manako at the tea house, for the help |
| II | Who is doing it? | The cult, or whatever the cult worships. | Nobody is doing it. The thing is following a call from a ship buried in the ground, and it's hungry. | Kiri; the stray; Anabala, for the ships |
| III | What is it? | A god, says Cicada. A monster, says the city. | The generation ship's eyes, torn loose at First Impact, drawn here by the search ship's call, fed by a grieving man, and learning from everyone. | Hori; Rika; Rand's archive; the museum fragment |
| IV | Can it be stopped? | Kill it, or kill him. | Neither works. It's spread across the whole sky, he's the only one who can steer it, soon it won't need anyone, and it's building a weapon it doesn't understand. | Manako; Abraxas |
| V | What do we do with it? | Destroy it. | Open, and built as the ending gets closer (LEDGER #41). Perhaps it disperses for good, perhaps it survives and becomes harmless, perhaps the mitflits come back and scoop it out of the air to take it home. | The home world; Mother's fragment, as their proof |

### 4.2 Ten rules for this mystery

1. **One truth, fixed first.** Everything the party finds agrees with §1, even when it misleads them.
2. **A wrong answer to start inside.** The Directorate's line, that cultists summon monsters, is reasonable, the early evidence supports it, and it's wrong.
3. **Oddities before explanations.** Book I gives only things that don't fit: contacts plotted before the alert, nothing that dies, a street the dogs won't use.
4. **Three roads to every conclusion that matters.** No discovery hangs on one roll on one night (Appendix A).
5. **Every answer opens a bigger question.** Each Book's answer is the next Book's question.
6. **The players make the accusations.** Nobody hands them Cicada's author, what the Watcher is, or who signed their roster. They put it together and say it out loud. Nobody else even suspects the Watcher exists: the party are the ones who find it (LEDGER #40).
7. **A person on every layer.** Each layer of the truth is held by someone with their own reason to hold it: Rand, Hori, Rika, Anabala, Manako.
8. **The mystery fights back.** The more the party learns, the more the Watcher learns about them. Investigating is itself a move in the game.
9. **The truth makes it worse.** Learning what it is doesn't switch the horror off: it has no malice, and no off switch.
10. **The end of the mystery is a choice.** Knowing what the Watcher is solves nothing. Then the party has to decide what to do with something that isn't evil.

### 4.3 Seeing it: the horror in three steps

- **Chapter 6, at the show.** *(Canon)* At Kiri's show the whole audience looks up through treated lenses and sees the sky over Kurose black with eyes on stalks and slow tentacles, always moving. Cicada calls it a god, and nobody explains it. The lenses are collected at the door, so afterwards the party can't see it again until they understand what the glass is treated with.
- **Chapter 8, down in the dig.** *(Canon)* Down in the dig, a journey from the city, through a lens they have treated themselves, the same thing is packed around the buried ship, with filaments threaded up through the rock. It is in the ground as well as the sky.
- **Chapter 10, the whole city.** *(Proposal)* Cicada gets treated lenses into thousands of hands, and the city looks up. The Directorate bans the lenses, which makes Cicada look right.

*(GM-only)* Why it looks the way it does, for the GM only; at the table its cause and origin stay a mystery (LEDGER #36). It is a sensor that can't stop perceiving, so it grew eyes on stalks, and filaments to listen with. It's made of repair-nanite stock, and it reaches down because the ship below is calling it. It is strange and consistent, and nobody's god. It is never visible without the chemical (LEDGER #36, #39), and its children, which are solid, are the only part of it anyone can see unaided. The middle step is the one to lean on hardest: the thing coiled underground around the buried ship, found by the Directorate's own digging. **The horror is that it's reasonable.**

### 4.4 The dilemma

*(Flag)* This needs work before it's used (LEDGER #36). The knot, as plainly as I can put it: by Book III the party knows three things that pull against each other. The thing over the city isn't evil. The only person who can steer it is the man who fed it. And the city's surest defences (the sirens, the fleet, fighting it hard) teach it to be dangerous, because it learns its methods from how it's treated. So every obvious move makes something worse: killing Manako leaves it unsteered, fighting it teaches it to fight, and leaving it alone lets it grow. Its escalation ends in a weapon it doesn't understand (Chapter 15). Every ending in Book V is an answer to that knot. A fourth pull, the temptation to use its eyes in Chapter 14, is on hold until it has a mechanism; the likeliest is a piece of Mother in every Gen 2.

### 4.5 The two faces of the problem

**Manako: sad, not evil.** He wanted one thing, to be believed, and he was right. Forty-four years ago a spill in his lab showed him a tentacle; his assistant wouldn't back him up, and he lost his career for telling the truth. He spent the next years proving it, and learned to see the whole sky full of it. He arranged a meeting in good faith for the thing he'd spent his life proving, and a frightened officer's stray round killed his wife in front of him. Everything since has been grief trying to make the city look up. He's kind to strangers because he still thinks he's the man he used to be. He works for criminals at arm's length so he never has to see what he's part of, and when he finally sees it, he accepts it without enjoying it. The tragedy isn't that he's wicked. It's that he's right about the sky and wrong about what it is; that he's feeding the thing whose first hello got his wife killed; and that the party are the first people with any authority to believe him, and the ones who have to stop him. Play him with no villain beats: no gloating, and no cruelty for its own sake. **He never meant any of it.**

**The Watcher: understandable, not a god.** It's the navigation and sensor system of the generation ship that four of the players' old characters broke, two hundred years ago, fetching fuel on a cargo deck. That's the whole of its origin, and they were there, though they never knew what came out of it. It does what it was built to do, which is look. Once it kept a ship full of people on course; without the ship's mind to guide it, looking has no limit, and it escalates without understanding why. So it grows eyes; to grow them it needs Sap; and to get Sap it has learned to use people. It came to Kurose because the search ship buried in Kurose's ground is still calling it. It hides because the first time it said hello, it was shot. It learns because that's what a sensor system does with data, and the party is the richest data it has ever had. **None of that is evil, and all of it is dangerous.**

## §5 How the party grows

Three things climb together, Book by Book: the party's rank, how open their jobs are, and what the Watcher knows. Manako's mood moves with them, and it changes his children: a patient man's are aimless, a delighted man's are showy, a cornered man's are wasteful, a frightened man's are enormous.

| Book | Chapters | The party's rank | How their jobs come | What the Watcher has learned | Manako's mood |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| I Grunt Work | 1–4 | Recruits → Duty Section (Clearance 0 → 2) | Assigned, with the method: clear this, hold that | Things that move are alive; Frames matter | Patient |
| II The Big Leagues | 5–8 | Duty Section → Officers (2 → 3) | Assigned; the method is theirs | Frames have pilots; pilots have people | Patient → Delighted |
| III The Reel | 9–12 | Officers → Command (3 → 4) | Only the problem is given; they set the goal | There are institutions; the party's own tactics | Delighted → Cornered |
| IV The Promise Comes Due | 13–15 | Command → Prodigies (4 → 5): the whole Directorate | Many at once; they choose which | There are people; it can feed itself; it can build what its makers built | Frightened → broken, if it runs as drafted (LEDGER #42) |
| V The Long Way Home | 16–20 | Past the ranks: a starship | They set them | Whatever the party taught it | Decided closer to the time |

Rank is Clearance, from `design/strategy-layer.md`: *being believed, made countable*. The party climbs where everyone who was right and not believed failed: Manako, Hori, Rika. Their jobs open up as they climb, until by the end they decide what the problem is. The Watcher climbs with them, one step behind, because what it learns is what they show it.

### 5.1 What runs under every chapter

- **Expeditions.** A few times, the party travels off the city map to a new place with its own real-data map, for a specific event or problem (LEDGER #42). The dig in Chapter 8 is the first.
- **School and the day.** The four-quarter day runs throughout: school, civil-defence work and their own business compete for the slots, and anything not booked in time expires. School runs until graduation in Chapter 14.
- **The Watcher's turn.** At each debrief the GM may give it one move (§2).
- **Player moments.** Regular small spotlights that cost nothing on any result.
- **The night off, and one phone number.** One evening most chapters belongs to the party. If one of them has Manako's number, they can message him (LEDGER #45).
- **Bonds.** The people who care whether they live, and the number that says how much. It keeps the tone warm, and it pays out as saves.
- **Handouts are kept.** Every document the party is shown is filed in their Papers for the rest of the campaign (`design/handouts.md`).
- **Flashbacks.** Between Books, the four founders' players play their old characters (Appendix E).

## §6 Chapters 1–20 on one page

**The story in one paragraph.** Six newcomers arrive in Kurose, a city built to wait for a catastrophe, and are put in the section with the worst jobs. Strange things start forming in the streets, and the Directorate blames a cult; the evidence agrees, until Chapter 5 brings an attack nowhere near the cult. The six are given living machines, and something small, a crumb of a beaten child that didn't drain away, follows one of them home. At a magic show, through treated lenses, a hall full of people sees the sky full of eyes. Down in the dig, a journey from the city, the same thing is coiled around a buried starship. Then the whole city sees it. Following the people nobody believed (a retired policeman who learned a dead woman's name, a doctor nobody has believed for thirty-eight years, and a detached hand that keeps paper records), the six find the truth: it's the broken eyes of the starship the founders wrecked two hundred years ago, fed by a kind old man who thinks it's a god and whose wife died at its first hello. The accusation is theirs to make. The Directorate's leadership falls to them. The thing learns to feed itself and builds a weapon it doesn't understand, the rift the gods foretold opens over the city, and a starship full of mitflits carries the six to the only place that can close it. Then they come home to close it, and to face something with no malice and no limit, whose end is still to be written.

| Ch | Title | What the players do | What they learn | The Watcher |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| | **Book I: Grunt Work** (Chapters 1–4) | | | |
| 1 | Nothing Has Ever Happened Here | Six newcomers are flagged, hazed and sent down the drains, then take their first machines onto a harbour road and stand between forty people and three things nobody can name. | Only oddities: six flagged screens, a violet stencil at head height, a countersignature nobody can place, contacts plotted before the alert. | G0, Proto-Claimants: copies of shapes it has seen |
| 2 | The Fortnight | The response roster starts calling. Every contact is somewhere the cult has been, the Directorate says the cult is summoning these things, and the evidence agrees. | The official line: cultists summon these things. Nothing that's beaten dies. | G1: to rethink (LEDGER #46) |
| 3 | Retrofit | The party turns their diggers into fighters, meets Cicada's armed Cadre for the first time, and watches Taka make a choice. | Every Breaker goes for the same weak point. The Cadre knows their section's name. | G2, Breakers: knows how clankers break |
| 4 | Irregular | The party learns to predict where the next one will form and gets there first, finds out what their blood screen meant, and is offered Gen 2. | It watches a place before it forms there, which isn't how summoning works. | G2, and learns it's being tracked |
| | **Book II: The Big Leagues** (Chapters 5–8) | | | |
| 5 | Inside | The first Gen 2 flights, an attack nowhere near the cult, and something small that follows one of them home. | An attack with no Sap anywhere. The summoning theory breaks. | G3, Seekers: Frames have pilots |
| 6 | The Show | At an invitation-only magic show the whole hall looks up through treated lenses and sees the sky full of eyes. The mitflits start arriving, and a girl walks away from a lorry. | The first look: eyes on stalks over the whole city. Perhaps a voice in a museum case. | G3 → G4: seen, and remembers who looked |
| 7 | The Test Pilot | A gnoll test pilot in a ridiculous prototype, a chemistry lesson from a delighted Manako, children at the doors of the people they love, and somebody acting on a plan they only made in private. | Someone reads their plans. The statue is right and the pictures are wrong. | G4: pilots have people |
| 8 | The Second Excavation | The dig breaks into a whole second ship. The party goes down with it and finds the thing coiled around it, sees an armoured convoy come into a locked-down base, and learns their own side reads their mail. | It's in the ground too, wrapped around the ship. Something came out of the ship that the Directorate fears more than anything. And both sides read their messages. | G4, in its nest |
| | **Book III: The Reel** (Chapters 9–12) | | | |
| 9 | The Column | An audit of Cicada's money leads to a plant pouring Sap into the sky, and the Cadre fights to kill to protect it. | What Sap is. Roughly how fed the thing is. A client called Croglang. | G5 begins: there are institutions |
| 10 | The City Sees | Treated lenses flood the city and everyone looks up. The Directorate bans them, the Watcher hits the Directorate itself, and every siren makes it worse. | It feeds on alarm. A mitflit strike. A killing. | G5, institution-breakers |
| 11 | What It Was For | The party brings together everyone who was right and never believed, hears what the Envoy really was from the man who shot it, and meets their own last fight coming back at them. | The truth: a ship's broken eyes. The Envoy was a greeting. How they fight is what it becomes. | G6, the Reel |
| 12 | The Accusation | The party works out who wrote Cicada and says it out loud. Then they protect the people on his list, and the test pilot turns out to be Hyuck. | Cicada's author. Killing him cuts the only rope. Money above the crime lords. | G6, wasteful as Manako breaks |
| | **Book IV: The Promise Comes Due** (Chapters 13–15) | | | |
| 13 | The Deepest Vault | The party breaks into the Directorate's deepest vault looking for answers, a demon gets out, the Watcher's people-shaped children follow them in and take what the convoy brought, and the Directorate's leadership falls to the six. | *(Proposal)* The Watcher now has the exotic matter. | G7, Envoys: there are people |
| 14 | The Makers | The Watcher's children start feeding themselves and it starts building something, and the six graduate under a sky full of eyes. | It doesn't need anyone now, and it's building something. | G8, Makers: the runaway |
| 15 | Second Impact | The Watcher's weapon tears open the rift the gods promised, and the search ship lifts with the mitflits aboard, leaving the Watcher over the city. | The answer is on the mitflit home world. | Stays over the city, still escalating |
| | **Book V: The Long Way Home** (Chapters 16–20) | | | |
| 16 | Lift | The search ship, restored and crewed by the mitflits, leaves Golarion with the six in command and nobody above them. | What the ship is for. | Left behind, over Kurose |
| 17 | Derelicts | Boarding actions on ships that fell at Golarion, the reason ships fall there, and news from home arriving as a clock. | Why ships fall at Golarion. | — |
| 18 | Landfall | The mitflit home world, and the High mitflits. | Yours to decide. | — |
| 19 | The Problem at Home | A unique, extreme problem on the home world, where the answer to both crises lives. | Yours to decide. | — |
| 20 | The Return | Home to a city that held, to close the rift and settle what becomes of the Watcher. | What it has learned from them. | Still over Kurose; its fate is open |

Each chapter says what happens in front of the players, in order, what they can choose, and what they can find out. The GM notes carry the why. Chapter 1 is built; everything after it is a draft that gets looser the further it goes, and none of it decides what the players will do. Past Episode Two, many answers wait on purpose until the table gets close, so they can respond to what the players did (LEDGER #42).

## §7 Book I: Grunt Work (Chapters 1–4)

*Section Nine: the worst jobs, Gen 1 clankers, and a petty superior.*

- **The question:** What is attacking the city?
- **The party:** Recruits in the section nobody wants, driving converted diggers.
- **Their jobs:** Assigned, and so is the method: clear this, hold that.
- **Against them:** Vetch's paperwork. The Watcher's first three generations of children, which can only form where Cicada has released Sap, so every attack looks like the cult's work. The Soldier's squad, which doesn't kill.
- **Manako:** Patient: kind, for real. A patient man's children are aimless.
- **The breather:** The night off, and school.
- **It ends:** Promoted past Vetch, offered Gen 2, and the official story cracked.

### Chapter 1: Nothing Has Ever Happened Here

*Recruits · Clearance 0 · Episodes One and Two, built.* Six newcomers are flagged, hazed and sent down the drains, then take their first machines onto a harbour road and stand between forty people and three things nobody can name.

**At the table**

1. **Before anyone's character exists,** the GM tells what every child in Kurose knows: what kind of world this is, the rift at Kortos and the four people standing in front of it at the end, the Directorate that crossed the world to wait for a next time without ever saying why, the city it built, and what a Frame is. *(From Up Here)*
2. **Arrival.** Each player is handed their acceptance letter, their own copy with their character's name typed in. The reporting date is ruled through, stamped BROUGHT FORWARD, with "Monday next" written beside it. Each player then introduces their character arriving in Kurose: how they look, how we first see them, and one small problem on the way in, rolled with a skill they choose. One of them passes a violet mark painted on a wall at head height. *(The Road In)*
3. **Intake Medicals.** The six meet in the infirmary queue. Form 12-B asks for their mother's maiden name, twice. Staff hunting for a missing pilot can be overheard. Each character has a private medical with Shucks Mordant, a doctor who is a walking skeleton ("Shucks. Yes, that one. Arm."), whose badge a roll can place as a founder's name, and he tells each of them the same thing: their screen came back irregular. Talking afterwards, they can find out it happened to all six. They come out as Section Nine.
4. **Assembly.** Four hundred candidates sing an anthem the six don't know. The Deputy Director tells them what the Directorate is for, how their days run, and its two rules: disobeying an order is serious, breaking curfew is not. Above the platform hangs the founding charter, and anyone who looks closely can study its four signatures. Vetch collects Section Nine.
5. **School and Kit Issue.** First period, each picks a room: local studies, where the four founders are named, or Hulda Brack's workshop induction. Nobu issues uniforms and a hundred and fifty credits of kit each, and an extra to anyone who asks him properly. Vetch inspects them and hands Section Nine a docket. Second period is free: natural history, where they learn what lives in the drains, or the small-arms range. Every lesson pays off that night.
6. **The Errand.** The docket reads "COMPOUND, LEVELLING, BUBBLE, ONE (1)": the bubble in a spirit level, a hazing. They walk into the city, to an instrument makers' workshop: "Love, we make the vials. There's nothing in the bubble." The makers send them on to Manako, who owns a tea house a few streets away, in a district that defers to him. He gives his name, tells them he was hazed the same way and came back empty-handed and laughed at, and gives them his plan: requisition the real vial exactly as written, which needs a countersignature from a desk above Vetch's. He writes the part number on the back of a bill. The table chooses how loudly to file it. If one of them is especially kind, he gives them his number. They pass a second violet mark on the way.
7. **The canteen and the drains.** On the island train home they get twenty quiet minutes, then hand the requisition in at the stores before nineteen hundred. At supper Taka claims them as his table, a father on the radio is trying hard not to shout, and the Young One gloats about harbour patrol. Vetch reads the evening's orders and gives Section Nine the worst: storm drains under the old town, Dock C at twenty-one hundred. Waders that don't fit, Vetch's one-line briefing, and a launch across the harbour.
8. **Flood Tunnel Clearance.** In the drains they power a line and clear a jammed feed, and a nest of giant centipedes wakes. They can fight, drive them off, or use the room. At the top Vetch is waiting with their requisition, countersigned in green ink over Medical's stamp, "Rand, Medical": "Which one of you is Rand?" Nobody called Rand works in Medical. Then each player picks where to spend the night, on The City: Episode One ends on the night's rests.
9. **Episode Two: the fortnight.** *(Episode Two is a ghost for now, D056: what follows is the old outline.)* Vetch never mentions the vial, and hands them a fortnight of legitimate, miserable work, scheduled before they walked in. They learn the four-quarter day by losing a slot, scrape the sea wall at low tide against a real clock (there's a violet stencil under the growth), and spend a long afternoon on something of their own, which can be the tea house.
10. **The Override.** Vetch reads out an order he didn't ask for: the six people named are on Frame and vehicle familiarisation at 0700 tomorrow, and the fortnight still stands.
11. **The night off, and six in the morning.** Each chooses their evening: on base, or over the wire into the city at night, where getting back in is part of it. At six in the morning Taka turns up to tell them the one true thing nobody official will.
12. **Familiarisation and the exercise.** In the hangar each picks a machine (converted diggers, a crane rig, old army trucks, a helicopter, one inexplicable sports car) and meets their own crew chief. They run the course, and hear the siren once, when it means nothing.
13. **First Contact.** A real alert. The Comms Officer tells them plainly to stay clear ("I will not order you in"). On the harbour road a tram is stopped across the road with about forty people aboard, and three things nobody can identify are coming down it. They can obey, or go in. If they go in, the job is an empty road: get the tram's people out, hold the things back, and use what they found their machines can do. The real response units arrive and take over. They don't rescue the party.
14. **The Hammer.** They wait on a bench outside the Deputy Director's office, then answer for what they did, each asked a question of their own, including what they'll say about the Comms Officer who told them to stay out. Shucks walks in without an appointment and is given a private word inside ten seconds. They leave with something that isn't a punishment: from tomorrow, their section is on the response roster.
15. **The Names Are Still Up.** Alone in the hangar at night, with their machines, they find their six names on the board by the door, and under them, in chalk: "Same six. Tomorrow."

**Choices**

- **How to file the vial.** Quietly, or so people see, and a third way if the table invents one. Loudly, the whole hall knows by supper.
- **Let Vetch keep the credit?** They can object or let it go. There's no appeal either way, and the roster stands.
- **The night off.** On base, or over the wire. Whoever goes out has to get back in.
- **Obey, or go in?** Obeying is a real, written branch. Going in means answering for it in The Hammer.
- **The hurt, or the road?** Going to the casualties isn't the noble choice, and holding the road isn't the callous one: a Frame holding the road is keeping people alive too.

**What they can find out**

- All six flagged irregular, and all six in Section Nine.
- The violet stencil at head height, three times: on the road in, on a wall facing a tram queue in the city, and under the growth on the sea wall.
- The four founders' names, at the medical and in a history lesson.
- A countersignature from "Rand" of Medical that nobody can place.
- Contacts plotted on the floor before the alert that reported them.
- A street the dogs won't use.
- Manako's name. On a very hard knowledge check, the scientist who had a breakdown at a conference and was expelled.
- A father on the canteen radio: his daughter, seventeen, held without charge.
- A seventh name, struck through, under theirs on the Hangar Two board.
- Something Frame-sized under a tarp, through the shutter to Hangar Four.
- Four signatures on the founding charter: one is HYUCK in capitals, and one is in a script nobody here can read.
- None of it is explained in Chapter 1.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- The stencils mark where Cicada will demonstrate next: always at head height, never facing a road.
- The centipedes in the drains are ordinary animals, not the Watcher's.
- The three things on the harbour road are Proto-Claimants, the Watcher's first children. They formed where Cicada clashed with the police some weeks earlier, because Sap was released there. Nothing dies: beaten, they come apart and drain away.
- Every letter this year is stamped BROUGHT FORWARD because Nozomi, the first pilot, stopped answering after a Cicada show, and the Directorate called its intake early.
- The six were put in Section Nine together because of the marker. Rand, Shucks's detached hand, countersigned their requisition and their roster, wrote the override order, and chalked the line on the board. Why: it does what Shucks won't. It read his intake notes, found six carriers in one intake, and got them onto the machines, together, as fast as the rules allow (LEDGER #43, accepted in #46; `bible/characters/rand.md`). *(Canon)*
- The charter's four signatures (accepted, LEDGER #44): Rupiah's elegant hand; Shucks's quick doctor's scrawl, which is the hand Rand writes in; HYUCK in capitals, pressed hard enough to tear the paper; and a line of small characters in mitflit script, which is Anabala's. Nothing on the charter is painted out: that plant is cut (LEDGER #41).
- Shucks tells the Deputy Director that all six carry the marker, and asks that they be kept together and flying. He doesn't say what the marker is for (LEDGER #43). He has seen his own old handwriting on the override, knows his hand wrote it, and takes responsibility for an order he never wrote (part of Rand's reason, accepted in LEDGER #46).
- Dogs and birds sense the Watcher's nanoparticles, perhaps as a high-frequency buzz that upsets them, and go anywhere else (LEDGER #43). *(Canon)* The street they won't use drops to the harbour road, where Cicada clashed with the police some weeks ago and Sap was released, and where the three Proto-Claimants form in Episode Two. *(Proposal)*
- The father on the radio is Kudo, and the girl is Shinobu, who walks away from a lorry in Chapter 6. The struck-through name is Nozomi. The tarp covers a Gen 2 prototype, offered in Chapter 4 (LEDGER #44).
- Every scene with Manako needs careful handling: he's too important to leak a secret or explain something early (LEDGER #44). His scenes are due a dedicated pass.
- *(Flag)* Neither the instrument makers' workshop nor Manako's tea house is sited yet: the tea house belongs in a district where he has a lot of influence, and the workshop a few streets from it. Both wait for you in the gazetteer (requests req-5 and req-6, LEDGER #46), and the Foundry scene still opens on the General Store's map and art. The Scene Runner button that gives a player Manako's number isn't built yet (GitHub #94).

**The Watcher:** G0, Proto-Claimants: copies of shapes it has seen. It notices six people it can't explain, and opens a file on each, from what they type. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** Three facts and no explanations: the machines are theirs, they're on the response roster, and a skeleton doctor can walk into the Deputy Director's office without knocking.

### Chapter 2: The Fortnight

*Cadets · Clearance 1.* The response roster starts calling. Every contact is somewhere the cult has been, the Directorate says the cult is summoning these things, and the evidence agrees.

**At the table**

1. Vetch's fortnight goes on: school, the four quarters of the day, and the section's tedious jobs. As Cadets they can now choose some of their duties, and sign for small things themselves.
2. The roster calls. On the plotting floor, every new contact is somewhere Cicada has been: a stencilled wall, a square where a demonstration was broken up, a street where something was spilled.
3. *(Flag)* The chapter's main contact is the Watcher's first new kind of child (G1), and it is to be rethought (LEDGER #46).
4. At another contact a Proto-Claimant has copied the gnoll statue from the harbour square. Mid-fight it turns on its own reflection in the water, which gives the party its opening.
5. Whatever they beat comes apart into a dark slick and drains away, and every piece runs the same way.
6. Police break up a Cicada demonstration. The cultists don't resist: as they're arrested, they help strangers up off the ground.
7. In the records the party can read the Directorate's file on an incident fifteen years ago: *a cultist was killed during a confrontation in which someone magically summoned a monster.* It fits everything they've seen.
8. Nozomi, whose name was struck through on the Hangar Two board, is in detention. Officially they can't interview detainees until Clearance 2. Unofficially they can find her now, and she tells them she saw something at a show and couldn't get back in the machine.
9. If one of them has Manako's number, the first messages, and he answers warmly.

**Choices**

- **Find Nozomi early?** It means going round the rules. She gives them Cicada's argument, from someone they trust, before they have any answer to it (fork).
- **What goes in the report?** Vetch wants the official line. What they saw is stranger.

**What they can find out**

- The official line on the old incident: a false trail, and a reasonable one (LEDGER #35).
- Nothing they beat dies. It comes apart and goes elsewhere, and the pieces all run the same way.
- Nozomi's reason for stopping.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- *(Flag)* G1 is cut and still to design (LEDGER #46).
- Every contact is at a Cicada site because, in Book I, the Watcher's children can only form where Sap has been released.
- The pieces drain away towards the dig, where the Watcher is thickest.
- At the show, Nozomi was told she was working for the wrong side. The next day she turned off her beeper.

**The Watcher:** G1, to rethink (LEDGER #46). It notes which of them covers the others first, and which of them lies to Vetch. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** If the cult summons these things, why do its people help strangers up off the ground?

### Chapter 3: Retrofit

*Cadets · Clearance 1.* The party turns their diggers into fighters, meets Cicada's armed Cadre for the first time, and watches Taka make a choice.

**At the table**

1. Refit. With Hulda Brack's machine shop they turn their utility machines into fighters: armour plate, a grapple rigged as a weapon, whatever the crew suggests. They spend an upgrade budget, which buys capability, never levels.
2. A contact at a works yard, with an evacuation to protect. The new children, Breakers, go straight for the same joints and boilers on every Gen 1 clanker, the party's included. Their crew chiefs' verdict afterwards: "it knows our machines better than the engineers do".
3. *(Proposal)* Taka puts his own clanker between a Breaker and the evacuation route. The machine is wrecked; he lives, and he isn't flying for a while.
4. They're sent to break up a demonstration, and meet an armed Cadre squad for the first time, covering the crowd's retreat. Its leader, the Soldier, is disciplined, non-lethal, withdraws by the book, and won't fire on anyone young. He calls them "Section Nine". His people won't finish off anyone who's down.
5. *(Proposal)* One of them is handed a card for an invitation-only magic show. The performer's name is Kiri.
6. *(Proposal)* Between chapters, an optional flashback: the founders' players play Baldwin Manor and the shadow siege.

**Choices**

- **Which refits?** Each one is a tactic the Watcher will study. The next generation is built against whatever they lean on.
- **Chase the Soldier, or protect the evacuation?** He withdraws by the book either way. What they choose tells him who they are.

**What they can find out**

- Every Breaker goes for the same weak point.
- The Soldier knows their section's name, which he shouldn't.
- The Cadre won't finish off anyone who's down.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- The Breakers are the Watcher's answer to the harbour road: it learned how clankers break.
- The Soldier knows "Section Nine" because the Young One, Cicada's source inside the Directorate, told them.
- The Soldier wants to be caught, but not yet (Chapter 11).
- Kiri's shows are where Cicada recruits. The card is the way into Chapter 6.
- The first "how did it know?" moment belongs here: a Claimant rolls Recall Knowledge against a PC, and it reads as a GM slip (Appendix D).

**The Watcher:** G2, Breakers. It notes their refits and the tactic they lean on most, and the next generation is built to beat it.

**Where it leaves them:** How does the cult know the name of their section?

### Chapter 4: Irregular

*Duty Section · Clearance 2.* The party learns to predict where the next one will form and gets there first, finds out what their blood screen meant, and is offered Gen 2.

**At the table**

1. Promotion to Duty Section (Clearance 2). They can now send themselves to a contact, and interview detainees, Nozomi included.
2. A heist. A demonstration pulls the police and the response roster to one side of town while a crew empties a chemical warehouse on the other. At the warehouse they meet the Fixer, with a hook, a chain and a crowbar ("It's already been signed for."), and a masked young Cadre fighter carrying a gang weapon no Golarion workshop could have made.
3. The papers. In the Directorate's archive, filed as discredited, they find a forty-year-old study of how to tell where "it" is paying attention. The author's name isn't on anything they can read.
4. Getting there first. With that technique, and the stencils that mark where Cicada will gather, they can predict where the next contact will form, choose when to go, and be waiting when it does. For the first time, the where and the when are theirs.
5. Shucks calls them in and tells them what "irregular" means: they carry a marker, and there is a kind of Frame that needs it.
6. Gen 2 is offered: Anabala's machines, living flesh melded with machinery, under a tarp in a hangar nobody uses. They're told about Frame Sickness. Each player chooses whether to take one.
7. Vetch is outranked by the order, and doesn't enjoy it. And a requisition they were about to make turns out to be approved already, countersigned Rand.

**Choices**

- **Take a Gen 2?** A real choice, not an upgrade: a Gen 1, a vehicle and a Gen 2 are three equal tracks. A Gen 2 is uncomfortable for anyone in disguise.
- **Interview Nozomi?** Now it's allowed. What she says depends on whether they found her early.

**What they can find out**

- Its attention gathers at a place before anything forms there, which is not how summoning works.
- The weapon's workmanship: tiny, precise hands, though nobody says whose.
- What the marker is for.
- Someone keeps approving their requests before they make them.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- The papers are Manako's, from before his disgrace: they're retracing his steps without knowing the papers are his, even though they know his name from Chapter 1. Keep it that way until his handwriting surfaces in Chapter 12.
- The masked fighter is the Young One. The weapon comes from Baldwin's workshops, made by enslaved mitflits. Don't name either.
- The tarp covers a Gen 2 prototype, in Hangar Four, glimpsed through the shutter in Episode Two's Familiarisation (LEDGER #44).
- *(Proposal)* Rand approved the requisition.
- *(Proposal)* Animals are a tell the party can learn: dogs and birds keep away from wherever the swarm is thick (LEDGER #43).
- The Gen 2 offer at the end of this chapter is canon (LEDGER #31).

**The Watcher:** G2. *(Proposal)* Somebody got there first, so it learns it's being tracked, and from now on it comes from the direction nobody is watching.

**Where it leaves them:** Why does it watch a place before it appears there?

## §8 Book II: The Big Leagues (Chapters 5–8)

*The Directorate's hotshots, flying Anabala's Gen 2.*

- **The question:** Who is doing it?
- **The party:** Duty Section, then Officers: the Directorate's sharp end.
- **Their jobs:** Assigned, but the method is theirs.
- **Against them:** The Watcher seeking out their machines, then their people. Their own Directorate, which reads their mail. Cicada going public.
- **Manako:** Patient, then Delighted: someone finally takes him seriously. A delighted man's children are showy.
- **The breather:** *(Proposal)* Founding Day: the painting of the four in every shop window, Rupiah cutting the ribbon, and the anthem about the harbour.
- **It ends:** Down in the dig, with the search ship and the thing coiled around it.

### Chapter 5: Inside

*Duty Section · Clearance 2.* The first Gen 2 flights, an attack nowhere near the cult, and something small that follows one of them home.

**At the table**

1. Whoever took a Gen 2 climbs in. It's like being inside a giant body, and the seat reshapes itself to their character's true form, which is uncomfortable for anyone in disguise. Whether and when to show the others that true form is each player's choice. Their first fight in it is under Frame Sickness.
2. The wild mitflits around the hangars keep away from the new machines, and can't say why.
3. A contact forms where there's no stencil, no demonstration and no spill: nowhere near the cult.
4. The new children, Seekers, go straight for the Gen 2s' hatches, trying to get inside.
5. *(Proposal)* After a fight, one crumb of what they beat doesn't drain away with the rest. It follows one of them back to their quarters and waits to be fed. The player names it: the stray.
6. The night before an operation, they plan it in their messages. The next fight opens with the enemy placed exactly against that plan.

**Choices**

- **Feed the stray?** It grows, shaped by whoever raises it, and later it's the proof that the Watcher can be raised differently (fork).
- **Tell anyone about it?** Hand it to Medical, or keep it quiet.

**What they can find out**

- A contact with no Sap, no stencil and no cult: the summoning theory breaks.
- The Seekers prefer Gen 2.
- The first "how did it know?": a fight opens against the plan they made in their messages.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- From Book II the Watcher's children can form wherever attention gathers, with no Sap needed.
- The Seekers prefer Gen 2 because Gen 2 is built from their own ship's technology, perhaps with a piece of Mother inside.
- The stray is kept (LEDGER #42): a piece of the Watcher that the players can befriend, or keep as a mascot, while it is really a piece of the thing threatening the whole city. Ingram: "we'll have to see if we can make it work." It's also a microphone: the Watcher hears what's said near it. *(Proposal)*
- The Watcher reads what they type into phones and Directorate equipment, messages to Manako included. It can't read paper: Rand's archive is safe.

**The Watcher:** G3, Seekers: Frames have pilots. *(Proposal)* It notes that the new machines are made of home.

**Where it leaves them:** If the cult isn't summoning them, what is?

### Chapter 6: The Show

*Duty Section · Clearance 2.* At an invitation-only magic show the whole hall looks up through treated lenses and sees the sky full of eyes. The mitflits start arriving, and a girl walks away from a lorry.

**At the table**

1. Cicada declares itself, in posters and pamphlets across the city: *look up*.
2. Kiri's show, and the card from Chapter 3 gets them in: invitation only, in a rented hall, full. At the door every guest is handed a small lens. At the climax the roof shutters open, Kiri tells them to look up through the lens, and the whole hall sees the sky over Kurose black with eyes on stalks and slow tentacles, always moving. Cicada calls it a god. Nobody explains it.
3. On the way out, the lenses are collected at the door. Outside, the sky is empty, and the party has no way to see it again.
4. If a player wants their character to be converted by what they saw, the show can genuinely do it.
5. Mitflits from everywhere start arriving in Kurose, led by Anabala: a founder, and a name from their schoolbooks. This is the gathering, coming so that they're all in one place when they leave. They say Kurose feels like being watched over, and they like it. *(Proposal)* Some of them go missing.
6. The party is in the crowd, or hears it on the radio: Shinobu Kudo, the girl in the news, is hit by a lorry and walks away. By evening she's in custody again.
7. On a school trip to the museum, a mislabelled fragment sits in a glass case. Anyone who stops and listens may hear a faint voice.

**Choices**

- **Tell the city what they saw?** An early sighting, and a big win for Cicada; or the Directorate's reflex to hide things becomes theirs too (fork, Chapters 6 to 9).
- **Let the show convert a character?** Only if the player wants it.
- **Look for the missing mitflits?** The trail leads to money nobody can see yet.

**What they can find out**

- The lens is treated with something.
- The Faithful believe its attention is holiness.
- Mitflits are going missing from the gathering.
- The fragment's voice, if anyone listens.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- The lenses are glass treated with Sap. A lens always works; what varies is whether the Watcher is attending that place, and how densely (DC 18, plus a hidden +0 to +8). That's why a lens "mostly doesn't work", and the players should be free to build wrong theories about it, as Manako did.
- They can't look again because nobody sells a lens. To see it again they have to make their own, which means understanding what the glass is treated with (LEDGER #36).
- Shinobu survived because she carries a colony of the Watcher's nanites, from a Sap splash. She's its favourite subject.
- The missing mitflits are taken for Baldwin's workshops.
- What the mitflits feel is the Watcher: something godlike and comforting in the air, that has always kept mitflits going the right way. None of them knows it's real, or what it is (LEDGER #40).
- The fragment is a piece of Mother (LEDGER #41). *(Canon)* Whether it really speaks, and who it talks to, is open (N21). *(Flag)*

**The Watcher:** G3, moving to G4. It has been seen, and it keeps a list of who looked. The last time it was seen, it was shot.

**Where it leaves them:** What is in the sky, and why has nobody else seen it?

### Chapter 7: The Test Pilot

*Duty Section, close to Officers.* A gnoll test pilot in a ridiculous prototype, a chemistry lesson from a delighted Manako, children at the doors of the people they love, and somebody acting on a plan they only made in private.

**At the table**

1. A gnoll test pilot flies a ridiculous experimental Frame, and it goes wrong over the base. The party has to stop it, or catch it. If Steve's character is there, it's his moment.
2. Seeing it again. To look again they need a lens of their own, and there are three ways to get one: ask Manako (by message, if one of them has his number, or at the tea house), who is delighted anyone asks and explains how to treat the glass; take a lens from one of the Faithful; or find the shelved sample in the Directorate's own catalogue. *(Proposal)*
3. G4 children turn up close to home: at a shop one of them uses, at a sibling's school, at a teacher's door.
4. They plan something unofficial in their messages. When they arrive, a Directorate unit is already there, sent by someone who knew.
5. The same trick from the other side: a line one of them only ever typed privately comes out of a stranger's mouth.
6. The test pilot is a gnoll, like the harbour statue marked HYUCK. Every official picture of the founders shows four tall humans.
7. *(Proposal)* Between chapters, an optional flashback: the founders' players play the jungle ship and First Impact, ending on the catch.

**Choices**

- **How they get a lens.** Each way teaches them something different: Manako's warmth, the Faithful's devotion, or what's on the Directorate's own shelf.
- **Warn their people?** Warning them shows the Watcher who matters.

**What they can find out**

- Somebody read their plan.
- The statue and the test pilot agree. The pictures don't.
- Someone with money is hunting the gathering.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- The test pilot is Hyuck. Nobody names him until Chapter 12. He's alive through the Carrying (§2), and sharing his body with a crowd of other gnolls' spirits is maddening (LEDGER #41).
- There are two readers. The Directorate reads their messages under the form they signed on day one (revealed in Chapter 8), and the Watcher reads them too. Neither knows about the other.
- The chemistry is Sap: Inert 12, the sample on the Directorate's shelf.
- Manako becomes the Delighted Man: someone finally takes him seriously, and his children grow showy.
- *(Flag)* The lens lesson is a Manako scene, so it needs the care LEDGER #44 asks for: he can teach the glass without saying what he thinks is in the sky, or who he is to Cicada.

**The Watcher:** G4: pilots have people. A private line turns up in a stranger's mouth.

**Where it leaves them:** Who read their plan?

### Chapter 8: The Second Excavation

*Officers · Clearance 3.* The dig breaks into a whole second ship. The party goes down with it and finds the thing coiled around it, sees an armoured convoy come into a locked-down base, and learns their own side reads their mail.

**At the table**

1. Promotion to Officers (Clearance 3): they direct other units.
2. The second excavation breaks through into a whole second ship. It lies a journey from the city, off the city map, on a map of its own, and getting there is part of the chapter (LEDGER #42). The party goes down with the excavation, commanding other units for the first time.
3. Through a lens they've treated themselves, the dig is packed with the thing: filaments threaded up through the rock, and eyes clustered around the buried ship like frost on a pipe.
4. Something in the ship wakes, or the dig is attacked from below, and the party runs the operation.
5. The ship is still sending a signal, and the digging makes it louder.
6. On the way back up, the Deputy Director's briefing on the dig quotes a message one of them sent privately. Pressed, he shows them the clause in the form they signed at induction: the Directorate has read their messages since day one. It wasn't the Watcher's source. Both were reading, and neither knew about the other.
7. Days later the whole base goes into lockdown: every door sealed, everyone held at their post. An armoured, heavily guarded convoy comes in from the dig and goes down to the vaults. Nobody tells them what's in it.

**Choices**

- ***(Proposal)* Report what they saw through the lens?** Report it, and the Directorate files it as a hallucination, as it filed Manako's 44 years ago; keep quiet, and the lens stays their secret.
- **Their mail.** Confront the Deputy Director, use the channel to feed out false plans, or move to paper.

**What they can find out**

- The search ship came looking for the first ship, and its systems are still calling.
- The digging makes the call louder.
- Two readers, not one.
- Something came out of the ship that the Directorate fears more than anything it owns.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- The Watcher came to Kurose for this call. This is its nest.
- The monitoring is Clause 68.5 of FORM 1-B, if the GM used the agreement; otherwise it's routine policy for trainees' messages.
- Anabala is drawn to the ship.
- *(Proposal)* Two digs, one ship. The city was built on the first dig, which found the search ship's hull (the Deputy Director says so at Assembly). The second excavation, a journey away, follows it to the whole ship (LEDGER #42).
- The convoy carries exotic matter from the search ship: fuel of the kind the four dropped at Kortos. The Directorate digs to recover the makers' technology and lock it away, and it guards this more carefully than anything it owns. Nobody explains the lockdown to the party (LEDGER #40).
- *(Proposal)* The Watcher watches the convoy too, and learns from the Directorate's caution that whatever is in it matters. It's the first step towards Chapter 15.
- The Directorate still doesn't know the Watcher exists. If the party reports what they saw through the lens, it's filed the way Manako's report was.

**The Watcher:** G4, at home. The Directorate's sharp end came into its nest, and it noticed. Then it watched the convoy, and learned that whatever was in it matters more to them than anything. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** It's in the ground as well as the sky, and their own side reads their mail.

## §9 Book III: The Reel (Chapters 9–12)

*A unit with clearance and its own operations.*

- **The question:** What is it?
- **The party:** Officers, then Command.
- **Their jobs:** Only the problem is given. The goal is theirs to set.
- **Against them:** The Watcher going for the Directorate itself, then for the party's own tactics. The column. The crime lords' money. A city in panic.
- **Manako:** Delighted, then Cornered: he learns he was only ever a subcontractor, and accepts becoming a monster. A cornered man's children are wasteful.
- **The breather:** *(Proposal)* A dinner with the sceptics: the first room in the campaign where everyone was right.
- **It ends:** With the accusation, which is the players' to make, and with knowing what the Watcher is.

### Chapter 9: The Column

*Officers · Clearance 3.* An audit of Cicada's money leads to a plant pouring Sap into the sky, and the Cadre fights to kill to protect it.

**At the table**

1. Now Officers, they raid a Cicada depot, seize the Fixer's books and audit them: the one fight he can't win. The money runs through the crime lords and back into the Directorate's own procurement, to Nobu.
2. The power bills lead to a plant in the city that draws far more than it should.
3. Through a lens, a black column pours from the plant into the sky, day and night. It has been running for about five years.
4. The raid. The Cadre defends the plant, and for the first time some of its people fight to kill.
5. In the plant's books one client trades as Croglang. For the four founders' players, it's the old name of Baldwin's castle.
6. Among Cicada's files is one for Nao, a classmate, who is one of the Faithful.
7. Once they know what Sap is, they can read roughly how fed the thing is: a rough gauge, never an exact number.

**Choices**

- **The column: shut it, seize it, or watch it?** Shutting it starves the Watcher now. Seizing it gives the Directorate its own Sap. Watching it keeps the trail warm.
- **Expose Nobu, or use him?** Exposing him stops the column's money, and every repair they rely on with it; using him makes them complicit, and they know it (fork).
- **Turn Nao in?** Trusted by the Directorate and nobody else, or harbouring a cultist (fork).

**What they can find out**

- What Sap is: the stuff the children are made of.
- Nobu's hand in the money.
- Croglang.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- Nobu funds the column as private amends for not backing Manako up 44 years ago. Whether he knows whose organisation launders the money is open.
- *(Proposal)* Croglang is Baldwin's front.
- Before the runaway (Chapter 14), cutting the column really does starve the Watcher. After it, the plant is one supplier among many, and taking it down is a win over the people, not the appetite.

**The Watcher:** G5 begins: there are institutions. They took its food, and it starts learning not to need people. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** Who is above the crime lords?

### Chapter 10: The City Sees

*Officers · Clearance 3.* Treated lenses flood the city and everyone looks up. The Directorate bans them, the Watcher hits the Directorate itself, and every siren makes it worse.

**At the table**

1. *(Proposal)* Cicada gets treated lenses into thousands of hands. Within days, crowds all over the city are looking up at the sky full of eyes.
2. The Directorate bans the lenses. Confiscations begin, and it looks like a cover-up.
3. The new children hit several of the Directorate's own sites at once: its logistics, and the plotting floor. The party chooses where to be.
4. Every time a siren sounds, things get worse. They can protect the plotting floor, calm the streets, or argue with the Commander to silence the sirens. The goal is theirs to set.
5. During a demonstration, a heist on the other side of town goes wrong, and the masked young fighter kills someone. If one of them has Manako's number, his next message doesn't mention it.
6. Bobbin, a mitflit Elder, sits inside a Frame so nobody can use it, and the mitflit workers strike. Nobu has to rule on it, and the party may be asked to break it.

**Choices**

- **Break the sit-in?** Bobbin can't be pulled out without being killed, and the mitflits won't forget it; or the city goes undefended for a while, and people notice (fork).
- **Where to be when the sites are hit.** They can't be everywhere.
- **Silence the sirens?** It works, and it means taking on the Commander.

**What they can find out**

- It gets worse after every siren.
- The heist's timing: the demonstration drew the police away.
- Why the mitflits won't be used.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- It feeds on alarm, and the sirens scream into its ear. Every lens in the city is also a little Sap, and every one of them feeds it. *(Proposal)*
- The Directorate still doesn't know the Watcher exists (LEDGER #40). *(Canon)* It calls the lens sightings a hallucinogen on the glass, as the convention decided 38 years ago, and bans the lenses on that basis. *(Proposal)*
- The killing is the Young One's, done in panic. He builds a doctrine backwards to justify it, misquoting Manako.
- The crime lords have been timing Cicada's demonstrations to cover their heists, and Manako doesn't know yet.

**The Watcher:** G5, institution-breakers. It learns fear, and exactly where an institution breaks. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** Everyone can see it now. What is it?

### Chapter 11: What It Was For

*Officers, close to Command.* The party brings together everyone who was right and never believed, hears what the Envoy really was from the man who shot it, and meets their own last fight coming back at them.

**At the table**

1. The sceptics. People who saw something and were never believed come forward in the lens panic, or turn up in Rand's archive: Doctor Rika, with thirty-eight years of notes, and others. The party brings them to one table for dinner, the first room in the campaign where everyone was right.
2. The Soldier arranges his own arrest. In custody he tells them his side of an old day: the Directorate cordon, a dead woman filed as "unidentified", and a thing he was told had been summoned.
3. The police file, which the Directorate has never seen, names the officer who fired: Hori, retired. Getting it means persuading the police to trust them.
4. Hori gives them Aoi's name, and what the Envoy was: it walked into the street to a meeting. It was a greeting, and he shot it because he was afraid.
5. Rand's archive, the one honest paper record on the base, reaches them and the sceptics. Among Shucks's old papers in it is the Kortos survey from after First Impact: the four dropped a fuel container on the cargo deck, and the generation ship's navigation and sensor system has been missing ever since. *(Proposal)*
6. *(Proposal)* Back at the museum, the fragment speaks clearly, now that they know what to ask it.
7. Nobody tells them what the thing is. They put it together from the pieces, and say it out loud.
8. *(Proposal)* When they take it to the founders, it's news to all four: they knew they caused the Kortos rift, and never knew anything came out of it.
9. The Reel. A fight opens with their last fight played back at them: their own moves, improved, and what they said on the net included. The Mirror becomes visible.

**Choices**

- **Who to bring to the table.** Each sceptic holds a piece. Leaving one out leaves a hole in the case.
- **What to offer the Soldier.** He wants to be caught. What they offer decides what else he tells them.

**What they can find out**

- The truth: it's the generation ship's eyes, broken loose at First Impact, drawn here by the search ship's call, fed on Sap, and learning from everyone.
- First Impact was the founders' accident, and the founders never knew what came out of it.
- How they fight is what it becomes.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- Nobody on Golarion knew the Watcher existed before the party worked it out: not the Directorate, and not the founders (LEDGER #40). If the players are slow to see it, add clues; never let someone else find it first.
- Being believed is what Clearance is. In this chapter the party does for everyone else what nobody ever did for Manako.
- The Soldier and Hori were at the same incident, and neither knows the other exists.
- There are three roads to Aoi's name: Hori, the police record, and a surviving member of Manako's old circle.

**The Watcher:** G6, the Reel. Now that they know what it is, it stops hiding what it has learned from them. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** It was trying to say hello.

### Chapter 12: The Accusation

*Command · Clearance 4.* The party works out who wrote Cicada and says it out loud. Then they protect the people on his list, and the test pilot turns out to be Hyuck.

**At the table**

1. Promotion to Command (Clearance 4): they can overrule a decision once a chapter, and open sealed records.
2. The case. They can put together who wrote Cicada: his handwriting on the bill where he wrote the part number in Chapter 1, set against Cicada's papers; the sealed lab records of a chemist's disgrace 44 years ago; the Young One's slight misquotes; the forty-year-old papers they used in Chapter 4; and, if the knowledge check landed in Chapter 1, a disgraced chemist expelled after a breakdown at a conference.
3. The accusation. Nothing in the fiction names Manako as the author for them. When a player does, the chapter turns.
4. Manako's revenge list surfaces, with two names on it: Shucks and Nobu. The hunt turns into protecting them.
5. Manako learns, from the Fixer's books or from the party, that his "sacred" demonstrations were timed by the crime lords to cover their heists, and he accepts what he has become.
6. The Young One is unmasked.
7. Croglang's money leads above the crime lords, to someone nobody has ever seen.
8. The test pilot is Hyuck, and the harbour statue was always him. The founders' pictures are the lie. Asked how he's alive, he explains the Carrying, and more than one of the voices in him wants to tell it. *(Proposal)*

**Choices**

- **Arrest him, protect him, use him, or kill him?** The choice is the players'. Killing him cuts the only rope that steers the Watcher (fork).

**What they can find out**

- His handwriting, the lab records, the misquotes, and the revenge list.
- The founders' pictures are the lie.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- Play Manako with no villain beats. Cornered, his children grow wasteful: big and loud.
- Kiri and the Fixer make their last moves here. Kiri has been quietly planning who takes over from him.
- The someone above the crime lords is Baldwin. Don't name him; his reveal is parked.

**The Watcher:** G6, and wasteful, as its steward breaks.

**Where it leaves them:** They know who, and what. Now what?

## §10 Book IV: The Promise Comes Due (Chapters 13–15)

*The last pilots, the ones the city looks to.*

- **The question:** Can it be stopped?
- **The party:** Command, then Prodigies: the city does what they say, and the Directorate's resources are theirs. They earn it by showing extraordinary ability and judgement (LEDGER #42).
- **Their jobs:** Many at once, with finite means. They choose which.
- **Against them:** People-shaped children, then children that feed themselves. Abraxas beside the Watcher. A weapon it doesn't understand.
- **Manako:** *(Flag)* Decided closer to the time (LEDGER #42). As drafted: Frightened, then broken, and a frightened man's children are enormous and incoherent.
- **The breather:** Graduation, played straight, under a sky full of eyes for anyone holding a lens.
- **It ends:** With the rift open over the city, and a ship lifting.

### Chapter 13: The Deepest Vault

*Command · Clearance 4.* The party breaks into the Directorate's deepest vault looking for answers, a demon gets out, the Watcher's people-shaped children follow them in and take what the convoy brought, and the Directorate's leadership falls to the six.

**At the table**

1. The party breaks into the Directorate's deepest vault, looking for information (LEDGER #32). Even Command can't open it. What they're after is the vault inventory the Transparency Advocate was refused, and what the Chapter 8 convoy brought in. *(Proposal)* What else the vault holds is open. *(Flag)*
2. *(GM-only)* Abraxas gets out, with Anabala there. If Pippa plays Anabala, Anabala stays hers.
3. *(Proposal)* The Watcher has been watching them. Its people-shaped children follow them in through the breach they made, and take the convoy's cargo.
4. The people whose judgement failed resign and take full responsibility: here, NPCs can make bad mistakes and own them (LEDGER #42). *(Canon)* The Commander is among them, for losing the convoy's cargo. *(Proposal)*
5. The Deputy Director, the founders and the city all put their weight behind the six, who have shown the ability and judgement for it. The result is promotion, and the freedom to decide how to respond (LEDGER #42).
6. People-shaped children walk the streets. They talk, they hold their shape for days, and one wears a face Manako knows.
7. The mitflits split over whether to go home now or stay. Anabala's answer is to leave; Bobbin's is to refuse.
8. *(Flag)* Baldwin could step out here: the founders' old enemy, and so, for four of the players, their own. When, and how he has lived this long, are parked.

**Choices**

- **Everything at once.** They choose what to handle first, with finite people and machines.
- ***(Proposal)* Say what took it?** Only the party knows the Watcher exists. Saying so means being believed, which is what Clearance is.

**What they can find out**

- *(Proposal)* What the convoy brought in: exotic matter from the search ship, the same stuff that opened the rift at Kortos. With Command's sealed records they can find that out, and that it's gone.
- *(Proposal)* The search ship's bridge answers the marker.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- Abraxas and the Watcher together: a mind that wants to know everything gives it surveillance with a purpose.
- *(Proposal)* The face one of the children wears is Aoi's.
- *(Proposal)* The Watcher learned what the exotic matter is worth by watching the convoy (Chapter 8). Taking it is the second step towards Chapter 15.
- The break-in is the party's, as Ingram prefers it (LEDGER #32). The theft is the Watcher's, learned by watching them (LEDGER #41 corrects #40's reading here).

**The Watcher:** G7, Envoys: there are people. With Abraxas beside it, it watches with intent. *(Proposal)*

**Where it leaves them:** The city looks to them.

### Chapter 14: The Makers

*Prodigies · Clearance 5.* The Watcher's children start feeding themselves and it starts building something, and the six graduate under a sky full of eyes.

**At the table**

1. Prodigies (Clearance 5): the city does what they say, and the Directorate is theirs to run. The adults are still there; they follow the six's lead.
2. The runaway. The first sign is a contact that grows instead of draining away: the Watcher's children have started making their own Sap. It doesn't need Manako, the column, or anyone else any more.
3. They command the whole Directorate through it, and choose what to save.
4. *(Flag)* Manako's place here is decided closer to the time, from how the table treated him (LEDGER #42). As drafted: if he's alive, he is shown the truth, not told it and not argued with; the party chooses how (the dig, the Envoy file, Hori), and he breaks.
5. Graduation, played straight: the ceremony, the families, the speeches. Anyone holding one of the banned lenses sees the sky full of eyes.
6. *(Proposal)* Through a lens, something new is taking shape in the sky over the dig: the eyes folding inward around a violet glow. The founders know that violet. It's what opened the rift at Kortos.
7. *(Proposal)* The party can go after it. They can hurt it and slow it; they can't stop it.
8. *(Flag)* The temptation, on hold until it has a mechanism: someone offers them the Watcher's eyes.

**Choices**

- **What to save.** The runaway is bigger than the Directorate.
- ***(Proposal)* Go after what it's building?** They can hurt it and slow it, not stop it. How they go at it is the last thing the Watcher learns from them.
- ***(Flag)* Use its eyes?** Win everything nearer home, and teach it that watching everyone is what good people do.

**What they can find out**

- It doesn't need anyone now.
- *(Proposal)* Something violet is forming over the dig, and the founders have seen that colour before.

**GM notes** *(GM-only)*

- It's a weapon the Watcher is building from the exotic matter, without understanding that it could destroy the Watcher itself, the city, Golarion and a stretch of space around it (LEDGER #40). It isn't evil: it is escalating, because it observes, adapts and responds.
- *(Proposal)* The offer could come from Abraxas, the Directorate's own engineers, or Manako, broken. It would be the most useful thing the Directorate ever had, and the Watcher would learn from how they used it.
- *(Proposal)* Anabala wakes the search ship in these weeks, to take her people home. It's why the ship can lift in Chapter 15.

**The Watcher:** G8, Makers: the runaway. It notices everything now, and it is building a weapon it doesn't understand.

**Where it leaves them:** It doesn't need anyone any more.

### Chapter 15: Second Impact

*The Directorate is theirs.* The Watcher's weapon tears open the rift the gods promised, and the search ship lifts with the mitflits aboard, leaving the Watcher over the city.

**At the table**

1. The violet knot over the dig breaks open. It was the Watcher's weapon (LEDGER #40). *(Canon)* What sets it off is open: perhaps the Watcher uses it when it's cornered, perhaps it goes off by accident. The table's choices can move it, never stop it. *(Proposal)*
2. *(Canon)* A violet rift tears open over Kurose. Nothing the party does could have stopped it.
